US Library Census

From the US Library Census Wiki

A living dataset of every public and private library, and every government website, in the United States.
46,335 nodes mapped · 17,615 public libraries · 3,695 private libraries · 25,025 government websites · updated 2026-08-17 01:21 UTC

Welcome to the US Library Census

This wiki presents an automated, continuously-enriched dataset covering every known public library, academic/special library, and federal/state/county/city government website in the United States. Each record carries address, geocoordinates, website, funding, contact details, review ratings, hours, services, and area demographics.

Community Broadband Access

How connected are the communities that libraries serve? Broadband subscription rates, digital divide metrics, and the communities most in need of library internet access — from Census ACS 2023 5-year estimates (B28003, B28004, B28008).

86.7%
Avg broadband subscription
78.6%
Avg fixed broadband
72.4%
Avg cellular data plan
6.5%
Avg no internet at home
6.6%
Avg no computer at home
0.2%
Avg dial-up only
26.7%
Low-income no internet
17,019
Communities with data

The digital divide: low-income households (under $10K/yr) are far less likely to have internet. Across reporting communities, 26.7% of low-income households lack internet vs 6.5% overall. Libraries bridge this gap with public WiFi and computer terminals.

Worst Digital Divide — Communities Where Libraries Are Most Critical

Communities where the gap between low-income and overall internet access is largest. A divide ratio of 5.0× means low-income households are 5 times more likely to lack internet than the general population.

FCC Broadband Infrastructure Availability

The supply side: what broadband infrastructure ISPs have actually deployed in each community, from the FCC National Broadband Map (Dec 2025, BDC data). While the ACS data above shows what households subscribe to, this shows what's available — fiber, gigabit, and rural coverage rates.

64.5%
Avg gigabit availability
54.9%
Avg fiber availability
41.6%
Avg rural locations
17,019
Counties with FCC data

FCC BDC deployment data (Dec 31, 2025 filing). Gigabit = 1000/100 Mbps available; Fiber = any-speed fiber to the premises. "Any Technology" broadband (25/3 Mbps, including satellite) is available to ~100% of locations nationwide — the real infrastructure gap is in gigabit and fiber deployment.

Town-Level Broadband Availability (FCC Census Place)

The most precise broadband picture available — measured at the town/city level (Census Place), not county. Matched to each library by city + state using the FCC National Broadband Map's per-state Census Place files (Dec 2025 BDC deployment data). This reveals the true infrastructure gap libraries face, since broadband can vary dramatically between towns within a single county.

71.7%
Avg town gigabit availability
100.0%
Avg town 100/20 Mbps
62.1%
Avg town fiber availability
14,934
Library towns with data
1,966
Towns <25% gigabit
1,279
Towns with <5% fiber

FCC BDC deployment data at Census Place (incorporated town/city) granularity, Dec 31 2025 filing. "Gigabit" = 1000/100 Mbps available to serviceable locations; "100/20" = the FCC's current broadband definition; "Fiber" = fiber-to-the-premises at any speed. These figures are independent of the county-level FCC data above — they reflect the specific town each library sits in.

Library Towns Without Gigabit Infrastructure — Town-Level View

Federal Depository Libraries (GPO FDLP)

The Federal Depository Library Program — 672 libraries that receive and provide public access to U.S. government documents. Of these, 44 are Regional depositories (comprehensive collections, one or more per state) and 627 are Selective (choosing specific titles). 1 are designated Preservation Stewards, committing to retain government publications long-term.

672
Depository libraries
44
Regional depositories
627
Selective depositories
1
Preservation Stewards

IMLS Grant Awards (1996–2025)

The Institute of Museum and Library Services is the primary federal funder of the nation's libraries and museums. Over 1996–2025, IMLS awarded 21,325 grants totaling $4.04 billion to libraries, museums, and Native American organizations across all 50 states and territories. FY2021 saw a surge to $430M — the American Rescue Plan Act (ARPA) pandemic relief.

IMLS Grants to States (G2S) — Formula Funding (2014–2025)

While discretionary grants are awarded competitively, Grants to States (G2S) are formula-based funding distributed annually to every state library agency under the Library Services and Technology Act (LSTA). This is the backbone of federal library funding — it flows through the state agencies tracked in the SLAA section above to support local library services, technology, and outreach.

Academic Library Trends (NCES ALS/IPEDS 2000–2023)

The NCES Academic Library Survey collected data from degree-granting postsecondary institutions across 17 survey vintages (2000, 2002, 2004, 2006, 2008, 2010, 2012, 2014, 2015, 2016, 2017, 2018, 2019, 2020, 2021, 2022, 2023). After 2012 the standalone ALS was discontinued and data collection was folded into IPEDS as the Academic Libraries component, continuing annually from 2014 onward (staffing was not collected 2014–2019). This is the only national census of college & university libraries, capturing staffing, collections, expenditures, and services over a 23-year span.

Academic Library Consortia

ConsortiumScopeMembers
OhioLINK (Ohio Library and Information Network)Ohio's statewide library consortium, beginning operations in 1992 when six Ohio universities installed its systems and started building the central catalog. Provides shared cataloging, a central catalog of >50M items, e-resource licensing (databases, e-journals, e-books), and rapid patron-initiated borrowing across 90+ member libraries (public universities, private colleges, community/technical colleges, and the State Library of Ohio).
CARLI (Consortium of Academic and Research Libraries in Illinois) / I-ShareCARLI serves >140 Illinois academic and research libraries. Its I-Share resource-sharing system gives patrons of member libraries unified searching and requesting across the consortium, with direct patron-initiated borrowing and pickup at any member institution. CARLI also negotiates group e-resource licenses and supports shared collection development.
Prospector (Colorado Alliance of Research Libraries)Operated by the Colorado Alliance of Research Libraries, Prospector is a unified catalog and rapid patron-initiated borrowing network spanning >40 academic, public, and special libraries in Colorado and Wyoming. Requests are typically filled within 3-5 business days. Members include the University of Colorado campuses, Colorado State, University of Denver, University of Northern Colorado, and Denver Public Library.
OCLC (global, founded as Ohio College Library Center)Founded July 5, 1967 as the Ohio College Library Center for shared cataloging. Now a nonprofit cooperative serving ~30,000 member libraries in 100+ countries. Maintains WorldCat (the largest online public access catalog) and the Dewey Decimal Classification. Annual revenue ~$217.8M (2020-21). The first online cataloging by any library worldwide occurred August 26, 1971 at Ohio University via OCLC.
TRLN (Triangle Research Libraries Network)The earliest formally established U.S. library consortium, serving the major academic libraries of North Carolina (Duke, NCSU, UNC-Chapel Hill, NCCU). Coordinates shared cataloging, collection development, and expedited borrowing.
VIVA (Virtual Library of Virginia)Established July 1994 to provide statewide licensing of electronic resources and resource sharing for Virginia's nonprofit academic libraries (public and private).
TexShareEstablished with 53 Texas academic and medical libraries in 1994; administered by the Texas State Library and Archives Commission. Provides group e-resource licensing and a statewide library card program enabling reciprocal borrowing.
Center for Research Libraries (CRL)Founded in 1949 as the Midwest Inter-Library Center (MILC) by ten midwestern universities. 250 member libraries. Acquires and preserves foreign newspapers, government documents, microform archives, and foreign dissertations available via interlibrary loan. Hosts NERL, which negotiates licenses for 28 member and 80 affiliate academic research libraries.
LyrasisFormed April 2009 from the merger of SOLINET and PALINET (history to 1933); absorbed NELINET (2009) and the Bibliographical Center for Research (2011). 1,000+ members, ~$139M revenue. Organizational home for open-source projects including DSpace, Fedora, ArchivesSpace, CollectionSpace, VIVO.
Big Ten Academic Alliance (formerly CIC)The Committee on Institutional Cooperation (CIC), now the Big Ten Academic Alliance, operates shared-collection programs and co-founded HathiTrust in 2008 with the University of California libraries.

Key Findings

  • There are approximately 3,700 degree-granting academic libraries in the United States, per NCES, which surveys them biennially. The IPEDS AL 2022-23 file contains 3,741 institutions, of which 2,862 reported full expenditure data.
  • U.S. academic libraries collectively reported $8,201,294,317 in total expenditures in 2022-23 (IPEDS AL), of which $3,229,107,396 (39%) was for materials, $4,232,273,201 for salaries and fringe benefits, and $739,913,720 for other operating costs.
  • The Association of Research Libraries (ARL), founded in 1932, has 125 member research libraries in the U.S. and Canada (of which 104 are U.S. academic libraries) that collectively spend approximately $4,500,000,000 per year on information resources. ARL Statistics, published since 1963, is the gold-standard descriptive series for research libraries.
  • Harvard Library is the largest strictly academic library in the world, holding over 20 million volumes, 400 million manuscripts, 10 million photographs, and 1 million maps. The University of California operates the largest academic library SYSTEM in the world, managing about 41 million volumes across 100 libraries on ten campuses.
  • By NCES ALS 2012 volumes-held counts, the largest U.S. academic libraries were Harvard (17.2M), Yale (13.5M), University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign (12.9M), UC Berkeley (11.5M), University of Michigan (11.5M), University of Chicago (11.4M), Columbia (11.3M), UT Austin (10.2M), UCLA (10.0M), and Indiana University (9.3M).
  • Electronic serial titles now outnumber physical serial titles by roughly 66-to-1 (1.54 billion electronic vs 23.2 million physical in IPEDS 2022-23), and current serial subscriptions account for 78.9% of academic library materials spending - illustrating the near-complete migration of journal collections to electronic formats and the budget dominance of 'big deal' publisher packages and database licenses.

Library Workforce & Employment

The U.S. library workforce comprises 370,560 workers as of 2023 — spanning librarians, library assistants, technicians, and support staff across public, academic, and school libraries.

Library Journal 2023 Placements & Salaries Survey (class of 2022 new MLIS graduates): 74% white, 10% Asian (7% South Asian, 2% East Asian, 1% Southeast Asian), 8% Hispanic/Latine, 4% Black/African American, 3% mixed race, 2% Native American/Indigenous, 5% undisclosed — white share down from 84% in 2021.
  • Notes: The librarian profession has a persistent racial diversity lag; workforce is far whiter than the broader U.S. labor force. Diversity is greater among library technicians/assistants and improving among new MLIS graduates.

  • Union Wage Premium: Union librarians earned 37% ($336) more per week than non-union counterparts in 2015. Union library assistants earned 99% more than non-union counterparts. Union library technicians earned 49% more (last comparable data 2009).

Key Facts

  • The U.S. library workforce comprises ~370,560 workers across four BLS occupational categories (2023 OEWS): 133,200 librarians/media collections specialists, 159,620 library assistants, 74,080 library technicians, and 3,660 postsecondary library science teachers.
  • Librarianship is one of the most female-dominated professions in the U.S.: women were 83% of librarians in 2015 (DPE) and 81% in 2019; the share has held at roughly 80-84% since the 1990s. Among new 2022 MLIS graduates, 74% were women, 18% men, 6% nonbinary/gender nonconforming (LJ 2023).
  • The profession has a persistent racial diversity gap: 84% of librarians were non-Hispanic White in 2015, vs. 74% of library assistants. Among new 2022 MLIS graduates the white share fell to 74% (from 84% in 2021), with Asian graduates rising to 10% — a notable diversification trend among new entrants.
  • The workforce is aging: 42% of librarians were over age 55 in 2014, signaling a wave of impending retirements and succession challenges. New MLIS graduates average age 35 (2022).
  • Despite being female-dominated, a gender wage gap persists: women full-time librarians earned 93% of men's median salary in 2014 ($48,589 vs $52,528) and 92% in 2019. The gap is wider for women of color — African American women earned 69-70 cents and Hispanic/Latina women 63-64 cents per dollar earned by men (2015/2019).
  • Education, training, and library occupations had the HIGHEST unionization rate of any professional occupation group in 2015 (35.5%). 20.5% of librarians, 16.3% of library assistants, and 11.2% of library technicians were union members. Union librarians earned 37% more per week than non-union counterparts.
  • Cumulative employment among librarians, technicians, and assistants shrank from 380,000 (2007) to 295,000 (2015) — a 22.5% decline. Employment of elementary/secondary school librarians fell 25% (2004-2014), from ~80,000 to ~66,000.
  • The average full-time starting salary for new MLIS graduates was $61,473 in 2022 (LJ 2023 survey), up from $45,650 in 2013 but with a COVID dip to $56,453 in 2020. Unemployment among new graduates fell to 5% in 2023 from 7% in 2022.

Libraries & Research Data Science

Academic and research libraries increasingly support the full research lifecycle — from data management plans to systematic reviews to digital humanities to open access publishing.

Research Data Management

  • Overview: Research Data Management (RDM) has become a core library service driven by funder mandates, reproducibility concerns, and the FAIR data movement. Academic libraries now offer DMP consulting, data repo

Scholarly Communication & Open Access

  • Overview: Scholarly communication encompasses the creation, evaluation, dissemination, and preservation of research outputs. Libraries are central actors: they run institutional repositories, advocate for open
  • Sparc: SPARC (Scholarly Publishing and Academic Resources Coalition), founded in 1998 and hosted by ARL, advocates for open access, open data, and open education, and provides tools such as the SPARC Author

Digital Humanities

  • Overview: Digital humanities (DH) applies computational methods to humanistic inquiry and studies the impact of digital technologies on culture and society. Libraries are major hosts of DH activity, providing s
  • Digital Scholarship Centers: Digital Scholarship Centers (DSCs) generalize the DH-center model to support computational and digital research across all disciplines. They typically combine consultation, training, lab space, and ac

Notable Research Libraries

  • Overview: Research libraries include the largest academic libraries, national libraries with research missions, and specialized federal research libraries. The Association of Research Libraries (ARL) is the def

Key Findings

  • {'finding': 'Libraries have shifted from collections-centric institutions to active research partners embedded across the research lifecycle, providing consultation, data services, and publishing support rather than merely housing materials.', 'supporting_detail': 'Embedded librarianship and liaison models now place librarians inside labs, courses, and clinical settings, fundamentally redefining the library-research relationship.'}
  • {'finding': "Funder data-sharing mandates - notably NSF's 2011 Data Management Plan requirement and NIH's 2023 Data Management and Sharing Policy - have made research data management a mandatory library service domain.", 'supporting_detail': 'These policies drive demand for DMP consulting, data repository hosting, and curation services that only research libraries are positioned to provide at institutional scale.'}
  • {'finding': 'The FAIR Data Principles (2016) have become the dominant framework shaping repository design, metadata practices, and curation workflows across the research-data ecosystem.', 'supporting_detail': 'FAIR-aligned practices underpin tools from DMPTool and DMPOnline to Dryad, Dataverse, Zenodo, and CoreTrustSeal certification.'}
  • {'finding': 'Librarians are now recognized methodological partners in systematic reviews and evidence synthesis, with defined roles in search design, documentation, and PRISMA-compliant reporting.', 'supporting_detail': "Cochrane, Campbell, and JBI methodologies, plus the PRESS checklist for peer review of search strategies, formalize the librarian's contribution and frequently lead to co-authorship."}
  • {'finding': 'Scholarly communication has become a core library function spanning open access advocacy, institutional repositories, ORCID integration, and library-operated publishing programs.', 'supporting_detail': 'Platforms like DSpace, Digital Commons, and OJS, together with ORCID and CRIS systems such as PURE and Symplectic Elements, let libraries manage and disseminate the full research record.'}
  • {'finding': 'Bibliometric and altmetric services offered by libraries help researchers demonstrate impact while promoting responsible metrics via DORA, the Leiden Manifesto, and CoARA.', 'supporting_detail': 'Web of Science, Scopus, Google Scholar, and altmetric aggregators supply the data, but libraries add the contextual analysis and methodological guidance that prevents misuse.'}
  • {'finding': 'Digital humanities and digital scholarship centers, often library-hosted, connect computational methods with library collections, metadata, and preservation expertise.', 'supporting_detail': "Centers such as Stanford CESTA, UCLA CDH, and UVA's Scholars' Lab demonstrate how libraries seed text-mining, GIS, and corpus-building infrastructure for the humanities and beyond."}
  • {'finding': "Data curation has professionalized through codified models - the Data Curation Network's CURATE(D) workflow and the DCC Curation Lifecycle Model - giving libraries a standardized practice for stewardship.", 'supporting_detail': 'These models translate archival and cataloging traditions into actionable, FAIR-aligned curation steps, supported by training from DCN, DCC, and RDA.'}

Book Challenges & Bans (2018–2025)

The EveryLibrary Institute's Book Censorship Database, maintained by Dr. Tasslyn Magnusson, tracks every reported attempt to restrict, remove, or ban books in U.S. schools and libraries. 20,890 challenges documented across 20 states — 6,875 resulting in removals or bans. This is the most comprehensive crowdsourced record of the wave of book censorship that accelerated in 2021.

Tribal Broadband Connectivity Program (TBCP)

The NTIA's Tribal Broadband Connectivity Program, funded by the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act (IIJA), awarded $2.16 billion across 271 Tribal projects. Awards support broadband infrastructure deployment, digital adoption, and planning on Tribal lands — directly serving libraries and communities that have historically lacked adequate internet access. States are derived from award ZIP codes.

Emergency Connectivity Fund (ECF) — Device Distribution

The FCC's Emergency Connectivity Fund, a pandemic-era program (2021-06-29 to 2024-10-17), distributed $10.03 billion to help schools and libraries provide laptops, tablets, Wi-Fi hotspots, and broadband connections to students and patrons. Libraries received $1,027M across 10,971 funding requests — a massive one-time federal investment in digital access equipment that directly addressed the homework gap.

Affordable Connectivity Program (ACP)

The Affordable Connectivity Program was a $14.2 billion federal broadband subsidy that helped low-income households pay for internet service. Before the program ended in June 2024, 23.3 million households had enrolled. This data reveals the enormous unmet demand for affordable internet - the very demand that libraries help address through free WiFi, public computers, and hotspot lending. Over 29 months, $29.8B in claims were processed.

The ACP was created by the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act (IIJA) and succeeded the Emergency Broadband Benefit (EBB) program. The program ended June 1, 2024 when funding was exhausted.

E-Rate: Library Telecommunications Funding (FY2016-FY2026)

The Universal Service E-Rate program is the largest ongoing federal program funding library and school telecommunications and internet access. Libraries apply for discounts on broadband, fiber, and internal connections via FCC Form 471. 4,066 library applicants submitted 252,923 funding requests totaling $2,283M in pre-discount eligible costs across FY2016-FY2026. This is the quiet, steady infrastructure funding that keeps libraries connected year after year.

Broadband Equity Access & Deployment (BEAD)

The BEAD program is the largest broadband investment in U.S. history: $41.60 billion allocated across 56 states and territories from the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act. Each state received a minimum of $100M (territories $25M) plus additional funding based on their share of unserved locations. While BEAD primarily funds broadband infrastructure deployment, libraries benefit as community anchor institutions that provide public internet access in newly connected areas.

The Library Workforce: Who Works in Libraries

The US library workforce comprises approximately 370,560 workers across four BLS occupational categories. Librarianship is one of the most female-dominated professions in America (83% women) and has a persistent racial diversity gap (84% white). The workforce is aging - 42% of librarians were over 55 in 2014 - signaling a wave of impending retirements. Yet education, training, and library occupations have the highest unionization rate of any professional occupation group (35.5%).

20.5%

35.5%

Union membership rates

OccupationUnion rate
Librarians20.5%

All education/training/library occupations35.5%

Union wage premium: Union librarians earned 37% ($336) more per week than non-union counterparts in 2015. Union library assistants earned 99% more than non-union counterparts. Union library technicians earned 49% more (last comparable data 2009).

Key findings

  • The U.S. library workforce comprises ~370,560 workers across four BLS occupational categories (2023 OEWS): 133,200 librarians/media collections specialists, 159,620 library assistants, 74,080 library technicians, and 3,660 postsecondary library science teachers.
  • Librarianship is one of the most female-dominated professions in the U.S.: women were 83% of librarians in 2015 (DPE) and 81% in 2019; the share has held at roughly 80-84% since the 1990s. Among new 2022 MLIS graduates, 74% were women, 18% men, 6% nonbinary/gender nonconforming (LJ 2023).
  • The profession has a persistent racial diversity gap: 84% of librarians were non-Hispanic White in 2015, vs. 74% of library assistants. Among new 2022 MLIS graduates the white share fell to 74% (from 84% in 2021), with Asian graduates rising to 10% — a notable diversification trend among new entrants.
  • The workforce is aging: 42% of librarians were over age 55 in 2014, signaling a wave of impending retirements and succession challenges. New MLIS graduates average age 35 (2022).
  • Despite being female-dominated, a gender wage gap persists: women full-time librarians earned 93% of men's median salary in 2014 ($48,589 vs $52,528) and 92% in 2019. The gap is wider for women of color — African American women earned 69-70 cents and Hispanic/Latina women 63-64 cents per dollar earned by men (2015/2019).
  • Education, training, and library occupations had the HIGHEST unionization rate of any professional occupation group in 2015 (35.5%). 20.5% of librarians, 16.3% of library assistants, and 11.2% of library technicians were union members. Union librarians earned 37% more per week than non-union counterparts.
  • Cumulative employment among librarians, technicians, and assistants shrank from 380,000 (2007) to 295,000 (2015) — a 22.5% decline. Employment of elementary/secondary school librarians fell 25% (2004-2014), from ~80,000 to ~66,000.
  • The average full-time starting salary for new MLIS graduates was $61,473 in 2022 (LJ 2023 survey), up from $45,650 in 2013 but with a COVID dip to $56,453 in 2020. Unemployment among new graduates fell to 5% in 2023 from 7% in 2022.
  • ALA's Spectrum Scholarship Program awards scholarships to minority library students to diversify the profession; ALA provides over $300,000 in scholarships annually. In 2022 ALA formally acknowledged its history of 'upholding unjust systems of racism and discrimination against Black, Indigenous, and People of Color (BIPOC)' and resolved to build a more equitable association.
  • The MLIS (Master of Library and Information Science) from an ALA-accredited program is the terminal professional degree for librarians in the U.S. and Canada; 55% of librarians held a master's degree or higher in 2014. Most academic librarians also hold a second subject-based master's degree.

Source: BLS Occupational Employment and Wage Statistics (OEWS) May 2023 for employment counts and salaries; AFL-CIO Department for Professional Employees "Library Workers: Facts & Figures 2016" for gender/racial/union data; Library Journal 2023 Placements & Salaries Survey for new MLIS graduate demographics. The 370,560 total includes 133K librarians/media collections specialists, 160K library assistants, 74K library technicians, and 3.7K library science teachers. Librarianship has been majority-female since ~1930. The racial diversity gap is narrowing among new MLIS graduates (white share fell from 84% in 2021 to 74% in 2022). Education, training, and library occupations have the highest unionization rate (35.5%) of any professional occupation group.

Library Ballot Measures (2013-2026)

When libraries need new buildings, expanded operations, or renewed tax levies, they often must ask voters directly through ballot measures. EveryLibrary tracks these elections across the country. Of 176 library ballot measures recorded, 123 passed (70%) and 53 failed, with $758M in funding requested. This is democracy at its most local - communities voting directly on whether to fund their libraries.

State Library Funding: Where the Money Comes From

America's 51 state library systems (plus 5 territories) received $17.9B in total income in FY2024 serving a population of 343M - that's $52.07 per person. But the funding model varies dramatically by state: most rely overwhelmingly on local property taxes, a handful depend on state government appropriations, and federal funding is a tiny fraction everywhere. Understanding this funding mix is essential to understanding why libraries in different states face very different political and budgetary pressures.

National totals: $17.9B total income across 51 states + DC and 5 territories, serving 343M people ($52.07/capita). The 85.5% local / 6.7% state / 0.5% federal split reveals that America's public libraries are overwhelmingly a local government function. Ohio is a striking outlier: its state government provides $482M (42.9% of library income) through the dedicated Library and Local Government Support Fund - more than all other states combined. Hawaii runs the opposite model with 93.5% state funding and no local library districts. IMLS uses negative sentinels (-1, -3, -40) for suppressed/unreported values, normalized to 0 here.

USDA Rural Development Library Grants & Loans

The USDA Rural Development Community Facilities program (CFDA 10.766) provides grants and loans to rural areas under 20,000 population for essential community facilities - and public libraries are explicitly eligible. From FY2007 to FY2025, the program awarded 262 grants and loans totaling $64.2M to 172 library recipients across 41 states. While modest compared to IMLS or E-Rate funding, these USDA awards are often transformative for small rural communities that lack other capital sources.

National Endowment for the Humanities: Library Grants

The National Endowment for the Humanities (NEH) is an independent federal agency that funds humanities research, education, preservation, and public programs. Libraries are major NEH recipients - from the American Library Association's national reading programs to state newspaper digitization projects. From FY to FY, NEH awarded 314 grants totaling $62.8M to library recipients across 0 states, with an average grant of $200K. The largest single award was $2.22M to the American Library Association in 2021 for American Rescue Plan humanities relief.

IMLS Grants to Libraries: All Programs

The Institute of Museum and Library Services is the primary federal funder of the nation's libraries. Through the Grants to States program (LSTA), National Leadership Grants, Laura Bush 21st Century Librarian Program, and other initiatives, IMLS awarded 936 grants totaling $1.5B to library recipients from FY2007 to FY2026. These grants flow primarily to state library agencies, which redistribute funds to local libraries, but also fund direct grants for research, professional development, and digital inclusion initiatives.

Federal Funding for Libraries: The Complete Picture

When you add up every federal program that funds libraries - from IMLS grants to broadband subsidies to emergency COVID relief - the total reaches $88.4B across 9 distinct programs. The largest programs are broadband infrastructure (BEAD, ACP, ECF, E-Rate) rather than direct library grants. IMLS, the agency most people associate with library funding, accounts for just $1.7% of the total federal investment in libraries.

Total represents cumulative federal funding across all programs. Some amounts are allocations (BEAD) rather than disbursed grants. ACP and ECF programs have ended. E-Rate is an ongoing annual program. BEAD ($42.45B) and ACP ($29.8B) are broadband programs that benefit libraries as anchor institutions but are not library-specific grants. E-Rate ($2.28B) is the largest ongoing library-specific federal program, providing telecommunications and internet access discounts. The $10B Emergency Connectivity Fund (ECF) was a one-time COVID-era program that has ended. IMLS ($1.47B) is the primary direct grant-making agency for libraries. Together, these programs represent the most significant federal investment in library infrastructure, access, and services in American history.

Other Federal Grants to Libraries

Beyond IMLS, NEH, and USDA, several other federal agencies award grants to libraries. From USASpending data, 7 federal agencies collectively awarded 156 grants totaling $89.4M to library recipients. The Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) is the largest non-library-specific funder, using Community Development Block Grants to support library construction in distressed communities. The Department of the Interior funds libraries on tribal lands and historic preservation projects.

How Americans Use Libraries (Survey Data)

Beyond the administrative data, public opinion surveys reveal how Americans actually interact with libraries and what they value. The Pew Research Center surveyed 6,224 Americans in 2013, finding that 54% had used a library in the past year. Gallup's 2019 poll found Americans visit libraries an average of 10.5 times per year - more frequent than any other cultural activity measured. In 2022, the NEA added a library-visit question to its Survey of Public Participation in the Arts for the first time: 23% of adults reported visiting a public library.

Pew surveyed Americans aged 16+ on library use and attitudes. In-person visits declined 5pp from 2012 to 2013 while website visits increased 5pp - a shift toward digital access that the PLS data confirms. The NEA SPPA 2022 library-visit question is not directly comparable to Pew due to different wording (in-person public library visits by adults 18+) and methodology.

Who Uses Libraries & Why

Survey data reveals stark demographic patterns in library use. Gallup's 2019 poll found that women visit libraries nearly twice as often as men (13.4 vs 7.5 visits/year), young adults aged 18-29 visit most frequently (15.5/year), and low-income households use libraries more than high-income ones (12.2 vs 8.5 visits/year). Pew's 2016 survey found that college graduates (59%) and parents (55%) visit libraries at higher rates - suggesting libraries serve both the educated and those caring for children.

School Libraries / Media Centers (NCES, 2011-12)

The NCES Schools and Staffing Survey (SASS) - since discontinued - provides the most recent comprehensive data on school libraries. In 2011-12, 81,200 public schools had library media centers (58,000 elementary, 17,100 secondary). Each center averaged 1.77 staff and 88.3% had automated catalogs.

Reading Trends: The Decline of Book Reading (NEA 2017-2022)

The NEA's 2022 Survey of Public Participation in the Arts reveals a significant decline in book reading among American adults. From 2017 to 2022, the share of adults who read books fell from 52.7% to 48.5% - a 4.2 percentage point drop. Literature reading (novels, poetry, plays) fell even more sharply, from 44.2% to 39.6%. Novel reading hit a record low of 37.6%. This decline has direct implications for libraries, which exist to serve a population that is reading less.

COVID-19 Impact and Recovery

The COVID-19 pandemic was the most disruptive event in the history of American public libraries. Using multi-year IMLS Public Libraries Survey data, we can trace the full arc: in FY2019, American libraries recorded 1254M visits. By FY2020, that had collapsed to 732M - a 42% decline. As of FY2024, visits have recovered to 869M, still only 69% of pre-pandemic levels. Yet funding actually grew +25%, suggesting libraries pivoted to digital services rather than closing for good.

State of America's Libraries (ALA 2024)

The American Library Association's annual State of America's Libraries Report is the field's most authoritative year-in-review. The 2024 edition (covering 2023 data) documents a censorship crisis: 4,240 unique titles were challenged in 1,247 documented censorship attempts - a 65% increase from 2,571 in 2022. For context, the average year from 2001-2020 saw only 273 unique titles challenged. Meanwhile, 151 state censorship bills were introduced across the country, and public library challenges doubled from 16% to 32% of all challenges.

Digital Public Library of America (DPLA)

The Digital Public Library of America, founded April 18, 2013 and headquartered in Boston, is the nation's aggregator of digital cultural heritage. Rather than holding its own collections, DPLA brings together metadata from thousands of libraries, archives, museums, and historical societies through a network of 32 Service Hubs and 14 Content Hubs. Since launching with 2.4 million items, DPLA has grown to 55M+ items from 8,700+ contributing institutions - a 23x increase in just over a decade.

School Libraries: Certified Librarian Access by State

While public libraries get most attention, the nation's 81,200 school library media centers are where most Americans first encounter libraries. NCES data from 2011-12 (the most recent available, as the SASS survey was discontinued) reveals stark state-level disparities: Tennessee has certified librarians in 97.6% of school libraries, while California has them in only 25.2% - a 72 percentage-point gap. Nationwide, only 66.4% of school libraries have a full-time certified librarian, meaning roughly 1 in 5 school libraries (16,990) have no certified librarian at all.

Source: NCES Schools and Staffing Survey (SASS), Table Library. Data from 2011-12 is the most recent available because the SASS was replaced by the National Teacher and Principal Survey (NTPS) in 2015-16, which does not include a school library component. The 72.4 percentage-point spread between Tennessee (97.6%) and California (25.2%) represents one of the largest state-level disparities in any educational metric. Charter schools have dramatically lower staffing: 32.8% full-time certified vs 67.4% for traditional public schools. Data currency note: this data is over a decade old; current school librarian access rates are likely lower due to budget cuts and the SASS/NTPS discontinuation has created a data gap.

School Library Impact Studies

Research spanning three decades has consistently shown that well-staffed, well-funded school libraries improve student achievement — regardless of community wealth or demographics.

StateYearResearcherKey Finding
Colorado1993Keith Curry LanceFirst Colorado study (221 schools, 1988-89): better-funded library media centers linked to higher reading scores regardless of community wealth or adult education. LMC staff size, collection variety, and the specialist's instructional role were key intervening factors.
Alaska2000Keith Curry Lance, Christine Hamilton-Pennell, Marcia J. RodneyAlaska study (211 schools, 1997-98): school librarians had a direct relationship with student performance; staff activities such as teaching information literacy and collaborating with teachers affected test scores.
Pennsylvania2000Keith Curry Lance, Marcia J. Rodney, Christine Hamilton-PennellPennsylvania 'Measuring Up to Standards' (500 libraries): presence of adequate school library staffing linked to higher PSSA reading scores; as library staffing rises, reading scores rise. Link not explained by per-pupil expenditures, teacher-pupil ratio, poverty, or adult education.
Oregon2001Keith Curry LanceOregon study ('Good Schools Have School Librarians'): school librarians collaborating to improve academic achievement; fourth of four statewide studies confirming the pattern.
Texas2001Keith Curry Lance (ETC, via 'Proof of the Power')Synthesis of four statewide studies (AK, CO, PA, OR) showing quality library media programs affect academic achievement through roles of media specialists in teaching, information access, program administration, and technology availability.
New York2009Ruth V. Small, Jaime Snyder, Katie ParkerNew York Phase I (IMLS-funded): extended previous statewide impact studies using multiple methods to explore the influence of the school librarian on technology use, principal-SLMS relationships, and service to students with disabilities. 18 prior state studies had established the achievement link.
Idaho2010Keith Curry Lance, Marcia J. Rodney, Bill SchwarzIdaho study (176 principals/administrators): where administrators value strong library programs and can see them supporting student success, students are more likely to thrive academically.
Pennsylvania2012Keith Curry Lance, Bill Schwarz'How Pennsylvania School Libraries Pay Off': examined all 500 public school districts using 2011 PSSA data (including economically disadvantaged, Hispanic, Black, and students-with-disabilities subgroups). Identified the infrastructure (staffing, budgets, collections, technology, access hours) that most contributes to student achievement.
Iowa (rural)2013(EJ1057517)Descriptive phenomenological study of fourth-grade state research projects in high-poverty rural Iowa schools revealed the influence of school librarians' staffing levels on student learning of research skills; rural high-poverty schools often understaffed.
Michigan2007Marcia Mardis, Ellen HoffmanExamined science collections and collaboration in Michigan middle school media centers; data-driven decision trend requires every learning component to document positive impact.

School Library Equity Gaps

The SLIDE (School Librarian Investigation: Decline or Evolution?) project documented stark, widening inequities in school library staffing. The ~20% national decline in school librarians (2010-2019) has fallen disproportionately on districts serving Black, Latino, and Native American students and students in poverty.

30
Pct Districts No Librarian
5600000
Students Without Librarians
3000000
Students Majority Nonwhite Without Librarians
54
Pct Majority Nonwhite Districts Without
24
Pct Majority White Districts Without
20
National Decline Pct 2010 2019
25
States Lost 20Plus Pct
35
Rural Districts No Librarian Pct
25
Urban Districts No Librarian Pct
20
Suburban Districts No Librarian Pct
  • 3 million students in majority-nonwhite school districts attend schools without a librarian.
  • 54% of students in majority-nonwhite districts lack access to a school librarian, versus 24% in majority-white districts.
  • Schools with higher percentages of Black, Latino, and Native American students are more likely to have lost librarians.
  • Rural districts are particularly affected: 35% have no librarian at all.
  • Districts with the most students in poverty have lost librarians at 2-3x the rate of affluent districts.
  • The decline in school librarians accelerated after the Great Recession (2008) and has not recovered.
  • 25 states have lost 20% or more of their school librarians since 2010.
  • California lost 46% of its school librarians between 2010 and 2019, the largest decline of any state.

School Libraries — Key Findings

  • As of 2011-12 (the most recent NCES SASS school library survey), 81,200 of ~90,000 public schools (90.2%) reported having a library media center; no comparable federal survey has replaced SASS.
  • Only 66.4% of school library media centers had at least one full-time, state-certified library media specialist in 2011-12; 20.9% had no certified specialist at all.
  • There were 88,520 paid professional library media center staff in U.S. public schools in 2011-12, supported by 55,010 aides and 273,260 volunteers.
  • The average public school library held 12,780 book titles and 470 audio/video items, acquiring 520 new titles annually; average expenditure on information resources was $9,340 per school ($6,010 on books).
  • The SLIDE project documented an almost 20% national decline in full-time-equivalent school librarians between 2010 and 2019; three out of ten districts had no librarians in any of their schools.
  • Access to school librarians is sharply inequitable: 3 million students in majority-nonwhite districts had no librarians (54% of the 5.6M students in all no-librarian districts), versus 24% in majority-white districts.
  • Since 1992, more than 20 statewide impact studies led by Keith Curry Lance consistently link well-staffed, well-funded school libraries to higher student achievement, regardless of socioeconomic status.
  • There is no national qualification standard for school librarians; 17 states require certified librarians, 7 have partial mandates, and 7 have no mandate at all, including large states (CA, MI, OR, WA, AZ, ID, AK).

Library Science Degree Programs (ALA-Accredited)

To become a professional librarian, most positions require a Master's degree from an ALA-accredited Library and Information Studies program. There are currently 56 such programs in the United States (plus 8 in Canada), spread across only 35 states and territories. This means 15 states have NO ALA-accredited library school within their borders. The profession is increasingly accessible online: 32 programs are fully online-only, and 48 of 56 offer some distance education option.

Source: ALA Office for Accreditation, Directory of ALA-Accredited and Candidate Programs in Library and Information Studies (ala.org/educationcareers/accreditedprograms). The directory includes 65 programs total: 56 in the US, 8 in Canada, and 1 in the UK. Only 35 US states/territories have an accredited program, leaving 15 states without one. Programs are reviewed every 7 years under the 2014 Standards for Accreditation of Master's Programs in Library and Information Studies. The shift toward online education is striking: 32 programs are fully online-only, making library science one of the most accessible professional degrees.

Book Bans by State: The Censorship Map

Book censorship is not evenly distributed across America. Of 50 states with documented challenges, just five account for the vast majority of all book bans. Florida alone recorded 4,332 challenges with 1,139 books banned or removed. 17,421 of all challenges occurred in school libraries versus 1,533 in public libraries - making K-12 education the primary battleground for intellectual freedom in America.

"Challenges" include formal and informal attempts to remove or restrict books. "Banned/Removed" means the book was actually pulled from shelves. School library challenges overwhelmingly dominate the data, reflecting the concentration of censorship efforts in K-12 education. States with zero challenges may reflect lack of reporting rather than absence of censorship.

Federal Depository Library Program: Full Directory

The Federal Depository Library Program, established by Congress in 1813, ensures that the American public has free access to government information. Today, 1,082 libraries across 55 states and territories participate - 46 regional depositories (which retain all publications) and 1,036 selective depositories (which choose materials relevant to their communities). The oldest participating library was designated in 1814, making this one of the oldest federal information programs still in operation.

Source: U.S. Government Publishing Office (GPO) Federal Depository Library Program Directory, via ask.gpo.gov/s/FDLD. Academic libraries make up the largest share of FDLP participants, followed by public libraries. Regional depositories (one or two per state) serve as permanent repositories for all federal publications, while selective depositories tailor their collections to community needs. The program traces back to the Act of December 27, 1813, which authorized the distribution of one copy of every House and Senate journal to universities and historical societies.

America's Museums (IMLS Museum Data File 2018)

The IMLS oversees both libraries and museums, making it the only federal agency with this dual mandate. Its 2018 Museum Data File - the final edition, as IMLS has retired this dataset - identified 30,175 museums across all 50 states and DC. Nearly half (49%) are historical societies or historic preservation organizations, reflecting America's grassroots approach to local history. The IMLS funds museums and libraries through parallel grant programs, and 2,566 museums are affiliated with academic institutions that also host libraries.

Prison Libraries: The Hidden Gap

Over 1.25 million Americans are incarcerated in U.S. prisons, yet no federal agency tracks how many of those prisons have functioning libraries. The Bureau of Justice Statistics counts prisoners but not library services; the BOP does not publish a public inventory of facility libraries; and state departments of corrections report inconsistently. What we do know: the Prison Libraries Act of 2026 (H.R. 7247) would authorize $60M over 6 years for prison library services - the first federal legislation to specifically target this gap. ALA's 2025 report found many prison libraries are "little more than a few dusty shelves."

Key context

  • Pell Grant eligibility for incarcerated people reinstated July 1, 2023, ending the 1994 ban
  • ALA benchmark: adequate prison library funding should be ~$13 per capita per incarcerated person
  • ALA recidivism-savings ratio: every dollar invested in prison education saves ~5x in future incarceration costs
  • ALA released "Investing in Prison Libraries" report in June 2025
  • ALA Standards for Library Services for the Incarcerated or Detained: 2024 Revised Edition (first revision since 1992)

Source: Bureau of Justice Statistics (BJS) yearend 2023 prison statistics, ALA "Investing in Prison Libraries" (2025), Congress.gov (H.R. 7247 / S. 4320), and Ithaka S+R (2024). The central data limitation: no comprehensive national dataset on prison libraries exists. BJS counts prisoners but not libraries; BOP facility-library inventories are not public; state DOCs vary widely. The facility census most recently collected was 2019. The Prison Libraries Act would be the first federal legislation to specifically establish and expand prison library services.

Library of Congress: The World's Largest Library

Founded on April 24, 1800, the Library of Congress is the largest library in the world and the research arm of the U.S. Congress. From Thomas Jefferson's personal library of 6,487 books (purchased for $23,950 in 1815 after British troops burned the original collection) to more than 181.1 million items today, the LoC serves as both the national library of the United States and an international cultural repository. The Library operates across three buildings on Capitol Hill and employs 3,263 permanent staff.

Historical growth timeline

YearEvent
1800Library of Congress founded (April 24, 1800) with an appropriation of $5,000; initial collection of 740 books and 3 maps.
1814British troops burned the Capitol, destroying the Library's core collection of 3,000 volumes (August 24, 1814).
1815Congress approved purchase of Thomas Jefferson's personal library of 6,487 books for $23,950 (January 30, 1815).
1897Library moved into its own building (now the Thomas Jefferson Building), completed 1890-1897.
1939John Adams Building opened to the public (January 3, 1939).
1987James H. Billington became 13th Librarian of Congress; analog collections stood at 85.5 million items.
2014Analog collections exceeded 160 million items, having doubled under Billington (1987-2015).
2016Carla Hayden became 14th Librarian of Congress (first woman and first African American in the role).

Notable facts about the Library of Congress

  • Approximately half of the Library's book and serial collections are in languages other than English; collections contain materials in some 470 languages.
  • The Law Library of Congress is the world's largest law library.
  • The Library holds the largest rare-book collection in North America, including the largest collection of 15th-century books in the Western Hemisphere.
  • The Geography and Map Division holds the world's largest collection of cartographic materials.
  • The Prints and Photographs Division contains millions of visual images.
  • Each working day the Library receives some 15,000 items and adds roughly 10,000 to its collections (per fascinating-facts page).

Source: Library of Congress FY2024 Annual Report of the Librarian of Congress (loc.gov/about/reports-and-budgets/annual-reports/), loc.gov/about/fascinating-facts/, and Wikipedia. The Library operates with a total budget authority of $898M ($852M appropriations + $46M offsetting receipts). The Library employs 3,263 permanent staff (1,871 female, 1,392 male), with an average of 14 years of service. Collections span 13 categories including cataloged books, manuscripts, maps, photographs, audio materials, and moving images. The NLS served 219,589 blind and print-disabled readers in FY2024.

National Library of Medicine: The World's Largest Biomedical Library

Founded in 1836 (as the Library of the Office of the Surgeon General of the Army); established as the National Library of Medicine in 1956 as the Library of the Office of the Surgeon General, the National Library of Medicine is the world's largest biomedical library and a vital component of the National Institutes of Health. Located on the NIH campus in Bethesda, MD, NLM serves researchers, healthcare professionals, and the public through its extensive digital databases - most notably PubMed, which indexes over 40 million biomedical citations. Unlike most libraries, NLM's impact is felt primarily through its digital services rather than its physical collection of 27.8M items.

Network of the National Library of Medicine (NNLM)

NNLM is coordinated by 14 institutions across the United States. Member organizations provide health professionals and the general public with health information resources and services. The network promotes and provides access to health information in communities across the United States.

Historical timeline

YearEvent
1836Founded as the Library of the Office of the Surgeon General of the Army with $150 for medical books; first catalog of books issued in 1840
1862Library moved to Riggs Bank Building at 15th Street and Pennsylvania Avenue, NW, Washington, DC
1865Lieutenant John Shaw Billings appointed to take charge of the collection, marking a turning point in the library's development
1866Library moved to Ford's Theatre (after Lincoln's assassination) at 513 10th Street NW, Washington, DC; remained until 1887
1879Billings began Index Medicus, the first comprehensive index of journal articles; predecessor to MEDLINE
1880Index-Catalogue launched, a combined author and subject catalog of the library's growing collection
1887Library moved to Army Medical Museum and Library Building at 7th Street and Independence Avenue SW, Washington, DC (remained until 1962)
1895By Billings' departure, holdings reached 116,847 books and 191,598 pamphlets; transformed into the world's greatest medical library
1922Officially renamed the Army Medical Library
1956Library transferred from Department of Defense to the Public Health Service and renamed the National Library of Medicine via Public Law 941 (August 3, 1956)
1958Library took first steps into the computer age, launching a mechanized system for producing its publications
1962Library moved to its current quarters on the NIH campus in Bethesda, Maryland

Notable facts

  • World's largest biomedical library, founded in 1836 as a small collection of medical books in the office of the U.S. Army Surgeon General
  • Located on the campus of the National Institutes of Health in Bethesda, Maryland, since 1962
  • An institute within the National Institutes of Health (NIH), part of the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services
  • Collection of 27.8 million items (2015), including 7+ million books, journals, technical reports, manuscripts, microfilms, photographs, and images
  • Annual budget of US$341,119,000 (FY2016 appropriation)
  • Employs approximately 1,741 staff
  • Electronic information resources searched billions of times each year by millions of people around the globe
  • Coordinates the Network of the National Library of Medicine (NNLM) with 8,000+ member organizations across 7 regions
  • PubMed contains 40+ million citations and abstracts (as of March 2025)
  • PubMed Central archives 10.8 million full-text biomedical and life sciences articles

Source: nlm.nih.gov/about/, en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_National_Library_of_Medicine, ncbi.nlm.nih.gov (GenBank statistics), nnlm.gov. NLM was founded in 1836 (as the Library of the Office of the Surgeon General of the Army); established as the National Library of Medicine in 1956 and established as the National Library of Medicine by the National Library of Medicine Act of 1956 (Public Law 941). Collection size of 27.8M items is from 2015; budget of $341M is FY2016 appropriation — more recent figures were not available from live .gov budget pages. PubMed contains 40M+ citations (March 2025); PubMed Central holds 10.8M full-text articles; ClinicalTrials.gov lists 444K+ trials from 221 countries; GenBank contains 53.9 trillion bases in 6.27 billion sequence records. The NNLM network connects 8,000+ member organizations across 7 regions.

Digital Libraries: HathiTrust, Internet Archive & Beyond

Beyond the physical library system, a parallel digital infrastructure has emerged over the past two decades. These digital libraries collectively hold billions of items - from the Internet Archive's 835+ billion archived web pages to HathiTrust's 18M digitized book volumes. Together they represent a massive expansion of access to human knowledge, though one constrained by copyright law, funding sustainability, and ongoing legal challenges.

HathiTrust Digital Library

HathiTrust is a large-scale collaborative repository of digital content from research libraries and publishers worldwide. Founded by the twelve universities of the Committee on Institutional Cooperation (now the Big Ten Academic Alliance) and the University of California system, it preserves and provides access to the cultural record. Content includes items digitized by the Google Books project, Internet Archive, and local library digitization initiatives.

MetricValue
Total digitized volumes18,000,000
Public domain volumes (US)6,700,000
Member institutions219
Shared print volumes (25-yr retention)18,000,000
FoundedOctober 2008
  • 18M+ digitized volumes (Sept 2024)
  • 6.7M volumes in the U.S. public domain
  • 219+ member research libraries across the US, Canada, and Europe
  • Shared Print Program: 18M monograph volumes committed for 25-year retention
  • Content from Google Books, Internet Archive, and local digitization
  • Emergency Temporary Access Service (ETAS) provided controlled access during COVID closures
  • Authors Guild v. HathiTrust (2014): court upheld transformative use for accessibility and research

Internet Archive

The Internet Archive is a nonprofit digital library offering free universal access to books, movies, music, and 835+ billion archived web pages. Founded by Brewster Kahle in 1996, it is one of the world's largest digital repositories, preserving cultural artifacts in digital format for researchers, historians, and the general public.

Founded1996
  • 835+ billion web pages archived in the Wayback Machine
  • 44M+ books and texts digitized
  • 15M+ video recordings (including 2.5M TV news programs)
  • 15M+ audio recordings (including Live Music Archive, 78rpm records)
  • 1.3M+ software programs (including classic games, shareware)
  • Founded 1996 by Brewster Kahle, headquartered in San Francisco
  • Controlled digital lending: lends digitized books one-at-a-time under copyright law
  • Open Library: catalog of every published book, 30M+ editions
  • Archive-It: web archiving service used by 600+ institutions
  • 30+ scanning centers in 5 countries

Other major digital libraries

LibraryFoundedKey figureDescription
Project Gutenberg197178,000 eBooksProject Gutenberg is the oldest digital library, founded in 1971 by Michael S. Hart at the University of Illinois. It was the first project to make books available digitally and focuses on cultural works in the public domain, primarily books for which copyright has expired.
Google Books200240,000,000 titles scannedGoogle Books is a service from Google that searches the full text of books and magazines that Google has scanned, converted to text using optical character recognition, and stored in its digital database. Launched in 2002, it has scanned over 40 million titles from library partners, making it the largest book digitization project in history.
Smithsonian Open AccessFebruary 25, 20205,000,000 CC0 imagesSmithsonian Open Access is an initiative launched on February 25, 2020, that makes millions of 2D and 3D images from the Smithsonian's collections available for download and unrestricted use under a Creative Commons Zero (CC0) license. The Smithsonian operates 21 museums, 21 libraries, and 9 research centers.

Library Philanthropy: Carnegie, Friends & the Gates Foundation

Before government funding, there was philanthropy. Andrew Carnegie alone funded 1,689 of the 2,509 library buildings he built worldwide - nearly half of all American libraries in existence by 1919. A century later, the Gates Foundation committed over $1 billion to wire libraries into the digital age. Between these bookends sits a vast ecosystem of Friends of the Library groups (thousands nationally, raising millions annually through book sales and membership drives), library endowments worth billions, and modern foundations whose grant budgets quietly shape the direction of American humanities and literacy.

Andrew Carnegie's Library Empire (1889-1919)

1883-1929 (first grant 1880 to Dunfermline, Scotland; first US commission 1886 Allegheny, PA; first US opening 1889 Braddock, PA; last grant shortly before Carnegie's death in 1919). By the time the last grant was made, Carnegie had funded construction of nearly half of the 3,500 libraries then in the United States. Women's clubs led establishment of 75-80% of libraries in communities across the country. Under segregation, Carnegie funded separate libraries for African Americans in the South (e.g., Houston Colored Carnegie Library; Carnegie Library of Savannah, GA opened 1914).

Libraries built worldwide2,509
Libraries built in United States1,689

Total US grant dollars (by-state parsed)$40,000,000
NYPL branch grant (1901)$5.2M for 65 branches
Endowed librariesBraddock, Homestead, and Duquesne libraries (southwestern PA) were owned and maintained by Carnegie Steel. Four libraries were fully endowed by Carnegie.

Friends of the Library: grassroots fundraising

Non-profit charitable volunteer groups formed to support libraries in their communities. Support is financial, political, and cultural. Groups are separate from the libraries they support and made up of volunteers; generally structured with memberships, meetings, bylaws, and 501(c)(3) tax-exempt status. Typical activities: used-book sales, membership drives, retail shops inside libraries, advocacy/lobbying, funding furniture/equipment/books/programming, and capital campaigns for new buildings.

MetricValue
Estimated national countthousands (no single registry
First US Friends group1922 — Glen Ellyn, Illinois
Historical growth1973: 1,000 groups / 100,000 members; 1979: 2,000 groups / 500,000 members

United for Libraries (ALA division)

FOLUSA joined with ALTA to form United for Libraries in 2009. ALA's Friends of the Library Committee predates this. Library Trustees, Friends of the Library groups, and library Foundations.

  • National Friends of Libraries Week (21st annual held Oct. 18-24, 2026; began 2006)
  • Literary Landmark program (60+ sites designated by 2009)
  • Trustee Academy and Short Takes for Trustees
  • Citizens-Save-Libraries Power Guide
  • Books for Babies
  • Awards & Grants for Friends/Foundations and Trustees
  • Statewide Training for Trustees, Friends, Foundations, and Library Staff (12 partner states: IL, IN, MA, MD, MI, MN, ND, NE, NV, OR, SC, VA)
  • Penguin Random House Grant to Rural and Small Libraries (with United for Libraries)

Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation: wiring libraries (1997-2018)

Introduced a U.S. Libraries initiative in 1997 with the goal of connecting public libraries to the internet. The foundation gave grants, installed computers and software, and provided training and technical support in partnership with public libraries nationwide to increase access and knowledge, helping move public libraries into the digital age. The international Global Libraries program extended this work to public libraries outside the U.S.

MetricValue
ProgramGlobal Libraries (international) and U.S. Libraries Program
US initiative launched1997
US reachReached public library systems across all 50 U.S. states with computers, internet access, and training.
Estimated total committedExceeds $1 billion cumulative (US + international library programs, 1997-2018); widely reported figure. Exact official total not stated on current gatesfoundation.org pages (JS-rendered; archived pages confirm program scope but not a single published total).
Program wound downGlobal Libraries program wound down ~2018 as internet access at libraries became near-universal; foundation shifted focus.

Adopt-a-Book & community programs

Adopt-a-Book programs allow donors to sponsor the purchase or conservation of specific library books, often with bookplates recognizing the donor. Run by individual libraries, their Friends groups, and affiliated foundations.

OrganizationNotes
Smithsonian LibrariesOperates an Adopt-a-Book program supporting acquisition and conservation of its 3-million-volume collection; grew through donations (e.g., 1976 gift of 10,000 rare scientific books from the Burndy Library).
ALA United for LibrariesPromotes Adopt-a-Book and similar donor programs among member Friends groups and foundations via awards, grants, and resources.

Crowdfunding & community fundraising

  • Friends Groups Book Sales: Used-book sales are a major revenue source for thousands of Friends of the Library groups; book sales can be major community events. Friends also receive book donations to resell.
  • Membership Drives: Annual membership drives are a primary fundraising method for Friends groups (membership fees/donations).
  • Library Retail Shops: Some Friends groups sell items in retail space inside the libraries they support (e.g., Friends of the Library Shop at Salt Lake City Main Library).
  • Capital Campaigns: Some Friends groups formed specifically to build new libraries (e.g., Naples, FL Friends formed 1957 to build Collier County library; accepted donated land leased to the city).
  • Statewide Friends Groups: State-level Friends organizations coordinate advocacy and fundraising across local groups (statewide groups began forming in the 1960s).
  • Modern Crowdfunding: Platforms such as GoFundMe, Kickstarter, and DonorsChoose are used by libraries and Friends groups for project-specific campaigns; ALA and United for Libraries provide fundraising toolkits (e.g., Citizens-Save-Libraries Power Guide). No single published total for library crowdfunding exists.
  • Ala Toolkits: ALA/United for Libraries provide toolkits, factsheets, listservs, free toolkits, and contact information for State Friends Groups to support local fundraising and advocacy.
  • Source: Wikipedia: Friends of Libraries; ALA United for Libraries

Key facts

  • Andrew Carnegie funded 2,509 libraries worldwide (1883-1929), including 1,689 in the United States, at a cost of ~$56.2 million (~$40 million for 1,670 US public library buildings in 1,412 communities).
  • By the time of Carnegie's last library grant, he had funded construction of nearly half of the 3,500 libraries then in the United States.
  • The first US Friends of the Library group was founded in 1922 in Glen Ellyn, Illinois; the first university Friends group at Harvard in 1925.
  • By 1973 there were ~1,000 Friends groups with over 100,000 members; by 1979 (at FOLUSA's formation) ~2,000 groups with ~500,000 members.
  • Friends of Libraries U.S.A. (FOLUSA, founded 1979) merged with the Association for Library Trustees and Advocates (ALTA) in 2009 to form ALA's United for Libraries, serving Trustees, Friends, and Foundations.
  • A 1987 FOLUSA study found Friends groups averaged 213 members and raised $9,408 per year for furniture, equipment, books, and programming.
  • The Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation's U.S. Libraries initiative began in 1997 to connect public libraries to the internet, reaching library systems across all 50 states with computers, software, and training; the international Global Libraries program extended this to 30+ countries.
  • The Gates Foundation committed more than $1 billion cumulatively to library programs (US and global) before winding down Global Libraries ~2018.
  • The New York Public Library holds an endowment of ~$1.45 billion (2017), founded from the Astor, Lenox, and Tilden Foundations and Carnegie's $5.2 million grant for 65 branches.
  • The Andrew W. Mellon Foundation, with a ~$6.1 billion endowment and a $540 million annual grant budget (2024), is a leading funder of libraries, humanities, and scholarly communication.
  • The Carnegie Corporation of New York (founded 1911 with a $135 million endowment) holds a ~$4.1 billion endowment (2022) and continues Carnegie's knowledge-advancement mission.
  • ALA's United for Libraries coordinates the National Friends of Libraries Week (21st annual in 2026), Literary Landmark program (60+ sites by 2009), and statewide training in 12 states.
  • Alaska and Delaware are the only US states with no Carnegie libraries.
  • Indiana (165), California (142), and Illinois/New York/Ohio (106 each) had the most Carnegie public libraries by state.
  • Women's clubs led the establishment of 75-80% of libraries in communities across the country during Carnegie's era.
  • Under segregation, Carnegie funded separate libraries for African Americans in the South (e.g., Houston Colored Carnegie Library; Savannah, GA Carnegie Library opened 1914).
  • Adopt-a-Book and similar sponsor-a-book programs operate at many libraries (e.g., Smithsonian Libraries) and are promoted by ALA United for Libraries.

Source: Wikipedia articles for Andrew Carnegie library grants, Friends of Libraries, Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation, Carnegie Corporation of New York, Andrew W. Mellon Foundation, and New York Public Library (citing annual reports, the Carnegie Corporation history page, and FOLUSA/ALA United for Libraries materials). Carnegie built 1,689 of 2,509 worldwide libraries across 50 states; by-state dollar total parsed from the Wikipedia detail table to $40,000,000. Friends group counts are historical snapshots (no single national registry exists). The Gates Foundation's cumulative library commitment is widely reported as exceeding $1 billion, though the foundation does not publish a single official total. Endowment figures are as of the year cited in each source.

Circulation & Library Cards: What Americans Borrow

Every year Americans check out 1.71 billion items from public libraries — 5.0 items for every person in the country. 155 million Americans hold library cards. Of all circulation, 37% is children's materials and 18% is electronic (e-books and e-audiobooks), a figure that was effectively zero just two decades ago. The library card — the most powerful free tool in America — is held by roughly 45% of the population served.

Key facts

  • Americans checked out 1.71 billion items from public libraries in FY2022 - roughly 5.2 items per person in the United States.
  • 155 million Americans hold library cards - about 45% of the population served by public libraries.
  • Electronic (e-book/e-audio) circulation reached 316 million, about 18.5% of total circulation - up from near-zero two decades ago.
  • Children's materials account for 36.9% of circulation (630M items), making children the single largest user demographic by material type.
  • Americans made 869 million visits to public libraries - 2.5 visits per capita.
  • Libraries hosted 5.1 million programs attended by 105 million people.
  • 101 million people used public library computers/internet in FY2022.
  • Public library collections hold 639 million book volumes nationally.

COVID-19 impact: circulation & visits FY2019-FY2024

Circulation peaked at ~2.19B items in FY2019 before the pandemic, fell ~25% in FY2020, rebounded to ~1.96B by FY2023, then eased as digital borrowing normalized. Physical visits collapsed ~42% in FY2020 and have only partially recovered.

Who holds a library card? (Pew Research)

Card holders are more likely to be female, white, under age 65, and suburban/urban residents, and to have higher levels of education or live in higher-income households. Pew Research Center's Library Services survey (2013) found that 61% of Americans age 16+ have a library card (down from 63% in 2012). 86% have used a public library at some point; 54% used one in the past 12 months.

The ALA estimate vs. IMLS count

The IMLS Public Libraries Survey counts 155M registered borrowers — but the American Library Association widely cites an estimate of ~166-170M Americans with library cards. The difference reflects methodology: IMLS counts active registered borrowers reported by each library system, while the ALA advocacy figure counts active and household cards and is often used in public messaging. Both are legitimate measures; the IMLS figure is the authoritative government statistic.

Source: IMLS Public Libraries Survey FY2022 (latest finalized PLS data), compiled via ALA State of America's Libraries 2024 report. Covers 48 states and territories with circulation data. Total circulation (1.71B) includes physical and electronic materials; electronic circulation (316M, 18%) is reported separately and may overlap with total_circulation depending on each state's reporting methodology. Registered borrowers (155M) are active library card holders as reported by each state. IMLS uses negative sentinels (-1, -3, -40) for suppressed/unreported values, normalized to 0. Per-capita figures divide by population served (min 1,000). ALA designates September as Library Card Sign-Up Month.

Five-Year Trends: COVID Shock & Recovery (FY2019-FY2024)

The IMLS Public Libraries Survey captures a natural experiment: FY2019 is the last pre-pandemic baseline, FY2020 shows the COVID shock, and FY2022-FY2024 trace the recovery. The story is not a simple bounce-back. Circulation peaked at 2.19B items in FY2019, crashed 25% in FY2020, rebounded to 1.96B by FY2023, then settled back to 1.71B in FY2024. Physical visits have recovered only partially — from 1254M to 869M (-30.7% vs pre-pandemic). Meanwhile, total income rose to $17.9B as federal relief funds flowed in.

Key facts

  • Pre-pandemic FY2019: 1.25B visits and 2.19B items circulated.
  • FY2020 COVID impact: visits fell 42%, circulation fell 25%.
  • FY2023 recovery: circulation rebounded to 1.96B (-10.2% vs FY2019).
  • FY2024: circulation eased to 1.71B as digital borrowing normalized.
  • Total library income was $17.9B in FY2024.
  • Libraries employed 143K staff (FTE) in FY2024, down 1.9% from FY2019.
  • Programs hosted: 5.9M in FY2019 → 5.1M in FY2024 (-14.2% change).

Source: IMLS Public Libraries Survey (PLS) historical trends FY2019-FY2024, compiled via ALA State of America's Libraries. FY2021 is omitted from the source due to IMLS data-structure changes during that collection cycle. All values aggregated from per-state PLS submissions across 56 reporting states/territories. IMLS negative sentinels (-1, -3, -40) normalized to 0. Percent changes are vs. the FY2019 pre-pandemic baseline. Income includes local, state, federal, and other revenue. The FY2024 circulation dip (from FY2023's rebound) reflects normalization of digital borrowing patterns and partial reporting adjustments.

Accessibility & Disability Services

Library service for blind, deaf, disabled, and homebound Americans dates to the Pratt-Smoot Act of 1931, which created what is now the Library of Congress National Library Service for the Blind and Print Disabled (NLS). Today NLS circulates more than 22 million items annually through a network of 101 cooperating libraries. The ADA (1990) extended accessibility obligations to every public library. This section traces the infrastructure — from braille and talking books to BARD digital downloads, refreshable braille eReaders, and sensory-friendly programming — that ensures libraries serve all Americans.

National Library Service for the Blind and Print Disabled (NLS)

The NLS, part of the Library of Congress, provides free braille and talking-book services to U.S. residents who are blind, visually impaired, or physically unable to read standard print.

Americans with Disabilities Act & libraries

Covered entities (including public libraries) must provide auxiliary aids and services when needed to communicate effectively with people who have vision, hearing, or speech disabilities, so that communication is equally effective as with people without disabilities.

MetricValue
ActAmericans with Disabilities Act of 1990
Enacted1990
Revised regulationsSeptember 15, 2010 (2010 Standards for Accessible Design)
Effective communication rulesMarch 15, 2011

Assistive technology in libraries

  • Standard Digital Talking Book Machine: 8 controls; basic playback (volume, tone, rewind, fast forward, variable speed); built-in rechargeable battery; internal audio user guide; key describer mode
  • Advanced Digital Talking Book Machine: Additional controls for bookmarks and structured navigation (chapters, sections); same power/features as standard model
  • High Volume Player: Amplified volume up to 120 dB for patrons who are hard of hearing; paired with NLS-provided stereo headphones; available as standard or advanced; bears warning label
  • Refreshable Braille Displays: Loan of refreshable braille displays permitted since 2016; approximately 8,500 Braille eReader devices deployed nationwide by FY2024

Bookmobiles & homebound delivery

Bookmobiles bring library services to rural communities, retirement centers, and homebound patrons who cannot visit a physical library. In FY2024, 767 bookmobiles operated nationally. The U.S. Postal Service complements this through "Free Matter for the Blind" mail for NLS patrons.

Indiana26
