Sign language services

Under the ADA, covered entities including public libraries must provide qualified sign language interpreters (ASL, oral, cued-speech, or tactile interpreters) when needed for effective communication with people who are deaf or hard of hearing.

Interpreter types

  • qualified sign language interpreter (ASL)
  • oral interpreter
  • cued-speech interpreter
  • tactile interpreter (for deaf-blind)

Sensory-friendly & autism-friendly programming

Sensory-friendly and autism-friendly programming in U.S. libraries includes sensory-friendly hours (reduced lighting, lowered noise, fewer patrons), quiet spaces/sensory rooms, autism-friendly storytimes, and adapted programming for neurodivergent patrons.

Common features

  • Sensory-friendly hours with dimmed lights and reduced noise
  • Quiet spaces and sensory/calming rooms
  • Autism-friendly storytimes with predictable structure and visual schedules
  • Fidget tools and noise-canceling headphones available
  • Social stories describing library visits in advance
  • Trained staff on autism awareness and de-escalation

Digital accessibility

Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG), developed by the W3C Web Accessibility Initiative, are the prevailing standard for accessible library websites. Section 508 of the Rehabilitation Act requires federal electronic and information technology (including federal library websites such as loc.gov/nls) to be accessible; the LOC NLS site itself provides an accessibility toolbar (increase/decrease text, high contrast, negative contrast, light background, underline links).

History of library accessibility

YearEvent
1931Pratt-Smoot Act signed by President Herbert Hoover on March 3, 1931 (introduced by Ruth Baker Pratt and Reed Smoot); established free library service for blind adults with embossed (braille) books, $100,000 appropriation administered by the Library of Congress. Operations began July 1, 1931. J. Robert Atkinson of the Braille Institute of America was instrumental in lobbying for the act.
1934Act amended to include sound recordings ('talking books'), beginning the Talking Book program.
1934NLS pioneered popularization of the 33 1/3 rpm record for talking books; early talking books were also recorded on vinyl at 16 2/3 rpm (half normal album speed, running twice as long).
1952Program expanded to include blind children.
1962Program expanded to include music materials (NLS music collection authorized by Congress, including braille and large-print musical scores).
1966Program expanded to include individuals with physical impairments that prevent reading standard print.
1990Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) enacted, applying Title II (state/local government, including public libraries) and Title III (public accommodations) accessibility and effective communication requirements.
1996Chafee Amendment (PL 104-197) added 17 U.S.C. section 121, allowing authorized entities (nonprofit organizations and governmental agencies) to reproduce or distribute copyrighted nondramatic literary works in specialized formats exclusively for use by blind or other persons with disabilities.
2009NLS launched the digital talking-book program, transitioning from cassette tapes to digital cartridges played on NLS digital talking-book machines; BARD (Braille and Audio Reading Download) launched for online downloads.
2013BARD Mobile app launched for iOS and Android, enabling direct playback of braille and audio materials on personal smartphones and tablets (including Kindle Fire).
2016NLS permitted to provide/loan refreshable braille displays to patrons.
2018Marrakesh Treaty implementation allowed NLS to exchange accessible-format books across international borders via WIPO's Accessible Books Consortium, enabling rapid growth of its foreign-language collection.
2021Jason Broughton became NLS's 11th director (September 2021); NLS marked its 90th anniversary, highlighting the role of blinded World War I veterans in its founding and its history of innovation.
2024NLS completed the nationwide rollout of the Braille eReader program, deploying approximately 8,500 refreshable braille display devices to all states, territories, and network libraries. In FY2024 NLS circulated more than 22.2 million copies and served 219,589 readers.

Key facts

  • The National Library Service for the Blind and Print Disabled (NLS), part of the Library of Congress, was founded in 1931 by the Pratt-Smoot Act (signed March 3, 1931 by President Hoover) and is the primary U.S. federal program providing free braille and talking-book library service to people who are blind or have visual, physical, or reading disabilities. (Wikipedia (Pratt-Smoot Act, NLS) + loc.gov/nls)
  • In FY2024, NLS circulated more than 22.2 million copies of braille, audio, and large-print items to 219,589 readers, with a collection of 332,077 titles, supported by a $66.13 million appropriation. (Library of Congress FY2024 Annual Report (Table 12))
  • NLS operates through a nationwide network of 101 cooperating libraries (55 regional, 32 subregional, and 14 advisory/outreach centers) serving every U.S. state and territory, grown from an original network of 19 libraries in 1931. (Wikipedia (NLS, citing loc.gov/nls About Local Network Libraries))
  • BARD (Braille and Audio Reading Download), NLS's digital download service, recorded 4,257,667 audio downloads in FY2024 and is accessible via the BARD Mobile app for iOS and Android; 46,646 patrons used BARD in FY2024. (Library of Congress FY2024 Annual Report (Table 12))
  • The Americans with Disabilities Act of 1990 applies to public libraries under Title II (state/local government) and Title III (public accommodations), requiring effective communication through auxiliary aids and services (e.g., qualified sign language interpreters, real-time captioning, screen-reader-compatible materials, Braille, large print) and compliance with the 2010 Standards for Accessible Design. (ADA.gov (Effective Communication))
  • Under the Marrakesh Treaty in FY2024, NLS acquired 1,987 works in 8 languages (1,240 audiobooks, 741 braille books, and 6 braille music scores); the WIPO Accessible Books Consortium holds 202,771 books originally produced by NLS or its network libraries, downloaded about 10,000 times by readers in 35 countries. (Library of Congress FY2024 Annual Report)
  • NLS completed the nationwide rollout of the Braille eReader program in FY2024, deploying approximately 8,500 refreshable braille display devices to all states and territories; NLS was first permitted to loan refreshable braille displays in 2016. (Library of Congress FY2024 Annual Report + Wikipedia (NLS))
  • There were 767 bookmobiles operating nationally in FY2024 (IMLS Public Libraries Survey), with Kentucky (74), Ohio (68), and California (65) leading; bookmobiles serve rural areas, retirement/senior care homes, homebound, and disabled patrons with books, internet access, and large-print materials. (IMLS Public Libraries Survey FY2024 via ALA + Wikipedia (Bookmobile))
  • NLS provides a high-volume digital talking-book machine amplified up to 120 dB with a paired headset for patrons who are hard of hearing, alongside standard and advanced digital players, all at no cost; materials are mailed postage-free as 'Free Matter for the Blind.' (Library of Congress NLS FAQ)
  • The Chafee Amendment of 1996 (17 U.S.C. section 121) allows authorized nonprofit entities and governmental agencies to reproduce copyrighted works in specialized formats exclusively for blind or other persons with disabilities, enabling NLS and partner organizations to produce accessible books without seeking individual publisher permissions. (Wikipedia (NLS))

Source: Library of Congress NLS FY2024 Annual Report (Table 12 + narrative), NLS website (loc.gov/nls), Wikipedia articles on NLS, the Pratt-Smoot Act, and the Talking Book program, ADA.gov effective-communication guidance, and IMLS Public Libraries Survey FY2024 (bookmobile counts via ALA). NLS circulated 22,188,638 items to 219,589 readers in FY2024; the commonly cited ~500,000 figure refers to the cumulative eligible-reader base, while 219,589 is the active annual count. BARD (Braille and Audio Reading Download) recorded 0 downloads. NLS operates through 0 cooperating libraries serving every state and territory.

Programs & Events: What Happens at the Library

Libraries are no longer just places to borrow books. In FY2022, US public libraries hosted 5.1 million programs attended by 105 million people — more than the combined populations of California and Texas. Children's storytimes, adult education classes, summer reading programs, maker workshops, and the pandemic-era explosion of online programming have transformed the library into a community programming hub. The average program draws 20.7 attendees.

Key facts

  • US public libraries hosted 5.1 million programs in FY2022, attended by 105 million people.
  • Children's programs (2.4M) and adult programs (1.7M) are the two largest program categories.
  • Online programs (4.3M) emerged during COVID and continue as a permanent service modality.
  • The average library program draws 20.7 attendees.
  • California leads in total program attendance (9,122,631).
  • Vermont leads in programs per 10,000 residents (478.16).

Source: IMLS Public Libraries Survey FY2022, compiled via ALA State of America's Libraries 2024. Program categories: children's, young adult (YA), adult, and online. Online programs were added as a reporting category during the COVID-19 era and represent a permanent shift in service delivery. IMLS negative sentinels normalized to 0. Per-capita and per-10K figures computed using population served (min 1,000).

Technology & Digital Inclusion: Libraries as Internet Gateways

For millions of Americans without home internet, the public library IS the internet. US public libraries provide 260,456 public-access computers and hosted 101 million internet-user sessions in FY2024, plus hundreds of millions of WiFi sessions. The Gates Foundation spent over $1 billion (1997-2018) wiring libraries into the digital age. Today, libraries draw ~$235M/year in E-rate discounts to keep their broadband affordable, and have become hubs for hotspot lending, makerspaces, and technology training.

WiFi access

Free public Wi-Fi is effectively near-universal at U.S. public libraries; ALA reported 98% in 2015 and earlier studies showed 91% in 2012. By the mid-2020s free Wi-Fi is a baseline expectation at virtually all public library outlets.

E-rate: federal broadband subsidies for libraries

The E-rate program (Universal Service Fund) provides discounts of 20-90% on telecommunications and internet services for schools and libraries. Library-attributed E-rate commitments total $0.00B cumulatively (FY2016-FY2026), averaging ~$235M/year.

Program budget (total, schools+libraries)~$3.9B/year

The digital divide: libraries as the internet of last resort

Approximately 23% of Americans lack a home broadband connection and 7% (~23 million people) do not use the internet at all (Wikipedia: Digital divide in the United States). Public libraries are the primary no-fee internet access point for these populations.

MetricValue
Americans without home broadband23%
Americans with no internet at all23M (7%)

Hotspot lending

No central registry publishes the number of U.S. public libraries offering mobile hotspot lending. Industry reporting (American Libraries Magazine; Public Libraries Online) documents widespread adoption among urban and suburban systems beginning ~2013. ALA describes 'a decade of library hotspot and device lending expertise' as of 2023. No central national count of participating libraries exists.

Makerspaces

IMLS PLS does not track makerspace presence as a reported field. No central national count is published. Industry surveys (ALA/PLA, American Libraries Magazine) indicate makerspaces spread rapidly after 2011 and are now offered by a large share of medium/large urban library systems, but a precise national total is not centrally aggregated. No central national count exists.

Technology training

As of 2012, 90% of public libraries offered formal or informal technology training (Wikipedia: Public library). Digital-literacy and tech-help programs remain a core service; libraries frequently offer computer classes, walk-in tech help, one-on-one device assistance, and coding clubs.

Gates Foundation legacy (1997-2018)

Introduced in 1997 to connect U.S. public libraries to the internet. The foundation gave grants, installed computers and software, and provided training and technical support in partnership with public libraries nationwide, helping move public libraries into the digital age.

MetricValue
Program start1997
ReachAll 50 states
Cumulative commitment>$1 billion

Technology timeline

YearMilestone
Telecommunications Act of 1996 (Section 254) authorized the Universal Service Fund and E-rate, making libraries eligible for telecom/internet discounts.
Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation launched the U.S. Libraries Program to connect public libraries to the internet.
First public library makerspace opened at Fayetteville Free Library (NY), launching the library makerspace movement.
FCC E-rate Modernization (July 2014) targeted $1B/year for Wi-Fi; December 2014 raised total E-rate budget from $2.4B to $3.9B.
Gates Foundation wound down Global Libraries as internet access at libraries became near-universal.
COVID-19 pandemic: libraries expanded outdoor/parking-lot Wi-Fi and hotspot lending to bridge the homework gap while buildings were closed; telehealth interest grew.
FCC Chairwoman Rosenworcel unveiled Learn Without Limits, proposing permanent E-rate eligibility for off-premises Wi-Fi hotspots for patrons/students; FCC improved E-rate rules to make more Tribal libraries eligible.
FCC added off-premises hotspot lending and school-bus Wi-Fi eligibility under E-rate. IMLS PLS FY2024: ~260,000 public internet terminals, ~100.9M public-internet-user sessions, ~418M Wi-Fi sessions.
October 2025: FCC voted 2-1 to end E-rate support for Wi-Fi hotspots and school-bus Wi-Fi; ALA and coalitions mobilized to 'Save Our E-rate.'

Key facts

  • U.S. public libraries provided ~260,000 public-access internet computers and recorded ~100.9 million public-internet-user sessions in FY2024 (IMLS Public Libraries Survey).
  • U.S. public libraries recorded ~418 million Wi-Fi sessions in FY2024 (IMLS PLS field WIRESRU); combined with public-computer sessions, libraries hosted ~519 million in-library internet uses in a single year.
  • Free public Wi-Fi is near-universal at U.S. public libraries: ALA reported 98% of public libraries offered free Wi-Fi in 2015, up from 91% in 2012 (Wikipedia: Digital divide in the United States; Wikipedia: Public library).
  • The E-rate program (USAC/FCC, authorized by the 1996 Telecommunications Act) provides 20-90% discounts on telecom, internet, and internal connections for libraries and schools; the total program budget is ~$3.9B/year (raised from $2.4B in 2014).
  • Library-attributed E-rate commitments totaled ~$2.28 billion over FY2016-FY2026 (~$235M/year average), with fiber accounting for 64% of library E-rate spending — most libraries now have fiber or high-speed connections (this project's USAC Form 471 analysis).
  • ALA reports 24 million U.S. households still lack home internet, making libraries essential to closing the digital divide (ALA Broadband page).
  • Pew Research (2013, 6,224 Americans 16+) found 56% of internet users without home access say public libraries' basic technological resources (computers, internet, printers) are 'very important' to them and their family, vs 33% of all respondents; 77% of those without home access say library tech is important overall.
  • Approximately 23% of Americans lack a home broadband connection and 7% (~23 million people) do not use the internet at all; public libraries are the primary no-fee internet access point for these populations (Wikipedia: Digital divide in the United States).
  • The Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation's U.S. Libraries Program (1997-2018) connected public libraries to the internet across all 50 states; in 2003 alone it contributed $250 million to install 47,000+ computers and train librarians in ~11,000 libraries. The foundation committed more than $1 billion cumulatively to library programs before winding down Global Libraries in 2018.
  • The first public library makerspace opened at Fayetteville Free Library (New York) in 2011; library makerspaces typically offer 3D printers, laser cutters, 3D scanners, coding/robotics kits, and audio/video tools (Wikipedia: Library makerspace).
  • Library hotspot lending grew rapidly 2013-2023; the 2023 FCC Learn Without Limits initiative proposed permanent E-rate eligibility for off-premises Wi-Fi hotspots, building on 'a decade of library hotspot and device lending expertise' (ALA State of America's Libraries 2024).
  • As of 2012, 90% of public libraries offered formal or informal technology training; for many communities the library is the only agency offering free computer classes and digital-skills learning (Wikipedia: Public library).
  • In October 2025 the FCC voted 2-1 to end E-rate support for Wi-Fi hotspots and school-bus Wi-Fi, prompting ALA and coalition partners to launch a 'Save Our E-rate' campaign (Wikipedia: E-Rate; ALA Broadband page).
  • In 2006, 73% of library branches reported being the only local provider of free public computer and internet access; a 2008 study found 100% of rural, high-poverty library outlets provided public internet access (Wikipedia: Public library).

Source: IMLS Public Libraries Survey FY2024 (public internet terminals, sessions, WiFi sessions), USAC E-rate Form 471 data (library-filtered commitments FY2016-FY2026), Pew Research Center Internet & American Life Project (2013/2016/2019), ALA State of America's Libraries 2024, and Wikipedia articles (Public library, E-Rate, Digital divide, Gates Foundation, Library makerspace) citing primary sources. The E-rate program's total budget is ~$3.9B/year for schools and libraries combined; the library-attributed share averages ~$235M/year. Where no single authoritative national count exists (hotspot lending, makerspace counts), this is noted rather than estimated.

Tribal & Indigenous Libraries

There are 574 federally recognized Native American tribes in the United States, yet no federal agency maintains a complete census of tribal libraries. IMLS estimates 200-300+ tribal libraries exist across Indian Country, many operating on shoestring budgets through Basic Grants of just ~$7,000. Over FY1998-FY2013, IMLS awarded 3,747 Native American library grants totaling $56.7M to 434 distinct institutions. The story of tribal libraries runs from the boarding-school assimilation era through modern cultural sovereignty, language revitalization, and digital repatriation via platforms like Mukurtu CMS.

Association of Tribal Archives, Libraries, & Museums (ATALM)

Association of Tribal Archives, Libraries, & Museums, founded 2010 in Oklahoma City, Oklahoma. ATALM was officially founded in 2010, building upon work begun at the 2002 National Conference of Tribal Archives, Libraries, and Museums in Mesa, Arizona, and continued at subsequent conferences in 2005, 2007, 2008, and 2009.

Notable tribal libraries

LibraryLocation
Navajo Nation LibraryWindow Rock, Arizona (Navajo Nation capital)
Cherokee Nation collections (Tahlequah, Oklahoma)Tahlequah, Oklahoma
Sequoyah National Research Center (SNRC)Little Rock, Arkansas (University of Arkansas at Little Rock)
Seminole Tribe of Florida - Ah-Tah-Thi-Ki Museum and Tribal Library ProgramBig Cypress Seminole Indian Reservation, Florida
Seneca-Iroquois National Museum / Seneca NationSalamanca, New York (traditional Seneca territory, Allegheny Territory)

Tribal college libraries (TCUs)

American Indian Higher Education Consortium (AIHEC), founded 1973, supports 37 tribally controlled colleges and universities. Diné College (founded 1968) was the first TCU. Enrollment grew from ~2,100 in 1982 to ~30,000 by 2003.

Language preservation

Tribal libraries and tribal college libraries serve as language revitalization centers. The suppression and attempted erasure of many Indigenous languages is tied to settler colonial assimilation practices, including the boarding-school era. Through collection development, programming, and digital archives, tribal libraries support Indigenous language revitalization.

Key languages served: Cherokee, Navajo/Diné, Lakota, Ojibwe, Seneca (Onöndowaʼga꞉ʼ), Passamaquoddy, Karuk, Hawaiian, Inupiaq, Dakota, Assiniboine, Cree, Mohawk

Digital repatriation

Digital repatriation is the return of cultural heritage items in a digital format to the communities from which they originated. The term originated in anthropology and has expanded to museum, library, and archives collections, including digital photographs, digital collections, virtual exhibits, 3D scans, audio recordings, and intangible cultural heritage.

Mukurtu CMS: Open-source content management system specifically designed for managing and sharing digital heritage in culturally relevant and ethically minded ways. Places the needs and concerns of Indigenous communities at the forefront of its structure and function.

History of tribal libraries

EraPeriodTheme
Boarding school libraries and assimilation (1870s-1930s)The federal government and religious organizations operated boarding schools for Native American children beginning in the 1870s, with the Carlisle Indian Industrial School (founded 1879 in Carlisle, Pennsylvania) as the flagship model. These schools were designed for assimilation: to remove Native children from their families and cultures and educate them in Euro-American ways. Libraries and reading materials in these institutions served assimilationist purposes, prioritizing English-language and Christian materials over Indigenous languages and knowledge.
Self-determination and the founding of tribal libraries (1960s-1970s)The Tribal College movement developed as part of the Native American 'self-determination' movement of the 1960s. The first tribal college, Navajo Community College (now Diné College), was founded by the Navajo Nation in 1968 in Tsaile, Arizona. The 1978 White House Pre-Conference on Indian Library and Information Services On or Near Reservations (Denver, Colorado) focused on amelioration of library services to American Indians and led directly to the founding of AILA in 1979.
Federal grant infrastructure (1990s-2010s)The Tribally Controlled Colleges and Universities Assistance Act of 1978 and the Equity in Educational Land-Grant Status Act of 1994 established federal funding and land-grant status for tribal colleges. IMLS (established 1996) administers Native American Library Services grants (Basic and Enhancement) as a dedicated funding stream for tribal libraries. The cached IMLS dataset documents continuous awards from FY1998 through FY2013.
Cultural sovereignty and digital repatriation (2000s-present)Modern tribal libraries, archives, and museums increasingly assert tribal sovereignty over cultural knowledge. Mukurtu CMS (2007), Local Contexts (2010), and ATALM (2010) represent a shift toward Indigenous-controlled digital infrastructure and advocacy. ATALM's work explicitly centers 'cultural sovereignty of Native Nations' - the right of Indigenous nations to conserve and house their own historical documents, artifacts, and histories through tribal memory institutions.

Key facts

  • There is no single comprehensive national census of tribal libraries in the United States; estimates range from roughly 200 to 300+.
  • 574 federally recognized Indian tribes are eligible for IMLS Native American Library Services grants.
  • The IMLS grants dataset (FY1998-FY2013) records 3,747 Native American grant awards totaling approximately $56.7 million, reaching 434 distinct institutions.
  • IMLS Basic Grants are typically ~$7,000-$10,000 (non-competitive); Enhancement Grants are competitive, up to $200,000.
  • ATALM was officially founded in 2010, building on conferences held in 2002, 2005, 2007, 2008, and 2009. It is based in Oklahoma City, Oklahoma, and is largely funded by IMLS grants.
  • There are 37 Tribal Colleges and Universities (TCUs) in the United States that are AIHEC members; 32 were fully accredited as of 2018.
  • The first tribal college was Diné College (originally Navajo Community College), founded by the Navajo Nation in 1968 in Tsaile, Arizona, and accredited in 1979.
  • The American Indian Library Association (AILA) was established in 1979 as a result of the 1978 White House Pre-Conference on Indian Library and Information Services, and became an ALA affiliate in 1985.
  • Mukurtu CMS, an open-source platform for Indigenous communities to manage digital heritage, was originally designed in 2007 for the Warumungu people in Australia and has become a popular tool for Indigenous digital archives worldwide.
  • Local Contexts (founded 2010) offers 18 Traditional Knowledge (TK) Labels that institutions can use in digital collections to let communities designate materials as restricted for access or use.
  • The Navajo Nation Museum, Library & Visitor's Center in Window Rock, Arizona, was constructed in 1997 at a cost of $7 million and houses the Navajo Nation Library (with five special collections) alongside the museum, which holds over 40,000 photographs.
  • The Sequoyah National Research Center in Little Rock, Arkansas, is home to the American Native Press Archives (begun 1983), one of the world's largest repositories of Native American publications, and the J.W. Wiggins Native American Art Collection of over 2,400 pieces.
  • Tribal college enrollment grew from approximately 2,100 in 1982 to 30,000 in 2003, reflecting both institutional growth and a return to reservations by many American Indians.
  • Less than 10% of tribal land in Indian Country has broadband access, per FCC acknowledgment cited by ATALM, compounding the digital divide for tribal libraries.

Source: IMLS Native American Library Services grant data (FY1998-FY2013, cached locally), Wikipedia articles on tribal libraries, ATALM, AIHEC, tribal colleges, Mukurtu CMS, and the American Indian Library Association (AILA), citing primary sources. IMLS awarded 3,747 Native American grants totaling $56.7M to 434 institutions over FY1998-FY2013. The estimated 300+ tribal libraries is an advocacy/scholarly estimate; no single federal census of tribal libraries exists. Tribal colleges (TCUs) number 37 as of 2018 (AIHEC), up from 6 founding members in 1973.

Academic Libraries: The Research Powerhouse

America's ~3,700 academic libraries hold 705 million physical volumes and spend $8.2 billion annually — a scale that dwarfs public library collections. The 125-member Association of Research Libraries (ARL) represents the largest research libraries. The digital transition is nearly complete: 98.5% of serial titles are now electronic, and e-books (1471M) outnumber physical books by 2:1. Harvard Library alone holds over 20 million volumes, making it the largest academic library in the world.

The digital transition

Academic libraries have led the shift to digital resources. 98.5% of serial titles are now electronic, and e-book holdings (1471M) exceed physical book volumes. Current serials consume 78.9% of materials budgets — a figure dominated by "big deal" journal packages from major publishers.

Association of Research Libraries (ARL)

ARL's Wikipedia infobox (edited July 2026) lists 125 members. Membership includes research libraries in the U.S. AND Canada (e.g., University of Toronto, McGill, University of British Columbia), plus the Library of Congress, National Agricultural Library, National Library of Medicine, New York Public Library, Boston Public Library, and the Smithsonian Institution — so the count of U.S. academic-library members is somewhat lower than 125.

Key facts

  • There are approximately 3,700 degree-granting academic libraries in the United States, per the National Center for Education Statistics (NCES), which surveys them biennially. The IPEDS AL 2022-23 data file contains 3,741 institutions, of which 2,862 reported full expenditure data. (NCES, nces.ed.gov/surveys/libraries/academic.asp)
  • U.S. academic libraries collectively reported $8.20 billion in total expenditures in 2022-23 (IPEDS AL), of which $3.23 billion (39%) was for materials (books, serials, e-resources), $3.42 billion (42%) for salaries and wages, $814 million for fringe benefits, and $740 million for other operating costs. (IPEDS AL 2022-23 revised microdata)
  • The Association of Research Libraries (ARL), founded in 1932, has 125 member research libraries in the U.S. and Canada that collectively spend approximately $4.5 billion per year on information resources — a large share of the entire academic library marketplace. ARL Statistics, published since 1963, is the gold-standard descriptive series for research libraries. (Wikipedia/Association of Research Libraries citing arl.org)
  • Harvard Library is the largest strictly academic library in the world, holding over 20 million volumes, 400 million manuscripts, 10 million photographs, and 1 million maps. The University of California system operates the largest academic library SYSTEM in the world, managing about 41 million volumes across 100 libraries on ten campuses. (Wikipedia/Academic library)
  • By NCES ALS 2012 volumes-held counts, the largest U.S. academic libraries were Harvard (17.2M), Yale (13.5M), University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign (12.9M), UC Berkeley (11.5M), University of Michigan (11.5M), University of Chicago (11.4M), Columbia (11.3M), UT Austin (10.2M), UCLA (10.0M), and Indiana University (9.3M). (NCES ALS 2012 microdata)
  • Electronic serial titles now outnumber physical serial titles by roughly 66-to-1 (1.54 billion electronic vs 23.2 million physical in IPEDS 2022-23), and current serial subscriptions account for 78.9% of academic library materials spending — illustrating the near-complete migration of journal collections to electronic formats and the budget dominance of 'big deal' publisher packages and database licenses. (IPEDS AL 2022-23)
  • The NCES Academic Library Survey 2012 was the LAST year national reference-transaction, gate-count, and presentation data were collected. In 2012, reporting libraries recorded 29.3 million service transactions, 541,940 presentations attended by 10.2 million people, and a gate count of 22.6 million. These service metrics were dropped when the survey moved to IPEDS in 2014, so current national reference trends cannot be tracked from federal data. (NCES ALS 2012)
  • Total library staff FTE in reporting academic libraries declined from a pre-recession peak of ~27,164 (2008) to ~24,450 (2023), and professional librarian FTE fell from 35,088 (ALS 2012) to 24,488 (IPEDS 2022-23) — a ~30% drop — as budgets tightened, e-resources reduced local technical processing, and institutions shifted toward paraprofessional and student staffing. (NCES ALS / IPEDS AL)
  • Interlibrary loan activity in academic libraries roughly halved between 2012 and 2022-23 (from 20.3M to 10.0M total lending transactions), reflecting expanded e-resource licensing, document-delivery automation (ILLiad, RAPIDILL, Tipasa), and shared-print agreements — though academic libraries remain the major participants in U.S. ILL, with research universities as net lenders. (NCES ALS 2012 / IPEDS AL 2022-23)
  • Doctoral/Research Universities (289 respondents in 2022-23) account for 61.5% of all U.S. academic library expenditures ($5.04B of $8.20B) and 59% of physical volumes (434M of 705M), despite representing only ~10% of reporting libraries — a dramatic concentration of resources at research universities versus the 846 Associate's (community) college libraries which collectively spend $675M. (IPEDS AL 2022-23)
  • ARL has NOT merged into ACRL. As of 2026 ARL remains an independent 125-member organization (executive director Andrew K. Pace); ACRL is a division of the American Library Association. The two organizations are affiliated through the Library Copyright Alliance. ALA has, however, restructured its own divisions — consolidating ALCTS/LITA/LLAMA into 'Core' (2019-20) and dissolving YALSA (reunified with ALSC) on August 31, 2026. (Wikipedia/Association of Research Libraries, Wikipedia/American Library Association)
  • The mean weekly hours of operation across reporting academic libraries was 69.3 in 2012 (ALS); large research universities commonly operate 24/7 during the academic term. The 'library as place' movement since the 2000s has driven conversion of stack space into group study rooms, learning commons, makerspaces, and digital scholarship centers. (NCES ALS 2012; ACRL/SCUP literature)

Source: NCES Academic Library Survey (ALS) 2012 and IPEDS Academic Library (AL) component 2022-23, Association of Research Libraries (ARL) statistics (via Wikipedia), and Wikipedia articles on major academic libraries citing primary sources. The 3,700 figure is the NCES survey universe; IPEDS AL 2022-23 contains 3,741 institutions. Physical volumes (705M) are IPEDS "physical books" — a narrower count than the legacy ALS "total volumes held" metric. Total expenditures ($8.2B) reflect the post-2014 IPEDS accounting basis, which runs higher than pre-2014 ALS figures. ARL membership (125) includes Canadian and non-academic research libraries.

A History of American Libraries

Colonial era to present (c. 1630s-2026), covering public, private/subscription, academic, school, federal, and special libraries; key legislation, philanthropy, professionalization, civil rights, digital transformation, and 21st-century challenges.

Historical Eras

Colonial and Early Republic (c. 1630s-1830s)

The earliest libraries in the American colonies were private collections of gentlemen, clergy, and colleges. Anglican parish libraries, subsidized by the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts (founded 1701), appeared at colonial churches. Subscription/social libraries - financed by membership fees or shares - became the dominant model and were the direct forerunners of the public library. Notable examples: the Library Company of Philadelphia (1731, Benjamin Franklin), the Darby Library Company (1743, PA), the Redwood Library and Athenaeum (1747, Newport RI), the Charleston Library Society (1748), the New York Society Library (1754), and the Boston Athenaeum (1807). After the Revolution, libraries served the new government: the Library of Congress was established in 1800, and the Library Company of Philadelphia functioned virtually as the Library of Congress until the national capital moved to Washington.

19th Century: The Rise of the Public Library (1830s-1899)

The 19th century saw the transition from subscription/social libraries to tax-supported public libraries. Key models included school district libraries (1830s-1850s), mercantile and mechanics' institute libraries (1820s-1850s) for the working class, and association/social libraries. The Peterborough, NH Town Library (1833) was the first tax-supported free public library; Boston Public Library (1848 statute, 1852 established, 1854 opened) was the first large free municipal library. The American Library Association was founded in 1876, Melvil Dewey opened the first library school in 1887, and Andrew Carnegie began his library grants in 1886, launching the era of library philanthropy. The Library of Congress gained copyright deposit rights in 1870 and built its Thomas Jefferson Building (1890-1897).

Early to Mid-20th Century: Professionalization, Federal Aid, and Civil Rights (1900-1956)

The early 20th century was dominated by the Carnegie library building boom (1900-1919): Carnegie funded 1,670 public library buildings in 1,412 US communities for ~$40 million, and by 1930 half of all American public libraries had been built by Carnegie. World War I saw the ALA's Library War Service (1917) provide books to soldiers. The ALA adopted the Library Bill of Rights in 1939, defining librarianship's commitment to intellectual freedom. The Library of Congress's National Library Service for the Blind began in 1931 (Pratt-Smoot Act). World War II prompted national planning for public libraries (Joeckel committee, 1943-1948), and the ALA opened a Washington, DC office in 1945 to advocate for federal library legislation - culminating in the Library Services Act of 1956.

Mid to Late 20th Century: Civil Rights, Construction, and the Digital Revolution (1956-1999)

The civil rights movement reshaped libraries: the ALA amended the Library Bill of Rights in 1961 to bar discrimination based on race, religion, national origin, or political views, and commissioned the 1963 'Access to Public Libraries' study documenting discrimination. The Library Services and Construction Act (1964, signed by LBJ) extended federal aid to urban areas and underserved groups; the White House Conference on Library and Information Services (1979, 1991) raised library policy's national profile. The digital revolution began: the Library of Congress converted its card catalog to machine-readable form (MARC) in the late 1960s, OCLC launched online cataloging, and the Telecommunications Act of 1996 created the E-rate program (FCC Order 97-157, May 7, 1997) subsidizing internet access for libraries. In 1996 the Institute of Museum and Library Services (IMLS) was established and the Library Services and Technology Act (LSTA) replaced LSCA, emphasizing technology. The Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation's US Libraries initiative began in 1997 to connect public libraries to the internet.

21st Century: Digital Lending, Pandemic, Legal Battles, and Book Bans (2000-present)

The 21st century brought digital transformation and crises. The Children's Internet Protection Act (2000) tied E-rate funding to internet filtering. The FCC modernized E-rate in 2014,expanding Wi-Fi subsidies. The COVID-19 pandemic (2020) closed physical libraries and exploded digital lending, even as e-book legal battles intensified: four major publishers sued the Internet Archive (June 1, 2020) over controlled digital lending, and the court found for the publishers (March 25, 2023; judgment August 11, 2023). A wave of book banning began in 2021 and surged to unprecedented levels (a 63% rise in reported cases between 2022 and 2023), targeting LGBTQ+ and racial-minority perspectives, with 20,890 documented challenges (2018-2025) and 6,875 bans/removals. In 2025 the Trump administration issued an executive order virtually eliminating IMLS; the ALA and AFSCME sued and courts temporarily blocked the dismantling. Artificial intelligence began entering library operations and discovery services in the 2020s.

Milestone Timeline

Key Facts

  • The earliest American libraries were private collections of gentlemen, clergy, and colleges; Anglican parish libraries subsidized by the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel (founded 1701) appeared at colonial churches.
  • The Library Company of Philadelphia (1731, Benjamin Franklin and the Junto) was the first successful subscription library in America and hired Louis Timothee, the first librarian hired in America; it served virtually as the Library of Congress until the national capital was established in 1800.
  • The Darby Free Library (founded 1743 as the Darby Library Company by 29 Quakers) claims to be 'the oldest library in the United States in continuous service'; it was a subscription library until 1898 and celebrated its 275th anniversary in 2018.
  • The Redwood Library and Athenaeum (1747, Newport, RI) is the first purposely built library in the US and the oldest neo-Classical building in the country, in continuous use since its opening; it predates the Charleston Library Society (1748), New York Society Library (1754), and Boston Athenaeum (1807).
  • The Library of Congress was established April 24, 1800; British forces burned its original collection in August 1814; Congress bought Thomas Jefferson's 6,487 books for $23,950 in January 1815; and it gained copyright deposit rights in 1870.
  • Peterborough, New Hampshire, founded the first tax-supported free public library in the US (and the world) at town meeting on April 9, 1833; its success led New Hampshire to pass enabling legislation in 1849.
  • The Boston Public Library (statute 1848, established 1852, opened March 20, 1854) was the first large free municipal public library in the US; it is Massachusetts' Library for the Commonwealth and the third-largest public library in the US (~24 million items).
  • The American Library Association, founded October 6, 1876, in Philadelphia by 103 librarians (90 men, 13 women), is the oldest and largest library association in the world; Justin Winsor was its first president (1876-1885).
  • Melvil Dewey published the Dewey Decimal Classification in 1876 and opened the first library school in the world - the School of Library Economy at Columbia College - on January 5, 1887, with 20 students (17 women, admitted against the Regents' wishes).
  • Andrew Carnegie funded 2,509 libraries worldwide (1,689 in the US) between 1883 and 1929; $40 million built 1,670 public library buildings in 1,412 American communities, and by 1930 half of all US public libraries had been built by Carnegie.
  • Under segregation, Carnegie funded separate libraries for African Americans in the South (e.g., Houston Colored Carnegie Library; the Carnegie Library of Savannah, GA, opened 1914) rather than insisting on integration.
  • Women's clubs led the establishment of 75-80% of libraries in communities across the country during Carnegie's era, organizing local fundraising and lobbying.
  • The ALA Library War Service (1917-1920) raised $5 million, erected 36 camp libraries (with a $320,000 Carnegie Corporation grant), distributed 7-10 million books and magazines, and served 500+ locations including military hospitals in WWI; it led to library departments in the Army, Navy, Veterans' Association, and Merchant Marine, and the American Library in Paris (1918).
  • The first Library Bill of Rights was drafted by Forrest Spaulding and adopted by the ALA in 1939, defining modern librarianship's commitment to intellectual freedom; the ALA strengthened it in 1948 and amended it in 1961 to bar discrimination by race, religion, national origin, or political views.
  • The National Library Service for the Blind (Pratt-Smoot Act, began July 1, 1931) is the federal library service for blind and print-disabled Americans; it was amended to add sound recordings/talking books (1934), blind children (1952), music materials (1962), and physical impairments (1966).
  • The Library Services Act (1956) was the first major federal funding for public libraries; the Library Services and Construction Act (1964, signed by LBJ) extended aid to urban areas and construction; the Library Services and Technology Act (1996) replaced LSCA with an emphasis on technology.
  • The Institute of Museum and Library Services (IMLS), established September 30, 1996, is the largest source of federal funding for libraries, directing population-based Grants to States funding to all 50 states, DC, territories, and Freely Associated States; in FY2022 it awarded $257.2M ($168.8M via Grants to States).
  • The E-rate program, authorized by the Telecommunications Act of 1996 (section 254) and launched by FCC Order 97-157 on May 7, 1997, provides discounts of 20-90% on telecommunications and internet access for schools and libraries; the FCC modernized it in 2014 and raised the budget to $3.9 billion.
  • The Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation's US Libraries initiative (1997) connected public libraries to the internet across all 50 states with computers, software, and training; the foundation committed more than $1 billion cumulatively to library programs (US and global) before winding down Global Libraries ~2018.
  • During the COVID-19 pandemic (2020), physical library closures drove a surge in digital lending; electronic circulation (e-books/e-audiobooks) reached about 316 million, ~18.5% of total public library circulation, up from near-zero two decades earlier.
  • The book-banning wave that began in 2021 reached unprecedented levels (a 63% rise in reported cases between 2022 and 2023); documented challenges totaled 20,890 (2018-2025) with 6,875 bans/removals, primarily targeting LGBTQ+ and racial-minority perspectives; school libraries saw 18,904 of the challenges.
  • Four major publishers sued the Internet Archive on June 1, 2020, over controlled digital lending; the court ruled for the publishers on March 25, 2023, and the August 11, 2023 judgment barred the Internet Archive from digitally lending books for which electronic copies are on sale.
  • On March 14, 2025, President Trump issued an executive order virtually eliminating IMLS; the ALA and AFSCME sued and courts temporarily blocked the dismantling; IMLS reinstated all cut federal grants on December 3, 2025.
  • The most-challenged books in the 2021-present wave include 'Gender Queer: A Memoir' (190 challenges), 'All Boys Aren't Blue' (179), 'Looking for Alaska' (175), 'The Perks of Being a Wallflower' (156), and 'The Bluest Eye' (153); the most-targeted authors include Ellen Hopkins (976), Sarah J. Maas (772), Toni Morrison (270), and John Green (247).
  • Florida had the most book challenges of any state (5,798, 2018-2025), followed by Iowa (3,719), Texas (2,282), and Tennessee (2,208); Texas issued the largest number of title challenges in 2022.

Source: Wikipedia articles on American library history including the Library Company of Philadelphia, Darby Free Library, Redwood Library and Athenaeum, Boston Public Library, Andrew Carnegie, Melvil Dewey, the American Library Association, the Library of Congress, IMLS, the Library Services and Construction Act, the NLS, the Pratt-Smoot Act, and book censorship history. Compiled and cross-referenced from primary sources cited in each article.

Library Buildings & Architecture

The architecture of American libraries reflects the evolution of the nation's civic ideals, from the colonial parish libraries of the early 1700s to the striking contemporary glass-and-steel structures of the twenty-first century. The earliest American libraries were subscription or social libraries, such as Benjamin Franklin's Library Company of Philadelphia (1731), and it was not until the mid-nineteenth century that the tax-supported public library took hold, led by the Boston Public Library (founded 1848, opened 1854) and the Peterborough, New Hampshire Town Library (1833), the first free public library supported by taxation in the world. The great Beaux-Arts monuments of the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, including the Library of Congress Thomas Jefferson Building (1897) and the New York Public Library Stephen A. Schwarzman Building (1911), were built to evoke temples of knowledge, with grand staircases, marble facades, and monumental reading rooms that symbolized a person's elevation through learning. The single greatest force shaping American library buildings was the philanthropy of Andrew Carnegie, who between 1883 and 1929 funded the construction of 1,689 library buildings in the United States, spending roughly $40 million and building nearly half of all the public libraries in the country by 1930. Carnegie libraries, typically simple rectangular structures of stone or brick with rusticated foundations and low-pitched hipped roofs, introduced revolutionary open-stack layouts and standardized floor plans emphasizing efficiency. In the mid-twentieth century, Modern and Brutalist designs replaced ornate classicism, exemplified by the Minneapolis Public Library and Information Center (1961). A new wave of internationally celebrated contemporary libraries arrived in the early 2000s, led by Rem Koolhaas's Seattle Central Library (2004) and Moshe Safdie's Salt Lake City Public Library (2003). The twenty-first century has also brought a focus on sustainability, with libraries pursuing LEED certification, green roofs, and carbon-neutral operations, while new buildings increasingly serve as mixed-use community hubs combining libraries with schools, recreation centers, and charter schools.

The Library of Congress: Three Buildings

BuildingOpenedArchitectStyleDescription
Thomas Jefferson Building1897John L. Smithmeyer, Paul J. Pelz, Edward Pearce CaseyBeaux-Arts / Classical RevivalThe oldest of the Library of Congress buildings, built between 1890 and 1897 and initially known as the Library of Congress Building. Named for Thomas Jefferson in 1980, whose 1815 book purchase formed a core of the collection. Located on First Street SE between Independence Avenue and East Capitol Street, across from the U.S. Capitol. Designated a National Historic Landmark on December 21, 1965 (refnum 66000000). Known for its classicizing facade and elaborately decorated interior.
John Adams Building1939Pierson & Wilson; Alexander Buel Trowbridge (consulting); David Lynn (Architect of the Capitol)Art Deco / Stripped ClassicalThe second-oldest Library of Congress building, built in the 1930s and opened to the public on April 5, 1939. Originally called The Annex, it was renamed for John Adams in 1980. Faced in white Georgia marble, it is five stories tall with 180 miles of shelving and capacity for ten million volumes across 12 tiers of stacks. Features bronze entrance doors by sculptor Lee Lawrie depicting figures associated with the history of writing, including Sequoyah, Thoth, and Brahma. Total appropriation reached $8,226,457.
James Madison Memorial Building1980DeWitt, Poor, and Shelton Associated Architects; John George Stewart (Architect of the Capitol)Modern (1970s)The third Library of Congress building, constructed from 1971 to 1976 and dedicated on April 24, 1980, serving as the official memorial to James Madison. One of the three largest public buildings in the Washington metropolitan area (along with the Pentagon and the FBI's J. Edgar Hoover Building), with 2.1 million square feet and 1.5 million square feet of assignable space. Houses the U.S. Copyright Office, the Law Library Reading Room, the Performing Arts Reading Room, the Manuscript Reading Room, and the Mary Pickford Theater. Total construction authorization reached $130.675 million.

Notable Library Buildings

LibraryCity, StateYearArchitectStyleSq FtDescription
Stephen A. Schwarzman Building (NYPL Main Branch)New York, NY1911Carrere and HastingsBeaux-Arts375,000The flagship building of the New York Public Library, opened May 23, 1911. Built 1897-1911, with a marble facade, flanking stone lions, and shelf space for 3.5 million volumes spread across 375,000 sq ft. Collection numbered approximately 2.5 million items as of 2015. Designated a National Historic Landmark in 1965. Projected final cost was $10 million.
Harold Washington Library CenterChicago, IL1991Hammond, Beeby and Babka (Thomas H. Beeby)New Classical Postmodernism972,000The central library of the Chicago Public Library system, opened October 7, 1991. Listed in the Guinness Book of Records as the largest public library building in the world. Ten floors, a rooftop winter garden, and painted aluminum owl acroteria by Kent Bloomer and Raymond Kaskey. Beeby won the Driehaus Architecture Prize in 2013. A $175 million bond issue funded it.
Seattle Central LibrarySeattle, WA2004Rem Koolhaas and Joshua Prince-Ramus (OMA/LMN Architects)Contemporary / Deconstructivism362,987Eleven-story glass-and-steel building opened May 23, 2004. Cost $165.9 million. Features a continuous Books Spiral spanning four floors without breaking the Dewey Decimal classification. Voted #108 on the AIA's list of Americans' 150 favorite structures; received a 2005 national AIA Honor Award. Over 2.3 million people visited in its first year, generating $16 million in new economic activity.
Minneapolis Central LibraryMinneapolis, MN2006Cesar Pelli and Architectural AlliancePostmodern / Contemporary353,000Opened May 20, 2006, on Nicollet Mall. Houses a collection of more than 2.4 million items, the third largest per capita public library collection of any major U.S. city. Features an 8,140 sq ft atrium, an 18,560 sq ft green roof planted with drought-resistant ground cover, over 300 public computers, and two levels of underground parking. Voters approved $140 million in funding in 2000.
Salt Lake City Public Library (Main Branch)Salt Lake City, UT2003Moshe Safdie and Associates with VCBO ArchitectureContemporary240,000Five-story wedge-shaped building opened February 8, 2003, featuring a five-story curved glass outer wall, a 20,000 sq ft skylight, a rooftop garden with tended beehives, and a lens on the south side that warms the building in winter. Named Library Journal's Library of the Year in 2006. An $84 million bond funded the project; the building points directly at the Salt Lake Mormon Temple.
San Diego Central LibrarySan Diego, CA2013Rob QuigleyContemporary366,673Nine-story, $184.9 million library at 330 Park Boulevard, opened September 30, 2013. Houses e3 Civic High School, billed as the only U.S. school housed within a library. Includes bay view terraces, roof gardens, nearly 300 public art pieces, and the Sullivan Family Baseball Research Center, the second largest baseball memorabilia collection in the U.S.
Boston Public Library (McKim Building)Boston, MA1895McKim, Mead, and White (Charles Follen McKim)Renaissance Revival0The Boston Central Library in Copley Square, based on the Bibliotheque Ste-Genevieve in Paris. Features the vast Bates Hall reading room and murals by Pierre Puvis de Chavannes. BPL, founded in 1848, was the first large free municipal public library in the United States; collection size is 24,079,520 items with 24 branches and 7.6 million annual circulation.
Nashville Public Library (Main Branch)Nashville, TN2001Robert A.M. Stern ArchitectsContemporary Classical0Opened in 2001 on the site of the former Church Street Center mall. Named Gale/Library Journal 2017 Library of the Year and recipient of the 2010 National Medal for Museum and Library Service. Features a Civil Rights Room documenting the Civil Rights Movement in Nashville; serves 1.5 million people across Davidson County.
Tigard Public LibraryTigard, OR2004SRG PartnershipContemporary47,500Two-story brick-faced building on Hall Boulevard, opened August 2, 2004. Cost $14.3 million including land. Features a 170-seat community room, gas fireplace, and glass atrium. Collection of about 230,000 items with 2012 circulation of 1,522,367 and 37,106 registered borrowers.
Woodridge Neighborhood LibraryWashington, DC2016Wiencek & Associates and Bing Thom ArchitectsContemporary20,000A branch of the DC Public Library, opened in 2016 at a cost of $16.5 million after nearly three years of construction. Features the first roof terrace of its kind in the city, used for library and community programming including DC Punk Archive concerts.
Deanwood Neighborhood LibraryWashington, DC2010Perkins EastmanContemporary7,500A branch of the DC Public Library within a $32 million community center that opened July 2010, also includes a swimming pool, gym, and child care and seniors facilities. Replaced a 120-square-foot library kiosk that had served Deanwood since 1976.
Franklin Public Library (Ray Memorial Building)Franklin, MA1904Ray family (donors)Classical Revival0The first and oldest public lending library in continuous existence in the United States, founded 1790 with 116 books donated by Benjamin Franklin. The Ray Memorial Building was dedicated in 1904 as the first permanent building for the collection.
Darby Free LibraryDarby, PA1872Benjamin D. PriceItalianate0Founded as the Darby Library Company in 1743 by 29 Quakers, just 12 years after Franklin's Library Company of Philadelphia. Claims to be the oldest library in the United States in continuous service. The 1872 Italianate building cost $8,895.54; became free and open to the public in 1898.
Lemieux Library and McGoldrick Learning CommonsSeattle, WA2010PfeifferContemporary0A LEED Gold-certified building on the Seattle University campus, completed in 2010.

Clapp. Renovated and expanded from 22,000 to 31,058 sq ft at a cost of $14 million, designed by Finegold Alexander Architects.

America's Oldest Libraries

LibraryCity, StateFoundedDescription
Library Company of PhiladelphiaPhiladelphia, PA1731Founded by Benjamin Franklin and the Junto club in 1731 as a subscription library. Continues to exist as a nonprofit independent research library. The first successful subscription library in the American colonies.
Darby Library Company (Darby Free Library)Darby, PA1743Organized March 10, 1743 by 29 Quakers, just 12 years after Franklin's Library Company. Claims to be the oldest library in the United States in continuous service. Became free in 1898.
Redwood Library and AthenaeumNewport, RI1747The oldest continuously operating lending library in the United States, a subscription library housed in a classical building designed by Peter Harrison.
New York Society LibraryNew York, NY1754Founded with the 238 volumes left by Rev. John Sharpe in 1713 as the core; a dozen men founded the library in 1754. The oldest library in New York City.
Boston Public Library (parish library)Boston, MA1725Rev. John Checkley founded the first public library in Boston between 1711 and 1725 at the Old State House; destroyed by fire on December 9, 1747.
Franklin Public LibraryFranklin, MA1790The first public lending library in continuous existence in the United States. Founded when Benjamin Franklin donated 116 books in lieu of a requested church bell; the town voted to lend the books free of charge.
Peterborough Town LibraryPeterborough, NH1833The first free public library supported by taxation in the world, founded at town meeting on April 9, 1833.
Boston Public LibraryBoston, MA1848Founded in 1848 and opened March 20, 1854. The first large free municipal public library in the United States and the second tax-supported public library.
Brumback LibraryVan Wert, OH1890Claims to be the first county library in the United States.
Robbins Library (children's library)Arlington, MA1835The first free continuous children's library in the United States, funded privately.
San Diego Public LibrarySan Diego, CA1882Established May 19, 1882; the first city west of the Mississippi River to receive a Carnegie Library grant (1899).
Nashville Public LibraryNashville, TN1901The Howard Library became Nashville's first free circulating library in 1901; Carnegie funded the Carnegie Library Building completed in 1904.

Includes a 108 kW photovoltaic system purchased through a $300,000 matching grant and offsets more carbon than it emits through renewable energy and carbon credits, offsetting an additional 167,620 lbs of CO2 from other community buildings.

Anythink Bennett (Rangeview Library District)Bennett, CONot certified2009Highly efficient and sustainable though explicitly not LEED certified; uses beetle-killed pine, Solatubes for natural light, ground-source heating and cooling, recycled materials, and a 10 kW photovoltaic system.

Architectural Timeline

YearEvent
1731Benjamin Franklin and the Junto club founded the Library Company of Philadelphia, the first successful subscription library in the American colonies.
1743The Darby Library Company was organized in Pennsylvania by 29 Quakers, becoming one of the oldest libraries in the United States.
1800President John Adams signed the law creating the Library of Congress.
1815The purchase of Thomas Jefferson's book collection formed a core foundation for the Library of Congress.
1833The Peterborough, New Hampshire Town Library was founded at town meeting on April 9, becoming the first free public library supported by taxation in the world.
1848The Boston Public Library was founded, becoming the first large free municipal public library in the United States; it opened in 1854.
1883The first Carnegie library opened in Dunfermline, Scotland; the first U.S. Carnegie library was commissioned in 1886 in Allegheny, Pennsylvania.
1889The Carnegie Free Library of Braddock opened in Pennsylvania, the first Carnegie library to open in the United States; later designated a National Historic Landmark in 2012.
1890The Minneapolis Public Library was founded; its first central library was built in 1889.
1897The Library of Congress Thomas Jefferson Building opened, built 1890-1897 in the Beaux-Arts style, designed by Smithmeyer, Pelz, and Casey.
1901Andrew Carnegie offered $100,000 for a Nashville public library; the Carnegie Library Building was completed in 1904.
1911The New York Public Library Stephen A. Schwarzman Building opened on May 23, the largest marble structure in the U.S. at the time, with shelf space for 3.5 million volumes across 375,000 sq ft.
1939The Library of Congress John Adams Building opened to the public on April 5, featuring Art Deco design, 180 miles of shelving, and capacity for ten million volumes.
1965The National Historic Preservation Act established the National Register of Historic Places; the Thomas Jefferson Building and NYPL Main Branch were designated National Historic Landmarks on December 21.
1976The Library of Congress James Madison Memorial Building superstructure was completed; it was dedicated on April 24, 1980, serving as the official memorial to James Madison.
1991The Harold Washington Library Center opened in Chicago on October 7, the largest public library building in the world per Guinness World Records at 972,000 sq ft.
1993The U.S. Green Building Council was founded, laying the groundwork for the LEED green building certification program.
2003The Salt Lake City Public Library main branch, designed by Moshe Safdie, opened on February 8; later named Library Journal's Library of the Year in 2006.
2004The Seattle Central Library, designed by Rem Koolhaas and Joshua Prince-Ramus of OMA/LMN, opened on May 23; it received a 2005 AIA Honor Award.
2006The Minneapolis Central Library, designed by Cesar Pelli, opened on May 20 with a green roof and over 300 public computers.
2009Anythink Brighton in Colorado opened, considered the first carbon-negative library in the United States, and LEED Gold certified.
2013The San Diego Central Library, designed by Rob Quigley, opened on September 30, housing e3 Civic High School, the only U.S. school within a library.

Key Facts

  • Andrew Carnegie funded the construction of 1,689 library buildings in the United States between 1883 and 1929, spending roughly $40 million and building nearly half of all American public libraries by 1930.
  • The Library of Congress comprises three buildings named for U.S. presidents: the Thomas Jefferson Building (1897), the John Adams Building (1939), and the James Madison Memorial Building (1976/1980).
  • The Thomas Jefferson Building was designated a National Historic Landmark on December 21, 1965, for its prominent monumental Beaux-Arts structure and historical significance.
  • The Harold Washington Library Center in Chicago (1991) holds the Guinness World Record as the largest public library building in the world, approximately 972,000 square feet across ten floors.
  • The Peterborough, New Hampshire Town Library, founded April 9, 1833, was the first free public library supported by taxation in the world.
  • The Boston Public Library, founded in 1848, was the first large free municipal public library in the United States; its McKim Building (1895) features the vast Bates Hall reading room and a collection of over 24 million items.
  • The New York Public Library Main Branch (Schwarzman Building) opened in 1911 with shelf space for 3.5 million volumes spread across 375,000 square feet; its collection numbered about 2.5 million items as of 2015.
  • The Seattle Central Library (2004) by Rem Koolhaas features a continuous Books Spiral spanning four floors without breaking Dewey Decimal classification; it received a 2005 AIA Honor Award and was ranked #108 on the AIA's list of Americans' 150 favorite structures.
  • The James Madison Memorial Building contains 2.1 million square feet with 1.5 million square feet of assignable space, making it one of the three largest public buildings in the Washington metropolitan area.
  • The Carnegie Free Library of Braddock (1889) was the first Carnegie library to open in the United States and was designated a National Historic Landmark in March 2012.
  • Carnegie required recipient towns to provide a building site, pay staff, maintain the library, draw from public funds, provide free service to all, and annually provide ten percent of the building's construction cost for operation.
  • The National Register of Historic Places was established by the National Historic Preservation Act in 1966 and now includes more than 100,000 individually listed properties; as of 1999, 982 properties had been removed from the Register, most often due to being destroyed.
  • A 1992 survey by George Bobinski found that 1,554 of the 1,681 original Carnegie library buildings in the United States still existed, with 911 still used as libraries, 276 unchanged, 286 expanded, 175 remodeled, and 243 demolished.
  • Anythink Brighton in Colorado (2009) is considered the first carbon-negative library in the United States, producing more renewable energy than it uses and offsetting an additional 167,620 lbs of CO2 from other community buildings.
  • The San Diego Central Library (2013) houses e3 Civic High School, billed as the only school in the United States to be housed within a library, serving grades 9 through 12.
  • The John Adams Building features bronze entrance doors by sculptor Lee Lawrie depicting deities and heroes associated with the history of writing, including Sequoyah, the only historical person represented.
  • Carnegie libraries were built in a variety of styles including Beaux-Arts, Italian Renaissance, Baroque, Classical Revival, and Spanish Colonial, with each style chosen by the community.
  • The Salt Lake City Public Library (2003) by Moshe Safdie features a five-story curved glass wall, a 20,000 sq ft skylight, and a rooftop garden with tended beehives; it was named Library Journal's Library of the Year in 2006.

Source: Wikipedia articles on library buildings, Carnegie libraries, the Library of Congress buildings, NRHP listings, LEED certification data, and Library Journal coverage of library architecture. Carnegie data from the Carnegie Corporation archives. NRHP data from the National Register of Historic Places.

Library Economics: Funding, ROI & Economic Impact

The United States public library system represents a massive public investment with significant economic returns. As of FY2022, US public libraries reported total operating expenditures of $14,147,646,938 and total revenue of $15,709,000,375, serving a population of over 334,210,711 through approximately 9,249 library systems. Funding is overwhelmingly local: municipal and county governments provide the vast majority of library revenue, with local sources contributing 86.03% of total income, followed by state government at 7.25%, other sources at 5.86%, and federal government at just 0.86%. The economic value of libraries extends far beyond their operating costs. Multiple state-level return on investment (ROI) studies have consistently demonstrated that public libraries return between $3 and $6.50 for every $1 invested, making them one of the highest-return public investments. Libraries support local economies through direct employment, workforce development, small business resources, technology access, and educational services. Despite this proven value, public awareness of library funding challenges remains low, and many systems face chronic underfunding. Voter support for libraries remains strong: 116 of 168 ballot measures passed (a 69.0% pass rate), seeking over $718,569,078 in total funding. The federal Institute of Museum and Library Services (IMLS) provides supplementary funding through 13,216 grants totaling $954,340,048, though this represents less than 1% of total library revenue. The combination of local funding dominance, proven ROI, and strong voter support underscores the deeply embedded community value of public libraries in American civic life.

Economic Impact Findings

  • Public libraries consistently provide returns on taxpayer investment far higher than other municipal spending, as documented across multiple state-level cost-benefit studies.
  • State ROI studies show returns ranging from $3.00 (New York, 2008) to $6.54 (Florida, 2004) for every dollar invested in public libraries.
  • Libraries generate economic value through direct employment of over 143,324 staff, workforce development programs, small business support, and technology access services.
  • The ALA reports that libraries are critical infrastructure for digital equity, providing free internet access to over 100,854,933 users annually.
  • Libraries serve as economic anchors in downtown areas and commercial districts, driving foot traffic and supporting local businesses.
  • Despite proven ROI, 84% of the public considers maintaining free library services a top priority (Public Agenda, 2006), yet awareness of funding challenges remains low.

Economic Timeline

YearEvent
1833Peterborough, New Hampshire establishes the first tax-supported public library in the United States.
1848Boston Public Library founded by city ordinance, becoming the first large free municipal library in the US.
1850UK Public Libraries Act passes, allowing towns to establish rate-supported public libraries, a model for US library legislation.
1854Boston Public Library opens to the public, becoming the first municipally funded library to lend books freely.
1883Andrew Carnegie begins funding library construction in the US; by 1929 he funded 1,689 libraries nationwide.
1956US Congress passes the Library Services Act, providing federal funding for public library services in rural areas.
1964Library Services and Construction Act (LSCA) expands federal library funding to include construction and urban services.
1996Institute of Museum and Library Services (IMLS) established by Museum and Library Services Act, becoming primary federal funder.
1997E-Rate program created under the Telecommunications Act, providing discounts on internet access for libraries and schools.
1998Library Services and Technology Act (LSTA) replaces LSCA, shifting focus to technology and information access.
2004ALA reports $162 million in library funding cuts across 41 states over four years.
2012Voters in 13 US states approve new library funding measures in ballot initiatives.

Key Facts

  • US public libraries had total operating expenditures of $14,147,646,938 in FY2022 (IMLS Public Libraries Survey).
  • Total public library revenue nationwide was $15,709,000,375 in FY2022.
  • Local government funding accounts for 86.03% of public library revenue, making it the dominant funding source.
  • State government provides 7.25% and federal government provides 0.86% of public library revenue.
  • The national average per-capita operating expenditure for public libraries is $42.33 (FY2022).
  • District of Columbia has the highest per-capita library spending at $121.10, while Mississippi has among the lowest at $16.48 (FY2024).
  • The median annual salary for librarians across US states is $61,540 (BLS Occupational Employment Statistics).
  • Washington state has the highest median librarian salary at $94,400; Idaho has the lowest at $46,570 (BLS).
  • Total US public librarian employment is approximately 49,741 FTE positions (IMLS FY2024).
  • Voters approved 116 of 168 library ballot measures, a 69.0% pass rate.
  • Library ballot measures nationwide sought over $718,569,078 in total funding.
  • IMLS awarded 13,216 grants totaling $954,340,048 to libraries and museums.
  • IMLS American Rescue Plan Act grants provided $77,736,457 across 96 awards to state library agencies in FY2021.
  • Andrew Carnegie funded 1,689 public libraries in the US between 1883 and 1929, accounting for nearly half of all US public libraries by 1930.
  • A 2013 Pew Research survey found 90% of Americans ages 16+ said closing their local library would affect their community.
  • A 2006 Public Agenda survey reported 84% of the public said maintaining free library services should be a top priority.
  • Cost-benefit studies consistently show libraries provide returns on taxpayer dollars far higher than other municipal spending.
  • Women librarians had a median annual salary of $48,589 compared to $52,528 for men as of 2014, reflecting a persistent wage gap.
  • US public libraries served a population of 343,094,915 with 9,249 systems in FY2024.
  • Total public library circulation nationwide reached 1,705,466,066 items in FY2024.

Source: IMLS Public Libraries Survey (PLS) FY2022 expenditure data, BLS Occupational Employment Statistics (librarian salaries by state), Library Research Service (Colorado) ROI studies, ALA economic impact reports, and state-level library ballot measure databases. ROI ratios vary by study methodology and should be compared with caution.

Library Law, Legislation & Censorship

American libraries operate within a complex legal framework spanning federal legislation, state mandates, local governance, and constitutional protections. Federal laws like the Library Services and Construction Act (1964), the E-Rate program (1996), and the IMLS Act (1996) have shaped the national library landscape. States vary widely in their requirements for school librarians, privacy protections for patron records, and responses to book challenges. The COVID-19 era and post-2020 political climate have seen a dramatic rise in book banning attempts, with PEN America documenting thousands of book bans across dozens of states. The USA PATRIOT Act (2001) created ongoing tension between national security and library patron privacy, with many states enacting reader privacy laws in response.

Key Facts

  • The ALA Library Bill of Rights (1939) is the foundational statement of library principles in the US, affirming that libraries should provide materials representing all points of view
  • All 50 US states have some form of library record confidentiality law protecting patron borrowing records from warrantless disclosure
  • The USA PATRIOT Act (2001) allows FBI to obtain library records via National Security Letters, which come with gag orders - prompting many libraries to post warnings
  • PEN America documented over 10,000 book bans in US public schools during the 2021-2023 period, affecting 1,500+ unique titles
  • Florida, Texas, and Missouri led the nation in book ban attempts in 2022-2023
  • The Supreme Court case Board of Education v. Pico (1982) established that school officials cannot remove books from libraries simply because they disagree with the ideas they contain
  • The Prison Libraries Act (2024), introduced by Rep. Emanuel Cleaver, would be the first federal legislation mandating library services in federal prisons
  • Bounds v. Smith (1977) required states to provide law libraries to prisoners, but Lewis v. Casey (1996) narrowed this requirement
  • The Children's Internet Protection Act (CIPA, 2000) requires libraries receiving E-Rate discounts to implement internet filtering, creating tension between funding and free access
  • Library ballot measures have a high pass rate nationally, typically exceeding 70%, reflecting strong community support for library funding
  • The Library Services Act (1956) was the first federal legislation specifically authorizing federal aid to public libraries
  • ALA's Standards for Library Services to the Incarcerated and Detained (2022) provide professional guidelines but are not legally binding

Source: PEN America book ban data, ALA Library Bill of Rights, censorship tracking from book_censorship.csv (9MB raw data), state legislation databases, court case records (Bounds v. Smith, Lewis v. Casey, Board of Ed v. Pico), ALA prison library standards, GovTrack legislation data, and library ballot measure databases.

School Libraries: The Foundation of Literacy

School libraries (also called school library media centers) are libraries located within schools that serve the school's curriculum and provide print and digital resources to students, staff, and sometimes parents. As of the most recent National Center for Education Statistics Schools and Staffing Survey (2011-12), 81,200 of roughly 90,000 U.S. public schools (about 90%) reported having a library media center, staffed by an estimated 88,520 paid professional library personnel plus 55,010 aides and 273,260 volunteers. However, only about two-thirds of these libraries (66.4%) had at least one full-time, state-certified library media specialist, and roughly one in five (20.9%) had no certified specialist at all. Since the SASS was last fielded for school libraries in 2011-12, no comparable federal survey has replaced it; the NCES National Teacher and Principal Survey (NTPS) did not include a school library media center component. The SLIDE (School Librarian Investigation: Decline or Evolution?) project, funded by the Institute of Museum and Library Services and led by Debra Kachel and Keith Curry Lance, documented an almost 20% national decline in full-time-equivalent school librarians between 2010 and 2019, and found that three out of ten districts had no librarians in any of their schools. The decline is sharply inequitable: in 2020-21, 3 million students in majority-nonwhite districts had no librarians, accounting for 54% of the 5.6 million students in all districts lacking any librarians. Districts with more students in poverty, more minority students, and more English language learners were the least likely to employ librarians. A large body of research spanning more than 20 states and three decades consistently links well-staffed, well-funded school libraries to higher student achievement. Beginning with Keith Curry Lance's 1993 Colorado study of 221 schools, statewide impact studies have repeatedly found that students with access to a strong school library media program and a qualified school librarian score higher on standardized reading assessments, regardless of socioeconomic status. Today there is no national qualification standard for school librarians; each state sets its own certification requirements, contributing to wide variation in staffing levels across the country.

LMC staff size, collection variety, and the specialist's instructional role were key intervening factors.Students at schools with better-funded LMCs scored higher on reading tests; relationship held after controlling for school/community socioeconomics.

Link not explained by per-pupil expenditures, teacher-pupil ratio, poverty, or adult education.Positive correlation between library staffing and PSSA reading scores, robust to school and community controls.
