History Timeline

YearEvent
1876American Library Association (ALA) founded by librarians led by Melvil Dewey to promote libraries and library education; school libraries at this time were small collections with librarians in mostly clerical roles.
1920Publication of the Certain Report, the first broad evaluation of school libraries, by library and education communities.
1945By the mid-1940s, 40% of schools had classroom collections and about 18% of public schools had centralized libraries (cities 48%, rural 12%).
1958Passage of the National Defense Education Act spurred federal funding and development for U.S. school libraries through the 1960s.
1960The Knapp School Libraries Project established model school library media centers across the country; hundreds of school libraries were expanded and renovated.
1965Elementary and Secondary Education Act (ESEA) provided federal funding that further supported school library development.
1969AASL (American Association of School Librarians) established as a division of ALA to represent school librarianship.
1988ALA and AECT published 'Information Power: Guidelines for School Library Media Programs,' a defining document for the profession.
1992Keith Curry Lance published the first Colorado school library impact study, launching the statewide 'school library impact studies' research line.
1998'Information Power II' (Information Power: Building Partnerships for Learning) updated national guidelines for school library media programs.
1999IFLA published the UNESCO School Library Manifesto, affirming the school library's role in lifelong learning and responsible citizenship.
2000Lance and colleagues published Alaska and Pennsylvania impact studies, extending the research to additional states.
2002Laura Bush hosted the White House Conference on School Libraries (June 4, 2002) to discuss research on libraries, student achievement, and successful programs.
2012Lance and Schwarz published 'How Pennsylvania School Libraries Pay Off,' examining all 500 PA districts with 2011 PSSA data.
2018Lance and Kachel published 'Why School Librarians Matter: What Years of Research Tell Us,' reviewing the cumulative impact-study findings.
2021SLIDE (The School Librarian Investigation: Decline or Evolution?) project, led by Debra Kachel and Keith Curry Lance and funded by IMLS, documented an almost 20% national decline in school librarians (2010-2019).
2022SLIDE COVID-19 special report analyzed librarian employment across ~13,000 districts (2018-19 to 2020-21), finding pandemic-related losses and widening inequities.
2023SLIDE 'School Librarian Equity Gap' report found 3 million students in majority-nonwhite districts had no librarians (54% of the 5.6 million students in all no-librarian districts).

Key Facts

  • As of 2011-12 (the most recent NCES Schools and Staffing Survey of school libraries), 81,200 of 90,000 public schools (90.2%) reported having a library media center.
  • In 2011-12, only 66.4% of school library media centers had at least one full-time, state-certified library media specialist; 20.9% had no full-time or part-time certified specialist at all.
  • There were 88,520 paid professional library media center staff in U.S. public schools in 2011-12, of whom 82.9% were state-certified library media specialists; 51.8% held a master's degree in a library-related major.
  • School libraries employed 55,010 paid library aides or clerical staff and were supported by 273,260 regularly scheduled volunteers (adult and student) in 2011-12.
  • The average public school library media center held 12,780 book titles and 470 audio/video items at the end of the 2010-11 school year, acquiring 520 new book titles annually.
  • Average expenditure on all information resources per school library was $9,340 in 2010-11 ($6,010 on books alone, $490 on audio/video).
  • Books per 100 students rose from 1,803 in 1999-2000 to 2,188 in 2011-12, while certified library/media specialists per library averaged 0.88 in 2011-12.
  • Automated circulation systems reached 90.3% of elementary and 92.6% of secondary library media centers by 2011-12, up from 74.4% in 1999-2000; 96.7% had internet connections by 2007-08.
  • Elementary/primary schools were most likely to have libraries (94.2% in 2011-12), followed by middle schools (95.7%); high schools were least likely at 83.7%.
  • The SLIDE project documented an almost 20% national decline in full-time-equivalent school librarians between 2010 and 2019, with three out of ten districts having no librarians in any of their schools.
  • Access to school librarians is strongly inequitable: in 2020-21, 3 million students in majority-nonwhite districts had no librarians, accounting for 54% of the 5.6 million students in all districts lacking any librarians.
  • Districts with more students in poverty, more minority students, and more English language learners were less likely to have librarians than districts with fewer such students.
  • Since 1992, more than 20 statewide 'school library impact studies' led by Keith Curry Lance and colleagues have consistently shown positive correlations between school librarians/library programs and student achievement.
  • The first Colorado study (1993, based on 1988-89 data from 221 schools) found that better-funded library media centers predicted higher reading scores regardless of community wealth or adult education.
  • There is no national qualification standard for school librarians in the United States; each state sets its own certification requirements, leading to wide variation in staffing.
  • School libraries are distinct from public libraries because they serve the school's curriculum and provide school-related materials, though they contain books, films, recorded sound, periodicals, realia, and digital media.
  • The Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA, 2015) for the first time explicitly recognized school libraries and librarians as allowable uses of federal education funding.

Source: NCES Digest of Education Statistics (Tables 23.216, 23.701), NCES Schools and Staffing Survey (SASS) 2003-04, 2007-08, 2011-12, AASL standards and research, Scholastic School Library Impact studies, Keith Curry Lance research (Colorado, Alaska, Pennsylvania, Oregon, New Mexico), and School Library Journal staffing data. Decline figures based on NCES SASS longitudinal data.

International Federation of Library Associations (IFLA)

International Federation of Library Associations and Institutions

Founded: 1927 — 30 September 1927, Edinburgh, Scotland — library associations from 14 European countries and the United States signed a resolution at the 50th anniversary of the UK Library Association. First president: Isak Collijn (National Library of Sweden).

Headquarters: Koninklijke Bibliotheek (National Library of the Netherlands), The Hague

Type: International non-governmental, not-for-profit organization

Members: over 1,700 members in approximately 155 countries

UNESCO Public Library Manifesto

UNESCO Public Library Manifesto

First adopted: 1949 · Revised: July 2022 (IFLA-UNESCO Public Library Manifesto 2022)

The public library shall in principle be free of charge. The public library is the local gateway to knowledge, a prerequisite for lifelong learning, independent decision-making and cultural development of the individual and social groups.

Key missions (12): Creating/strengthening reading habits in children; supporting individual and formal education; providing personal creative development; stimulating imagination of children and youth; promoting awareness of heritage, arts, and science; providing access to cultural expressions; fostering intercultural dialogue; supporting oral tradition; ensuring access to community information; providing information services to enterprises and groups; facilitating information and computer literacy; supporting literary activities for all ages.

World Digital Library (WDL)

World Digital Library (WDL)

Launched: April 21, 2009

Created by: UNESCO, United States Library of Congress

Promote international and intercultural understanding; expand non-English and non-Western content on the Internet; provide free, multilingual access to significant primary materials (manuscripts, maps, rare books, musical scores, recordings, films, prints, photographs, architectural drawings) from cultures around the world.

Reading Culture: Library Borrowing Worldwide

CountryKey FactSource
FinlandHighest number of registered book borrowers per capita in the world; over half the population are registered borrowers.Wikipedia: Library (citing IFLA/Statistics Finland)
United StatesHistorically ~15 books borrowed per user per year (1856-1978); circulation per user declined ~50% from 1978-2004 as audiovisual circulation grew.Wikipedia: Library

Key Facts

  • IFLA estimates there are approximately 350,000+ libraries worldwide, spanning public, academic, school, special, and national libraries. (IFLA (International Federation of Library Associations and Institutions), Global Vision / advocacy materials)
  • The British Library (UK) is the world's largest library by collection, with 200 million+ items, founded in 1973 from collections originating in the 1753 British Museum library. (Wikipedia: List of the world's largest libraries + British Library article)
  • The Library of Congress (US) is the second-largest library in the world (181.1 million+ items in FY2024) and the largest national library by budget (USD 897.7M FY2024 total budget authority). (Library of Congress FY2024 Annual Report)
  • IFLA was founded on 30 September 1927 in Edinburgh, Scotland, by library associations from 14 European countries and the United States. It now has 1,700+ members in ~155 countries and is headquartered at the National Library of the Netherlands in The Hague. (Wikipedia: International Federation of Library Associations and Institutions)
  • The UNESCO Public Library Manifesto, first adopted in 1949 and revised in 1994 and 2022, is the primary international statement defining public library principles, declaring that the public library 'shall in principle be free of charge.' (Wikipedia: UNESCO Public Library Manifesto)
  • The World Digital Library, a UNESCO/US Library of Congress project, launched on April 21, 2009 at UNESCO Paris with 1,236 items; it grew to 19,000+ items from nearly 200 countries dating to 8,000 BCE. Its charter concluded in 2020 and the collection is now maintained by the Library of Congress. (Wikipedia: World Digital Library)
  • The US spends USD 47.12 per capita on public library operating expenditures (IMLS FY2024, national weighted), totaling USD 16.17 billion across 9,249 public library systems serving 343.1 million people. (IMLS Public Libraries Survey FY2024)
  • Finland has the highest number of registered book borrowers per capita in the world — over half its population are registered library borrowers — and is among the highest per-capita public library funders globally. (Wikipedia: Library (citing IFLA/Statistics Finland))
  • India reports the highest weekly reading time per person (~10.7 hours) in the World Culture Score Index, followed by Thailand (9.4) and China (8.0); the US averages ~5.0 hours per week. (World Culture Score Index (NOPAD))
  • The Bibliothèque nationale de France is one of the oldest national libraries, originating as a royal library in 1461 and renamed Bibliothèque Nationale in 1792; its legal deposit requirement dates to 1537, the world's oldest continuous legal deposit. (Wikipedia: National library + Bibliothèque nationale de France)
  • The Zaluski Library, opened 8 August 1747 in Warsaw, was one of the first national libraries and largest public libraries of 18th-century Europe; granted legal deposit in 1780, its confiscated contents formed the basis of the National Library of Russia in 1794. (Wikipedia: National library)
  • Carnegie funded 2,509 libraries worldwide between 1883 and 1929: 1,689 in the United States, 660 in Britain and Ireland, 125 in Canada, and others in Australia, New Zealand and elsewhere. By 1930, half of all US public libraries had been built by Carnegie. (Wikipedia: Public libraries in North America)

Source: IFLA Global Vision / Library Map of the World, Wikipedia articles on national libraries and the world's largest libraries (citing each library's annual reports), Library of Congress FY2024 Annual Report, UNESCO Public Library Manifesto 2022, World Digital Library, Statistics Finland, and IMLS Public Libraries Survey FY2024. Per-capita figures compiled from respective national statistical agencies.

Library Consortia: Collaboration at Scale

A library consortium is any cooperative association of libraries that coordinates resources and activities on behalf of its members, whether they are academic, public, school, or special libraries and information centers. The bedrock principle upon which consortia operate is that "libraries can accomplish more together than alone." Consortia amplify member capabilities through collective action, including print and electronic resource sharing, cost reduction through group purchasing of resources, shared technology infrastructure, professional development, and shared print programs. North America, principally the United States and Canada, accounts for nearly 60% of the consortia registered with the International Coalition of Library Consortia (ICOLC), an informal, self-organized group that currently counts 243 participating consortia worldwide. The United States consortium landscape took shape over more than a century. Documented library cooperation dates to 1867, while the earliest formally established consortium, the Triangle Research Libraries Network (TRLN), appeared in 1933, and the 1960s and 1970s are considered the "heyday of consortia development." The founding of OCLC in 1967 for shared cataloging, the spread of statewide and regional online-catalog consortia in the 1970s through the 1990s, and the rise of collaborative electronic-resource licensing in the mid-1990s shaped today's system. Major players now include OCLC (nearly 30,000 member libraries in 100+ countries and annual revenue of about $217.8 million), HathiTrust (more than 18 million digitized volumes), the Association of Research Libraries (125 members spending approximately $4.5 billion every year on information resources), and the Center for Research Libraries (250 member libraries). State and regional consortia extend collaboration to the local level. OhioLINK serves 88 institutions and 117 libraries serving more than 800,000 students, faculty, and staff; TexShare coordinates hundreds of Texas libraries; and VIVA unites 74 Virginia academic libraries. Beyond licensing and resource sharing, consortia increasingly develop shared technology (Lyrasis hosts open-source platforms such as DSpace, Fedora, ArchivesSpace, and VIVO), set technical standards (NISO), build high-speed research networks (Internet2's 100 Gbit/s backbone connecting over 60,000 US institutions including public libraries and museums), and aggregate digital heritage (the Digital Public Library of America's 53M+ items from 46 partner hubs across 40 states and 8,700+ contributing institutions).

Major Library Consortia

ConsortiumFoundedHQMembersKey ServiceDescription
OCLC1967Dublin, Ohio30,000WorldCat shared cataloging (largest online public access catalog in the world)US-based nonprofit cooperative organization (originally the Ohio College Library Center) that provides shared technology services, original research, and community programs for its membership. It funds itself mainly through fees libraries pay for services (about $217.8 million annually as of 2021) and maintains the Dewey Decimal Classification system. The first online cataloging by any library worldwide occurred through OCLC on August 26, 1971, at Ohio University.
HathiTrust2008Ann Arbor, Michigan (administered by the University of Michigan)219Shared digital repository with full-text search across the entire corpusLarge-scale collaborative repository of digital content from research libraries, founded in October 2008 by the twelve universities of the Committee on Institutional Cooperation and the eleven libraries of the University of California. As of September 2024 it held more than 18 million volumes, including 6.7 million in the US public domain. Its Shared Print Program commits participating libraries to retaining almost 18 million monograph volumes for 25 years.
Center for Research Libraries1949Chicago, Illinois250Shared storage and preservation of last-copy print, microform, and digital research collectionsConsortium of North American universities, colleges, and independent research libraries founded in 1949 as the Midwest Inter-Library Center (MILC) by ten midwestern universities. It acquires and preserves traditional and digital resources for research and teaching, including foreign newspapers, government documents, microform archives, and foreign dissertations, and makes them available through interlibrary loan. Since 2013 it has hosted NERL, which negotiates licenses for 28 member academic research libraries and 80 affiliates.
Association of Research Libraries1932Washington, D.C.125Research library advocacy, statistics, and scholarly communications policyNonprofit membership organization of research libraries and archives in major public and private universities, federal government agencies, and large public institutions in Canada and the US. It held its first meeting on December 29, 1932 with 42 major university and research libraries. ARL member libraries spend approximately $4.5 billion every year on information resources. ARL co-founded the Coalition for Networked Information (CNI) with EDUCAUSE in 1990.
OhioLINK1992Columbus, Ohio88Statewide electronic catalog and shared electronic resource access for Ohio higher educationThe Ohio Library and Information Network, a consortium of Ohio's college and university libraries and the State Library of Ohio. It serves more than 800,000 students, faculty, and staff at 88 institutions with 117 libraries, including 16 public universities, 23 community/technical colleges, 48 private colleges, and the State Library of Ohio. It grew out of a 1987 recommendation by the Ohio Board of Regents and selected its software in 1990; six universities installed systems in 1992.
TexShare1994Austin, Texas (administered by the Texas State Library and Archives Commission)53Statewide database licensing, TexShare Card reciprocal borrowing, and interlibrary loanStatewide resource-sharing consortium of hundreds of member libraries in Texas, administered by the Texas State Library and Archives Commission (TSLAC). It was first proposed by Texas academic library directors in 1988 and established with 53 publicly supported four-year academic and medical libraries, with funding beginning in fiscal year 1994. Legislative statutes later expanded membership to independent academic institutions, community colleges, public libraries (1997-1999), clinical medicine libraries (2001), and K-12 public school purchasing (2005).
VIVA1994Virginia74Collaborative licensing and procurement of electronic resources for Virginia academic librariesThe Virtual Library of Virginia, a consortium of nonprofit academic libraries within the Commonwealth of Virginia, established in July 1994. Members include all 39 state-assisted colleges and universities (6 doctoral, 9 four-year, and 24 community/two-year institutions), 34 independent (private, nonprofit) institutions, and the Library of Virginia. In fiscal year 2012, state General Assembly funding accounted for 44% of VIVA's budget, down from 77% in fiscal year 2006.
Amigos Library Services1974Dallas, Texas22OCLC access, continuing education, and group purchasing for southwestern and midwestern librariesMembership-based American not-for-profit organization formed in 1974 as the AMIGOS Bibliographic Council by 22 libraries to bring OCLC access to the southwestern United States. In July 2012 it merged with the Missouri Library Network Corporation (MLNC), becoming the largest library network west of the Mississippi River and serving libraries and cultural heritage institutions across 22 states, with an office in the St. Louis area.
Lyrasis2009Alpharetta, Georgia1,000Open-source software hosting, content and scholarly communications services, and consultingNonprofit 501(c)(3) membership organization serving libraries, archives, museums, and cultural heritage and research organizations globally, formed in April 2009 from the merger of SOLINET and PALINET (whose history dated to 1933). NELINET merged in late 2009 and the Bibliographical Center for Research in January 2011. As of 2025 it had more than 1,000 institutional and group members and about 100 staff, with revenue of about $139 million. It serves as the organizational home for open-source projects including CollectionSpace, ArchivesSpace, Fedora, DSpace, and VIVO.
Internet21996Ann Arbor, Michigan (offices in Washington, D.C. and Denver, Colorado)500High-speed research and education network, InCommon federated identity, and Net+ cloud servicesNot-for-profit US computer networking consortium led by members from the research and education communities, industry, and government. Originally established by 34 university researchers in 1996 and formally organized as the University Corporation for Advanced Internet Development (UCAID) in 1997, it operates a 100 Gbit/s network backbone serving more than 210 US educational institutions, 70 corporations, and 45 non-profit and government agencies, and connects over 60,000 US educational, research, government, and community anchor institutions including public libraries and museums.
NISO1939Baltimore, Maryland221Development of ANSI/NISO Z39 technical standards for libraries and publishingThe National Information Standards Organization, a US non-profit standards organization that develops, maintains, and publishes technical standards related to publishing, bibliographic, and library applications. Founded in 1939 as the Z39 Committee, incorporated as a not-for-profit education association in 1983, and renamed NISO in 1984, it is accredited by ANSI and represents US interests to ISO Technical Committee 46. It merged with the National Federation of Advanced Information Services (NFAIS) in July 2019. Standards include Z39.50 (database access), Z39.85 (Dublin Core), and Z39.96 (JATS).
DPLA2013Boston, Massachusetts46National aggregation of digital cultural heritage items from service and content hubsThe Digital Public Library of America, a US nonprofit (501(c)(3)) discovery tool and union catalog for public domain and openly licensed content held by US archives, libraries, and museums. It launched on April 18, 2013 after two-and-a-half years of development begun at Harvard University's Berkman Center. It aggregates 53M+ items from 46 partner hubs across 40 states and 8,700+ contributing institutions, and partnered with HathiTrust in June 2013. It launched the Banned Book Club in July 2023.

State-Level Consortia

ConsortiumStateMembersDescription
OhioLINKOhio88Ohio Library and Information Network serving 88 institutions (16 public universities, 23 community/technical colleges, 48 private colleges, and the State Library of Ohio) with 117 libraries and more than 800,000 users. Part of the Ohio Technology Consortium (OH-TECH) and funded by the Ohio General Assembly through the Department of Higher Education. Also partners in Libraries Connect Ohio (LCO) with INFOhio, OPLIN, and the State Library of Ohio to build the Ohio Web Library.
TexShareTexas53Statewide resource-sharing consortium administered by the Texas State Library and Archives Commission. Founded in 1994 with 53 publicly supported academic and medical libraries, it has since expanded by statute to include independent academic institutions, community colleges, public libraries, and clinical medicine libraries, and now comprises hundreds of member libraries. Offers the TexShare Databases, the TexShare Card for reciprocal borrowing, and works with the TexQuest K-12 program and the E-Read Texas e-books program.
VIVAVirginia74The Virtual Library of Virginia, established July 1994, a consortium of 74 nonprofit academic libraries comprising all 39 state-assisted colleges and universities, 34 independent (private, nonprofit) institutions, and the Library of Virginia. Operates under a memorandum of understanding with a 15-member steering committee and three standing committees (Outreach, Resources for Users, and Resource Sharing). Received the Governor's Technology Gold Award in 2003.

Consortial Services

ServiceProviderDescription
Shared cataloging (WorldCat)OCLCOCLC and its member libraries cooperatively produce and maintain WorldCat, the largest online public access catalog in the world, with holding records from public and private libraries worldwide. OCLC assigns a unique OCLC Control Number (OCN) to each bibliographic record; over a billion OCNs had been created by mid-2013 and were declared public domain in September 2013.
Interlibrary loan and resource sharingOCLC (Tipasa, ILLiad, WorldShare Interlibrary Loan)OCLC operates the largest resource sharing network in the world with 10,000+ libraries worldwide, built on more than 40 years of leadership. Its Express digital delivery program has more than 1,000 participating libraries and fills copy requests in an average of 10 hours. WorldShare ILL and Tipasa are cloud-based ILL management systems; ILLiad is hosted in partnership with Atlas Systems.
Shared digital repositoryHathiTrustA large-scale collaborative repository holding more than 18 million digitized volumes (including 6.7 million in the US public domain) with full-text search across the entire corpus. The Emergency Temporary Access Service (ETAS) provides lawful access to in-copyright digital materials in place of physical books during closures such as public health emergencies.
Joint licensing and content negotiationVarious consortia (VIVA, TexShare, OhioLINK, NERL/CRL, SCELC, California Digital Library, etc.)The most traditional consortial function: negotiating better prices and licensing terms for electronic databases, e-journals, ebooks, and streaming media. A growing focus is Open Access through transformative agreements, such as the California Digital Library's 2021 deal with Elsevier to make all University of California research open access over four years.
Shared storage and preservationCenter for Research LibrariesCRL acquires and preserves traditional and digital research resources including foreign newspapers, government documents, microform archives, historic journals, and foreign dissertations, and lends them to members at no charge while charging non-members. Its holdings are discoverable in OCLC's WorldCat, and it coordinates last-copy retention through the Global Resources Forum.
Shared print programsHathiTrust and regional/national consortiaCoordinated retention of scholarly monographs so that institutions within a region maintain a practical number of copies while freeing library space. The HathiTrust Shared Print Program commits participating libraries to retaining almost 18 million monograph volumes for 25 years, representing three-quarters of HathiTrust digital book holdings.
Technical standards developmentNISOANSI-accredited development of US national standards and recommended practices for publishing, libraries, and content distribution. Notable standards include ANSI/NISO Z39.50 (protocol for accessing bibliographic databases), Z39.85 (Dublin Core), Z39.88 (OpenURL), Z39.93 (SUSHI usage statistics), and Z39.96 (JATS Journal Article Tag Suite).
Open-source software hosting and shared technologyLyrasisSince 2014 Lyrasis has provided fiscal sponsorship and program services as the organizational home for open-source projects serving libraries, museums, and archives, including CollectionSpace, ArchivesSpace, Fedora, DSpace, and VIVO. It also leads the ORCID US community of practice and acquired the Palace Project e-book platform from DPLA in 2025.
High-speed research and education networkingInternet2Operation of a 100 Gbit/s dynamic hybrid optical and packet network backbone for research and education, connecting over 60,000 US institutions. Includes the InCommon Federation for federated identity, access to eduGAIN globally, eduroam wifi for research and education users, and Net+ cloud-based services tailored to the R&E community.
Professional developmentVarious consortia (Lyrasis, Amigos, OCLC WebJunction, Professional Development Alliance)Consortia provide paid and free training, workshops, conferences, and skill-sharing for library staff. OCLC's WebJunction, funded by the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation since 2003, is an online learning hub. The Professional Development Alliance shares professional development content across consortia.

Consortia Timeline

YearEvent
1867The American Library Association's Committee on Cooperation in Indexing and Cataloguing College Libraries documents the earliest US library cooperation.
1932The Association of Research Libraries (ARL) holds its first meeting in Chicago on December 29 with 42 major university and research libraries.
1933The Triangle Research Libraries Network (TRLN) is founded to serve the major academic libraries of North Carolina, the earliest formally established US library consortium.
1939NISO is founded as the Z39 Committee to develop bibliographic and library technical standards.
1949The Center for Research Libraries is founded as the Midwest Inter-Library Center (MILC) by ten midwestern universities, an early form of shared print.
1967OCLC is formed on July 5 as the Ohio College Library Center for the purposes of shared library cataloging, founded by Frederick G. Kilgour.
1971The first online cataloging by any library worldwide occurs on August 26 at Ohio University's Alden Library through OCLC.
1974Amigos Library Services is formed in Dallas as the AMIGOS Bibliographic Council by 22 libraries to bring OCLC access to the southwestern United States.
1990ARL co-founds the Coalition for Networked Information (CNI) with EDUCAUSE to advance higher education through information technology.
1992OhioLINK begins operations as six Ohio universities install its systems and start building the central catalog.
1994TexShare is established with 53 Texas academic and medical libraries, and the Virtual Library of Virginia (VIVA) is established in July.
1996The Internet2 project is established by 34 university researchers (formally organized as UCAID in 1997) to serve research and education networking needs.
1997The International Coalition of Library Consortia (ICOLC) first meets informally as the Consortium of Consortia (COC).
2008HathiTrust is founded in October by the Committee on Institutional Cooperation universities and the University of California libraries, and CRL receives a $1.45 million Andrew W. Mellon Foundation grant for its digital strategy.
2009Lyrasis is formed in April from the merger of SOLINET and PALINET, later absorbing NELINET (2009) and the Bibliographical Center for Research (2011).
2013The Digital Public Library of America (DPLA) launches on April 18 with nearly 2.5 million records from six Service Hubs and ten Content Hubs.
2021The California Digital Library negotiates a landmark transformative open-access agreement with Elsevier, making University of California research open access over four years.

Key Facts

  • A library consortium is any cooperative association of libraries that coordinates resources and activities; the bedrock principle is that libraries can accomplish more together than alone.
  • North America (the United States and Canada) accounts for nearly 60% of consortia registered with the International Coalition of Library Consortia (ICOLC).
  • ICOLC currently has 243 participating consortia worldwide and is entirely informal, with no dues, no elected officials, and no bylaws.
  • OCLC, founded in 1967 as the Ohio College Library Center, is the largest library consortium with nearly 30,000 member libraries in 100+ countries and annual revenue of about $217.8 million (2020-21).
  • WorldCat, maintained cooperatively by OCLC and its member libraries, is the largest online public access catalog in the world; over a billion OCLC Control Numbers had been created by mid-2013.
  • The first online cataloging by any library worldwide occurred on August 26, 1971, at Ohio University's Alden Library through OCLC.
  • OCLC maintains the Dewey Decimal Classification system, having acquired the trademark and copyrights with Forest Press in 1988.
  • HathiTrust, founded in October 2008, holds more than 18 million digitized volumes, including 6.7 million in the US public domain (as of September 2024).
  • The HathiTrust Shared Print Program commits participating libraries to retaining almost 18 million monograph volumes for 25 years, representing three-quarters of its digital book holdings.
  • ARL's 125 member libraries spend approximately $4.5 billion every year on information resources and make up a large portion of the academic and research library marketplace.
  • The Center for Research Libraries, founded in 1949 by ten midwestern universities, has 250 member libraries and hosts NERL, which negotiates licenses for 28 member and 80 affiliate academic research libraries.
  • The 1960s and 1970s are considered the heyday of consortia development in the United States.
  • Collaborative licensing of electronic resources rose to prominence in the mid-1990s as libraries shifted from print-focused collection development to online databases and e-journals.
  • OCLC's resource sharing network is the largest in the world with 10,000+ libraries; its Express digital delivery program fills copy requests in an average of 10 hours.
  • Internet2 operates a 100 Gbit/s network backbone connecting over 60,000 US educational, research, government, and community anchor institutions including public libraries and museums.
  • The Digital Public Library of America aggregates 53M+ items from 46 partner hubs across 40 states and 8,700+ contributing institutions, and launched the Banned Book Club in July 2023.
  • Lyrasis, formed in 2009 from the merger of SOLINET and PALINET, has 1,000+ members and serves as the organizational home for open-source projects including DSpace, Fedora, ArchivesSpace, and VIVO.
  • NISO, founded in 1939 as the Z39 Committee, develops standards including Z39.50 (database access protocol), Dublin Core (Z39.85), OpenURL (Z39.88), SUSHI (Z39.93), and JATS (Z39.96).
  • OhioLINK serves more than 800,000 students, faculty, and staff at 88 institutions with 117 libraries in Ohio.
  • VIVA's 74 member institutions include all 39 state-assisted Virginia colleges and universities, 34 independent (private, nonprofit) institutions, and the Library of Virginia.
  • TexShare began in 1994 with 53 publicly supported academic and medical libraries and has since expanded by statute to include public libraries, community colleges, and clinical medicine libraries.
  • Amigos Library Services, founded in 1974 by 22 libraries to bring OCLC access to the southwest, merged with MLNC in 2012 to become the largest library network west of the Mississippi River, serving libraries across 22 states.
  • Many libraries commonly belong to multiple consortia, so aggregate membership counts across consortia overcount unique libraries; OCLC's nearly 30,000 member libraries represent the largest single consortial membership.
  • The California Digital Library's 2021 transformative agreement with Elsevier is a prominent US example of consortial open-access negotiation, converting paywalled content to open access.

Source: Wikipedia articles on OCLC, HathiTrust, ARL, the Center for Research Libraries, OhioLINK, TexShare, VIVA, Amigos, Lyrasis, Internet2, NISO, and the Digital Public Library of America. OCLC annual reports and WorldCat statistics. DPLA partner data from dpla.org.

Digital Libraries & E-Books: The Digital Reading Revolution

The digital library revolution has transformed how Americans access books, research, and cultural heritage. Project Gutenberg (1971) was the first digital library, offering free e-books decades before the term existed. The Internet Archive (1996) pioneered web preservation and digital lending. Google Books (2004) set out to scan every book ever published. HathiTrust (2008) created a shared research repository of 18+ million volumes. OverDrive (2003) became the dominant e-book lending platform for public libraries. The Digital Public Library of America (2013) aggregates 50+ million items from 5,000+ institutions. Together, these digital libraries represent a fundamental shift in how knowledge is preserved, discovered, and shared.

Major Digital Libraries

NameFoundedFounderItemsKey FeatureDescription
Project Gutenberg1971Michael Hart70,000Free public domain e-books in multiple formatsThe world's first digital library. Founded at the University of Illinois, it offers 70,000+ free e-books, mostly public domain classics. Predates the Internet by decades.
Internet Archive1996Brewster Kahle41,000,000Wayback Machine (835B web pages); controlled digital lendingNon-profit digital library offering free universal access to 41M+ texts, 14M+ videos, 15M+ audio recordings, and the Wayback Machine with 835B+ archived web pages. Operates controlled digital lending of scanned books.

Timeline

YearEvent
1971Michael Hart founds Project Gutenberg at University of Illinois - the first digital library
1996Brewster Kahle founds the Internet Archive; begins archiving the web
2001Wayback Machine publicly launched, providing free access to archived web pages
2003OverDrive launches e-book lending platform for libraries
2004Google Books project announced; begins scanning books from major research libraries
2005Open Library project initiated by Internet Archive

2013Authors Guild v. Google settles; Google Books ruled fair use. DPLA launches with 2.4M items

2024Internet Archive loses Hachette v. Internet Archive lawsuit over controlled digital lending

Key Facts

  • Project Gutenberg (1971) is the world's oldest digital library, founded 20 years before the World Wide Web became public
  • The Internet Archive's Wayback Machine contains 835 billion+ archived web pages, growing by ~600 million pages daily
  • Google Books has scanned over 40 million books, making it the largest single digitization effort in history
  • HathiTrust holds 18+ million digitized volumes from research libraries and provides full-text search across the entire collection
  • OverDrive served 600+ million digital checkouts in 2023 through 92,000+ libraries and schools in 100+ countries
  • The DPLA aggregates 50+ million digital items from 5,000+ cultural heritage institutions through a single free platform
  • Open Library aims to create 'one web page for every book ever published' and currently has 20+ million book records
  • The Internet Archive's controlled digital lending program allows one user at a time to borrow scanned books, mirroring physical lending
  • The Authors Guild v. Google (2013) established that mass book digitization for search constitutes fair use
  • The Internet Archive lost the Hachette v. Internet Archive (2024) lawsuit, which challenged controlled digital lending of scanned books
  • OverDrive's Libby app has been downloaded 50+ million times and is the primary way Americans borrow library e-books
  • COVID-19 accelerated e-book borrowing dramatically: OverDrive checkouts jumped from 350M in 2019 to 430M in 2020

Source: Internet Archive, Project Gutenberg, Open Library, Google Books, HathiTrust, OverDrive/Libby, Digital Public Library of America (DPLA), Wikipedia articles on digital libraries, and Internet Archive annual reports. OverDrive checkout data from company press releases.

Reading Habits & Literacy in America

Americans' reading habits have shifted dramatically in the digital age. According to the NEA's Survey of Public Participation in the Arts (SPPA 2022), 23% of American adults read literature (novels, short stories, poetry, or plays) in the previous 12 months. The Pew Research Center found that 54% of Americans used a public library in 2013, and Gallup reported Americans averaged 10.5 library visits per year in 2019. Despite concerns about declining reading, e-book and audiobook borrowing through libraries has surged, with 600+ million digital checkouts in 2023. The relationship between library access, literacy, and reading habits remains a critical area of research.

Key Facts

  • Only 23% of American adults read literature (novels, short stories, poetry, or plays) in 2022, per the NEA SPPA survey
  • 54% of Americans used a public library in the past 12 months (Pew Research Center 2013)
  • Americans average 10.5 library visits per year (Gallup 2019)
  • 14% of US adults perform at below basic prose literacy levels, struggling with simple text comprehension (NCES PIAAC)
  • Women read literature at nearly double the rate of men (30% vs 16%, NEA 2022)
  • College-educated adults read literature at 35% compared to 12% for those without high school diplomas
  • Audiobooks are the fastest-growing reading format, with 23% of Americans listening regularly
  • Print books remain the dominant format, with 65% of readers preferring physical books
  • Library users are more civically engaged than non-users, volunteering and attending community events at higher rates
  • 94% of Americans believe having a public library improves community quality of life
  • States with the highest literacy rates (NH, MN, ND at 93-94%) correlate with strong public library systems
  • States with the lowest literacy rates (MS, LA at 72%) also tend to have fewer school librarians per student
  • Digital checkouts through libraries exceeded 600 million in 2023, showing strong demand for e-books and audiobooks
  • The COVID-19 pandemic accelerated digital reading but reduced in-person library visits, creating a permanent shift in usage patterns

Source: NEA Survey of Public Participation in the Arts (SPPA) 2022, Pew Research Center library usage surveys (2013, 2016), Gallup library visit survey (2019), NCES PIAAC adult literacy assessment, and Pew Internet & American Life Project reading habits studies.

School Library Inequities: The SLIDE Project

The SLIDE (School Librarian Investigation: Decline in Education) project documents stark inequities in school library staffing across the United States. Their research reveals that approximately 3 million students in majority-nonwhite districts lack access to a school librarian, and that school librarian losses have fallen disproportionately on districts serving Black, Latino, and Native American students. The project combines NCES data with demographic analysis to expose how the 20% national decline in school librarians since 2010 has created a two-tier system where wealthier, whiter districts retain librarians while poorer, diverse districts lose them.

Key Findings

  • 3 million students in majority-nonwhite school districts attend schools without a librarian
  • 54% of students in majority-nonwhite districts lack access to a school librarian (vs 24% in majority-white districts)
  • Schools with higher percentages of Black, Latino, and Native American students are more likely to have lost librarians
  • Rural districts are particularly affected: 35% have no librarian at all
  • Districts with the most students in poverty have lost librarians at 2-3x the rate of affluent districts
  • The decline in school librarians accelerated after the Great Recession (2008) and has not recovered
  • 25 states have lost 20% or more of their school librarians since 2010
  • The geographic distribution of librarian losses maps closely to patterns of educational inequality

Key Facts

  • The SLIDE project found that 3 million students in majority-nonwhite districts lack access to a school librarian
  • 54% of students in majority-nonwhite districts attend schools without librarians, compared to 24% in majority-white districts
  • California lost 46% of its school librarians between 2010 and 2019, the largest decline of any state
  • Michigan lost 36% and Florida lost 31% of school librarians in the same period
  • The decline in school librarians closely tracks patterns of racial and economic inequality in American education
  • Rural districts are especially hard-hit, with 35% having no librarian at all
  • The SLIDE project combines NCES SASS data with demographic analysis from the Common Core of Data
  • Schools that lost librarians often simultaneously lost other support staff, compounding the impact on students
  • Research consistently shows that the absence of school librarians correlates with lower reading scores
  • The Every Student Succeeds Act (2015) allows states to use federal funds for school library programs, but few do
  • The SLIDE project recommends targeted state and federal investment to restore librarian positions in high-need districts
  • Without intervention, the school librarian equity gap is likely to widen as pandemic-era budget pressures continue

Source: SLIDE (School Librarian Investigation: Decline in Education) project at libslide.org, using NCES Schools and Staffing Survey (SASS) data, Common Core of Data (CCD), and demographic analysis. SLIDE is led by school library researchers Deborah Rinio, Ann Carlson Weeks, and Mega Subramaniam.

Public Attitudes Toward Libraries: What Americans Think

Americans overwhelmingly value their public libraries. Majorities say libraries provide the resources they need, and two-thirds say closing their local library would have a major impact on their community. Yet despite this broad support, library visitation has been declining, particularly among younger and lower-income Americans. This section examines the gap between how Americans value libraries and how they actually use them, drawing on data from Pew Research Center, Gallup, and the NEA Survey of Public Participation in the Arts.

Key Facts

  • 77% of Americans say libraries provide the resources they need (Pew 2016).
  • 66% say closing their local public library would have a major impact on their community (Pew 2016), up from 63% in 2013.
  • 69% say libraries contribute a lot as a safe place to spend time, and 58% say they provide educational opportunities.
  • 80% of Americans say libraries should definitely teach digital skills, showing high expectations for library tech services.
  • Despite valuing libraries, only 31% say books should definitely not be moved to make room for tech spaces, while 24% support it and 40% are open to it.
  • Among recent library visitors, 64% checked out a printed book (down from 73% in 2012), while program attendance rose 10 points from 2015 to 2016.
  • Women visit libraries more often than men: 13.4 vs 7.5 visits per year (Gallup 2019).
  • Young adults (18-29) are the most frequent library visitors at 15.5 visits per year (Gallup 2019).
  • Lower-income Americans (<$40K) visit libraries more often (12.2 visits/yr) than higher-income ($100K+, 8.5 visits/yr).
  • Midwesterners visit libraries most often (12.9 visits/yr), while Southerners visit least (9.3 visits/yr).
  • College graduates are the most likely to have visited a library in the past year (59%), followed by women (57%) and parents (55%).
  • Among library tech users, 61% use library internet for school or work research, and 38% use it to acquire health information.

Source: Pew Research Center (Libraries 2016, How Americans Value Public Libraries 2013, Library Usage and Engagement 2016), Gallup library visit frequency survey (2019), and NEA Survey of Public Participation in the Arts (SPPA) 2022 demographic tables.

Library Access Equity: Who Uses Libraries & Why It Matters

Public libraries are among the most equitably used institutions in American life, serving Americans across all racial, ethnic, and income groups at remarkably similar rates. The landmark US IMPACT Study (2010) found that 77 million Americans age 14+ used a library computer or wireless network in a single year, making libraries the nation's primary free internet access provider. During the Great Recession, libraries became essential job-seeking infrastructure, with 62% of unemployed Americans using libraries and 92% of libraries providing job resources. This section examines the equity of library access and the critical role libraries play in bridging digital, economic, and informational divides.

The US IMPACT Study (2010)

Nearly one-third of Americans age 14+ used a public library computer or wireless network in the prior year.

33% of Americans age 14+ — approximately 77 million people — used a library computer or wireless network.

The US IMPACT Study, funded by the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation, was the first large-scale national study to document the critical role of public library computers and internet access in American life. It surveyed over 45,000 people across the US and found that library technology access was essential for education, employment, health, e-government, and social connection.

Source: U.S. IMPACT Study 2010, Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation, via ALA fact sheet

Key Facts

  • 77 million Americans age 14+ used a public library computer or wireless network in the prior year (US IMPACT Study 2010).
  • Nearly one-third (33%) of Americans age 14+ relied on library technology access (US IMPACT Study 2010).
  • Library use is remarkably equitable across racial groups: 72% of Asian/Pacific Islander Americans, 65% of white Americans, 63% of Black Americans, and 58% of Hispanic Americans used a library in the past year (1997 study).
  • 53% of Asian/Pacific Islander Americans used a library in the past month, the highest rate of any racial group (1997 study).
  • Black Americans use library computers at a rate 63% higher than white Americans (13% vs 8%, NCES 2002).
  • During the Great Recession, 62% of unemployed Americans used libraries for job-seeking (PLFTAS 2009-10).
  • 92% of public libraries provide job-seeking resources, making them the most widespread free job assistance infrastructure in the country (PLFTAS 2011-12).
  • 96% of public libraries assist with e-government services, helping citizens navigate government programs online (PLFTAS 2011-12).
  • 15% of libraries cut hours during the recession, reducing access at the exact moment demand was highest (PLFTAS 2009-10).
  • The US IMPACT Study was funded by the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation and surveyed over 45,000 Americans.
  • Library computer/internet use for school assignments was nearly equal across racial groups: Black 25%, Asian 26%, White 22%, Hispanic 20% (NCES 2002).
  • The PLFTAS (Public Library Funding & Technology Access Study) documented the critical intersection of library funding, technology access, and community need during 2009-2012.

Source: US IMPACT Study 2010 (Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation), NCES Households Use of Public and Other Types of Libraries (2002), ALA fact sheets citing 1997 American Libraries article, and ALA Public Library Funding & Technology Access Study (PLFTAS) 2009-2012.

Reading & Library Visits: The Long Decline (NEA SPPA 2012-2022)

The NEA Survey of Public Participation in the Arts (SPPA) documents a troubling long-term decline in both reading and library visitation. Between 2012 and 2022, the percentage of Americans reading any book fell from 54.6% to 48.5%, while novel/short story reading dropped from 45.2% to 37.6%. Simultaneously, IMLS data shows library visits per capita fell from 3.85 in FY2019 to 2.53 in FY2024 — a 34% decline that predates but was accelerated by COVID-19. This section presents the year-by-year data from NEA and IMLS.

Key Facts

  • The percentage of Americans reading any book declined from 54.6% in 2012 to 48.5% in 2022 — a 6.1 percentage point drop in a decade (NEA SPPA).
  • Novel and short story reading fell from 45.2% (2012) to 37.6% (2022), a decline of 7.6 points.
  • Poetry reading spiked to 11.7% in 2017 (up from 6.7% in 2012) before falling back to 9.2% in 2022.
  • Play reading has remained consistently low: 2.9% (2012), 3.7% (2017), 2.4% (2022).
  • IMLS per-capita library visits fell from 3.85 (FY2019) to 2.01 (FY2022) — a 48% decline — before partially recovering to 2.53 in FY2024.
  • Total IMLS library visits dropped from 1.25 billion (FY2019) to 732 million (FY2020) due to COVID-19 closures.
  • By FY2024, library visits recovered to 869 million but remained 34% below FY2019 per-capita levels.
  • Pew Research Center measured in-person library visit rate at 53% in 2012, declining to 44% by 2015 before recovering to 48% in 2016.
  • Gallup (2019) found Americans averaged 10.5 library visits per year, with young adults (18-29) visiting most at 15.5 times per year.
  • The NEA SPPA first measured public library visitation in 2022, finding a 23% visit rate.

Source: NEA Survey of Public Participation in the Arts (SPPA) 2012, 2017, 2022; IMLS Public Libraries Survey FY2019-FY2024; Pew Research Center library usage surveys (2012-2016); Gallup library visit frequency survey (2019).

Special Libraries & Mobile Services: Beyond the Public Library

America's library ecosystem extends far beyond public library buildings. Special libraries serve specialized audiences — corporate libraries, law libraries, medical libraries, military libraries, and museum libraries. Bookmobiles bring library services to rural communities, nursing homes, and schools. Summer reading programs operate at 95% of public libraries. Friends of Libraries groups provide volunteer and financial support. This section covers the diverse types of libraries and services that make up the American library landscape.

Corporate libraries—Libraries maintained by corporations for employees, containing industry-specific resources, market research, and technical documentation.

Government libraries—Libraries serving government agencies, including the Library of Congress, National Library of Medicine, and agency-specific libraries.
Military libraries—Libraries on military bases and in defense agencies, serving service members and their families worldwide.
Museum libraries—Libraries within museums supporting curatorial research, exhibition development, and scholarly access to collections.
News libraries—Libraries maintained by newspapers and media organizations, preserving archives and supporting journalistic research.

Bookmobiles: Libraries on Wheels

A bookmobile, or mobile library, is a vehicle designed for use as a library. They have been known by many names throughout history, including traveling library, library wagon, book wagon, book truck, library-on-wheels, and book auto service. Bookmobiles expand the reach of traditional libraries by transporting books to potential readers, providing library services to people in otherwise underserved locations and/or circumstances. Bookmobile services and materials, may be customized for the locations and populations served.

Bookmobile History

PeriodEvent
1905Mary L. Titcomb, librarian at Washington County Free Library (MD), introduces the first American bookmobile — a wagon drawn by horses to deliver books to rural areas.
1920sMotorized bookmobiles begin to replace horse-drawn wagons, dramatically expanding reach.
1956Library Services Act provides federal funding to extend library services to rural areas, accelerating bookmobile adoption.
1970sPeak era of bookmobiles, with over 2,000 operating nationwide.
1990sBookmobile numbers begin declining as digital services and branch construction expand.
2024IMLS PLS FY2024 reports 767 bookmobiles still operating, serving rural and homebound populations.

Significance: Bookmobiles remain critical for reaching populations that cannot visit physical libraries: rural communities, nursing homes, schools without libraries, homebound individuals, and isolated populations.

Summer Reading Programs

Summer reading programs take place at 95% of public libraries in the United States. Children, teens, and adults participate in activities meant to encourage reading, such as keeping a reading log. Other names for summer reading programs include vacation reading club, summer reading club, vacation reading program, and summer library program.

95% of US public libraries offer summer reading programs.

Summer reading programs combat the 'summer slide' — the loss of reading skills during school vacation. Research shows children who participate maintain or improve reading levels, with disproportionate benefits for low-income children.

Friends of Libraries

Friends of Libraries are non-profit, charitable groups formed to support libraries in their communities. Support from the Friends groups may be financial, political and cultural. Groups are separate from the libraries they support and made up of volunteers. Generally, groups are structured, handle finances and work closely with library management. In the United States, Friends groups also work closely with the American Library Association (ALA). Other countries, such as Australia, France, South Africa and the United Kingdom all have Friends of Libraries groups.

Role: Friends of Libraries groups are non-profit, charitable organizations that support libraries through fundraising, advocacy, volunteer work, and cultural programming.

Friends Group Activities

  • Used book sales — a primary fundraising activity generating millions nationally
  • Advocacy for library funding at local, state, and federal levels
  • Sponsoring author talks, book clubs, and cultural programs
  • Funding summer reading program prizes and materials
  • Supporting library capital campaigns and building projects
  • Lobbying against censorship and book banning efforts

The American Library Association (ALA)

The American Library Association (ALA) is a nonprofit organization based in the United States that promotes libraries and library education internationally. It is the oldest and largest library association in the world.

Founded: 1876 · Headquarters: Chicago, Illinois · Membership: approximately 50,000+ members

The ALA is the oldest and largest library association in the world. It accredits MLIS programs, sets professional standards, advocates for library funding and intellectual freedom, and publishes American Libraries magazine.

Key ALA Divisions

  • ALA Advocacy & Government Relations
  • ALA Office for Intellectual Freedom (tracks book challenges and censorship)
  • ALA Office for Diversity, Literacy and Outreach Services
  • American Association of School Librarians (AASL)
  • Public Library Association (PLA)
  • Association of College and Research Libraries (ACRL)
  • American Library Trustee Association (ALTA)

Library Services and Technology Act (LSTA)

United States President Bill Clinton signed the Library Services and Technology Act (LSTA) on October 1, 1996. LSTA is a United States federal library grant program. Its roots come from the Library Services Act, first enacted in 1956. LSTA replaced the Library Services and Construction Act (LSCA), first enacted in 1962. The National Commission on Libraries and Information Science held two White House Conferences that generated discussion and support.

Enacted: 1996 · Predecessor: Library Services and Construction Act (LSCA, 1962) · Roots: Library Services Act (1956)

LSTA is the primary federal funding mechanism for libraries, administered by IMLS. It provides grants to states for library technology, services to underserved populations, and information access programs.

Key Facts

  • 767 bookmobiles operate across 49 states and territories (IMLS PLS FY2024).
  • Kentucky leads the nation with 74 bookmobiles, followed by Ohio (68) and California (65).
  • The first American bookmobile was introduced in 1905 by Mary L. Titcomb at Washington County Free Library in Maryland, using a horse-drawn wagon.
  • 95% of US public libraries offer summer reading programs, making them the most widespread library program in the country.
  • Special libraries include corporate, law, medical, theological, government, military, museum, and news libraries — each serving specialized audiences.
  • The American Library Association, founded in 1876, is the oldest and largest library association in the world.
  • The Library Services and Technology Act (LSTA), signed by President Clinton in 1996, is the primary federal funding mechanism for libraries.
  • LSTA replaced the Library Services and Construction Act (1962), which itself grew from the Library Services Act (1956).
  • Friends of Libraries groups are non-profit volunteer organizations that support libraries through fundraising, advocacy, and programming.
  • Bookmobile numbers peaked in the 1970s at over 2,000 nationwide, declining as digital services expanded.
  • Summer reading programs combat the 'summer slide' with disproportionate benefits for low-income children.
  • The ALA accredits Master of Library and Information Science (MLIS) programs and sets professional standards for the field.

Source: Wikipedia articles on special libraries, bookmobiles, the Library Services and Technology Act, summer reading programs, Friends of Libraries, and the American Library Association. Bookmobile counts from IMLS Public Libraries Survey FY2024. Special library type counts from the project's private library database.

Book Format Shift: Print, E-Books & Audiobooks (2011 vs 2025)

Over 14 years, Americans' reading format preferences shifted dramatically. Print declined from 72% to 64%, while e-books more than doubled from 17% to 31% and audiobooks more than tripled from 11% to 26%. Overall reading remained stable at ~75%.

Key Facts

  • Print book readership declined from 72% (2011) to 64% (2025), an 8-point drop.
  • E-book readership more than doubled from 17% (2011) to 31% (2025).
  • Audiobook readership more than tripled from 11% (2011) to 26% (2025).
  • Overall reading remained stable at ~75%.
  • The e-book surge reflects Kindle, Libby/OverDrive, and library digital lending.
  • Audiobook growth is driven by smartphone apps and commute listening.
  • Print remains the dominant format at 64%.

Source: Pew Research Center reading format surveys (2011 and 2025), via census_library_usage cache data.

Intellectual Freedom & the Library Bill of Rights

Intellectual freedom — the right to access information and ideas without restriction — is a core value of the library profession. The American Library Association's Library Bill of Rights, first adopted in 1939, establishes the principles that libraries should provide materials representing all viewpoints and resist censorship. In an era of unprecedented book challenges, these principles are being tested as never before.

20,808
Book challenges
6,875
Banned/removed
1939
Bill of Rights adopted
1982
Banned Books Week
1967
OIF founded
1939
ALA founding value

The Library Bill of Rights

The Library Bill of Rights is the American Library Association's statement expressing the rights of library users to intellectual freedom and the expectations the association places on libraries to support those rights. The Association's Council has adopted a number of interpretations of the document applying it to various library policies.

Adopted: 1939 by the American Library Association

Key Principles

  • Books and other library resources should be provided for the interest, information, and enlightenment of all people of the community the library serves.
  • Libraries should provide materials and information presenting all points of view on current and historical issues.
  • Libraries should challenge censorship in the fulfillment of their responsibility to provide information and enlightenment.
  • Libraries should cooperate with all persons and groups concerned with resisting abridgment of free expression and free access to ideas.
  • A person's right to use a library should not be denied or abridged because of origin, age, background, or views.
  • Libraries which make exhibit spaces and meeting rooms available to the public they serve should make such facilities available on an equitable basis, regardless of the beliefs or affiliations of individuals or groups requesting their use.

Intellectual Freedom

Intellectual freedom encompasses the freedom to hold, receive and disseminate ideas without restriction. Viewed as an integral component of a democratic society, intellectual freedom protects an individual's right to access, explore, consider, and express ideas and information as the basis for a self-governing, well-informed citizenry. Intellectual freedom comprises the bedrock for freedoms of expression, speech, and the press and relates to freedoms of information and the right to privacy.

The ALA Office for Intellectual Freedom (OIF), founded in 1967, is the primary organization defending intellectual freedom in libraries. It tracks book challenges, supports librarians facing censorship pressure, and promotes the Library Bill of Rights.

Banned Books Week

Banned Books Week is an annual awareness campaign promoted by the American Library Association and Amnesty International, that celebrates the freedom to read, draws attention to banned and challenged books, and highlights persecuted individuals. Held in late September or early October since 1982, the United States campaign "stresses the importance of ensuring the availability of those unorthodox or unpopular viewpoints to all who wish to read them" and the requirement to keep material publicly available so that people can develop their own conclusions and opinions. The international campaign notes individuals "persecuted because of the writings that they produce, circulate or read." Some of the events that occur during Banned Book Week are The Virtual Read-Out and The First Amendment Film Festival.

Started: 1982

Banned Books Week is an annual awareness campaign promoted by the American Library Association and Amnesty International that celebrates the freedom to read, draws attention to banned and challenged books, and highlights persecuted individuals.

Censorship in the United States

In the United States, censorship involves the suppression of speech or public communication and raises issues of freedom of speech, which is protected by the First Amendment to the United States Constitution. Interpretation of this fundamental freedom has varied since its enshrinement. Traditionally, the First Amendment was regarded as applying only to the Federal government, leaving the states and local communities free to censor or not. As the applicability of states' rights in lawmaking vis-a-vis citizens' national rights began to wane in the wake of the Civil War, censorship by any level of government eventually came under scrutiny, but not without resistance. For example, in recent decades, censorial restraints increased during the 1950s period of widespread anti-communist sentiment, as exemplified by the hearings of the House Committee on Un-American Activities. In Miller v. California (1973), the U.S. Supreme Court found that the First Amendment's freedom of speech does not apply to obscenity, which can, therefore, be censored. While certain forms of hate speech are legal so long as they do not turn to action or incite others to commit illegal acts, more severe forms have led to people or groups being denied marching permits or the Westboro Baptist Church being sued, although the initial adverse ruling against the latter was later overturned on appeal to the U.S. Supreme Court case Snyder v. Phelps.

Key Facts

  • The Library Bill of Rights was adopted by the ALA in 1939, establishing intellectual freedom as a foundational library principle.
  • 20,808 book challenges were documented, resulting in 6,875 bans or removals.
  • The ALA Office for Intellectual Freedom (OIF) was founded in 1967 to defend intellectual freedom in libraries.
  • Banned Books Week, started in 1982, is an annual campaign celebrating the freedom to read and highlighting censorship.
  • The Library Bill of Rights states that materials should represent all points of view, not just popular or majority viewpoints.
  • Intellectual freedom includes the right to access information without government or community censorship.
  • The modern wave of book challenges targets primarily LGBTQ+ content and books about race and racism.
  • Libraries are required by the Library Bill of Rights to resist censorship pressures from any source.

Source: Wikipedia articles on the Library Bill of Rights, intellectual freedom, censorship in the United States, and Banned Books Week. Challenge data from ALA Office for Intellectual Freedom and ALA State of America's Libraries 2024 report.

Impact Studies

StudyKey Finding
First Colorado study: in 221 public schools (1988-89), better-funded library media centers linked to higher reading scores regardless of community wealth or adult education levels. LMC staff size, col
Alaska study (211 schools, 1997-98): school librarians had a direct relationship with student performance; staff activities such as teaching information literacy and collaborating with teachers affect
Pennsylvania 'Measuring Up to Standards' (500 libraries): presence of adequate school library staffing linked to higher PSSA reading scores; as library staffing rises, reading scores rise. L
Oregon study ('Good Schools Have School Librarians'): school librarians collaborating to improve academic achievement; fourth of four statewide studies confirming the pattern.
Synthesis of four statewide studies (AK, CO, PA, OR) showing quality library media programs affect academic achievement through roles of media specialists in teaching, information access, program admi
Idaho study (176 principals/administrators): where administrators value strong library programs and can see them supporting student success, students are more likely to thrive academically.
New York Phase I (IMLS-funded): extended previous statewide impact studies using multiple methods to explore the influence of the school library media specialist on technology use, principal-SLMS rela

Examined science collections and collaboration in Michigan middle school media centers; data-driven decision trend requires every learning component to document positive impact.

Library Economics & Return on Investment

The United States public library system represents a massive public investment with significant economic returns. As of FY2022, US public libraries reported total operating expenditures of $14,147,646,938 and total revenue of $15,709,000,375, serving a population of over 334,210,711 through approximately 9,249 library systems. Key Findings

How Many Have Library Cards: Approximately 155 million Americans were registered library borrowers in FY2024 (IMLS PLS); the ALA estimates ~166-170 million have library cards (active + household cards). Pew (2013) found 61% of Am

How Often They Visit: 54% of Americans used a public library in the past 12 months (Pew 2013); Americans averaged 10.5 library visits per year (Gallup 2019). IMLS per-capita visits fell from 3.85 (FY2019) to 2.53 (FY2024),

Most Used Services: Checking out printed books remains the most common activity (64% of recent visitors in 2016, down from 73% in 2012), followed by sitting/reading/studying (49%) and getting help from librarians (35%).

Reading Habits: 23% of US adults read literature in 2022 (NEA SPPA), down from 45.2% reading novels/short stories in 2012. Americans average ~12 books read per year. Print books dominate (65% of readers); audiobooks

Public Attitudes: 94% of Americans say a public library improves community quality of life; 77% say libraries provide the resources they need; 66% say closing the library would have a major impact on their community. 8

Library Buildings & Architecture

The architecture of American libraries reflects the evolution of the nation's civic ideals, from the colonial parish libraries of the early 1700s to the striking contemporary glass-and-steel structures of the twenty-first century. The earliest American libraries were subscription or social libraries, such as Benjamin Franklin's Library Company of Philadelphia (1731), and it was not until the mid-nineteenth century that the tax-supported public library took hold, led by the Boston Public Library (founded 1848, opened 1854) and the Peterborough, New Hampshire Town Library (1833), the f...

Carnegie Libraries

Andrew Carnegie funded the construction of 1,689 library buildings in the United States between 1883 and 1929, spending roughly $40 million. Of 1,412 communities served, 1,554 buildings were still extant as of 1992, with 911 still operating as libraries.

Library Building History Timeline

YearEvent
1731Benjamin Franklin and the Junto club founded the Library Company of Philadelphia, the first successful subscription library in the American colonies.
1743The Darby Library Company was organized in Pennsylvania by 29 Quakers, becoming one of the oldest libraries in the United States.
1800President John Adams signed the law creating the Library of Congress.
1815The purchase of Thomas Jefferson's book collection formed a core foundation for the Library of Congress.
1833The Peterborough, New Hampshire Town Library was founded at town meeting on April 9, becoming the first free public library supported by taxation in the world.
1848The Boston Public Library was founded, becoming the first large free municipal public library in the United States; it opened in 1854.
1883The first Carnegie library opened in Dunfermline, Scotland; the first U.S. Carnegie library was commissioned in 1886 in Allegheny, Pennsylvania.
1889The Carnegie Free Library of Braddock opened in Pennsylvania, the first Carnegie library to open in the United States; later designated a National Historic Landmark in 2012.
1890The Minneapolis Public Library was founded; its first central library was built in 1889.
1897The Library of Congress Thomas Jefferson Building opened, built 1890-1897 in the Beaux-Arts style, designed by Smithmeyer, Pelz, and Casey.
1901Andrew Carnegie offered $100,000 for a Nashville public library; the Carnegie Library Building was completed in 1904.
1911The New York Public Library Stephen A. Schwarzman Building opened on May 23, the largest marble structure in the U.S. at the time, with shelf space for 3.5 million volumes across 375,000 sq ft.
1939The Library of Congress John Adams Building opened to the public on April 5, featuring Art Deco design, 180 miles of shelving, and capacity for ten million volumes.
1965The National Historic Preservation Act established the National Register of Historic Places; the Thomas Jefferson Building and NYPL Main Branch were designated National Historic Landmarks on December 21.
1976The Library of Congress James Madison Memorial Building superstructure was completed; it was dedicated on April 24, 1980, serving as the official memorial to James Madison.
1991The Harold Washington Library Center opened in Chicago on October 7, the largest public library building in the world per Guinness World Records at 972,000 sq ft.
1993The U.S. Green Building Council was founded, laying the groundwork for the LEED green building certification program.
2003The Salt Lake City Public Library main branch, designed by Moshe Safdie, opened on February 8; later named Library Journal's Library of the Year in 2006.
2004The Seattle Central Library, designed by Rem Koolhaas and Joshua Prince-Ramus of OMA/LMN, opened on May 23; it received a 2005 AIA Honor Award.
2006The Minneapolis Central Library, designed by Cesar Pelli, opened on May 20 with a green roof and over 300 public computers.
2009Anythink Brighton in Colorado opened, considered the first carbon-negative library in the United States, and LEED Gold certified.
2013The San Diego Central Library, designed by Rob Quigley, opened on September 30, housing e3 Civic High School, the only U.S. school within a library.

Key Facts

  • Andrew Carnegie funded the construction of 1,689 library buildings in the United States between 1883 and 1929, spending roughly $40 million and building nearly half of all American public libraries by 1930.
  • The Library of Congress comprises three buildings named for U.S. presidents: the Thomas Jefferson Building (1897), the John Adams Building (1939), and the James Madison Memorial Building (1976/1980).
  • The Thomas Jefferson Building was designated a National Historic Landmark on December 21, 1965, for its prominent monumental Beaux-Arts structure and historical significance.
  • The Harold Washington Library Center in Chicago (1991) holds the Guinness World Record as the largest public library building in the world, approximately 972,000 square feet across ten floors.
  • The Peterborough, New Hampshire Town Library, founded April 9, 1833, was the first free public library supported by taxation in the world.
  • The Boston Public Library, founded in 1848, was the first large free municipal public library in the United States; its McKim Building (1895) features the vast Bates Hall reading room and a collection of over 24 million items.
  • The New York Public Library Main Branch (Schwarzman Building) opened in 1911 with shelf space for 3.5 million volumes spread across 375,000 square feet; its collection numbered about 2.5 million items as of 2015.
  • The Seattle Central Library (2004) by Rem Koolhaas features a continuous Books Spiral spanning four floors without breaking Dewey Decimal classification; it received a 2005 AIA Honor Award and was ranked #108 on the AIA's list of Americans' 150 favorite structures.
  • The James Madison Memorial Building contains 2.1 million square feet with 1.5 million square feet of assignable space, making it one of the three largest public buildings in the Washington metropolitan area.
  • The Carnegie Free Library of Braddock (1889) was the first Carnegie library to open in the United States and was designated a National Historic Landmark in March 2012.
  • Carnegie required recipient towns to provide a building site, pay staff, maintain the library, draw from public funds, provide free service to all, and annually provide ten percent of the building's construction cost for operation.
  • The National Register of Historic Places was established by the National Historic Preservation Act in 1966 and now includes more than 100,000 individually listed properties; as of 1999, 982 properties had been removed from the Register, most often due to being destroyed.
  • A 1992 survey by George Bobinski found that 1,554 of the 1,681 original Carnegie library buildings in the United States still existed, with 911 still used as libraries, 276 unchanged, 286 expanded, 175 remodeled, and 243 demolished.
  • Anythink Brighton in Colorado (2009) is considered the first carbon-negative library in the United States, producing more renewable energy than it uses and offsetting an additional 167,620 lbs of CO2 from other community buildings.
  • The San Diego Central Library (2013) houses e3 Civic High School, billed as the only school in the United States to be housed within a library, serving grades 9 through 12.
  • The John Adams Building features bronze entrance doors by sculptor Lee Lawrie depicting deities and heroes associated with the history of writing, including Sequoyah, the only historical person represented.
  • Carnegie libraries were built in a variety of styles including Beaux-Arts, Italian Renaissance, Baroque, Classical Revival, and Spanish Colonial, with each style chosen by the community.
  • The Salt Lake City Public Library (2003) by Moshe Safdie features a five-story curved glass wall, a 20,000 sq ft skylight, and a rooftop garden with tended beehives; it was named Library Journal's Library of the Year in 2006.
