Library Innovation: Beyond Books — Makerspaces, WiFi & Emerging Services

America's public libraries have evolved far beyond book lending into hubs of technological innovation and community creation. From WiFi hotspot lending that brings internet access into homes, to makerspaces equipped with 3D printers and audio/video editing tools, libraries are bridging the digital divide and fostering creativity in ways unimaginable a generation ago. Key Findings

  • AI moved to the center of library innovation after ChatGPT's November 2022 release: the ALA Center for the Future of Libraries tracks AI as a core trend card, and major vendors (OCLC, EBSCO, ProQuest, Clarivate, OverDrive) have embedded AI into cataloging, discovery, and reader engagement by 2023-2025.
  • The ALA Center for the Future of Libraries is the profession's foresight hub, mapping 40+ trends (Artificial Intelligence, Blockchain, Gamification, Internet of Things, Robots, Co-Working, Sharing Economy, Subscription Boxes, etc.) to library practice through trend cards, a fellowship, and an annual Symposium on the Future of Libraries.
  • IMLS is the primary federal innovation funder: a $313.58 million FY2023 budget across National Leadership Grants, Sparks! Ignition Grants, the Laura Bush 21st Century Librarian Program, and ~1,500 annual LSTA Grants to States - seeding landmarks like the Fayetteville Free Library makerspace (2011).
  • Library of Things programs, sparked by a Sacramento librarian's 2013 idea (the term itself was popularized in the UK in 2014), now span tool, seed, kitchen, instrument, toy, hotspot, and energy-monitor lending - with 500+ U.S. seed libraries alone.
  • Streaming is a core library service: Kanopy (founded 2008, acquired by OverDrive in 2021) and Hoopla Digital (launched 2013 by Midwest Tape of Holland, OH; 2,700+ library partners and 6.5 million cardholders as of 2020) deliver films, TV, music, audiobooks, ebooks, and comics to cardholders.
  • Helsinki's Oodi (opened 5 December 2018; IFLA Public Library of the Year 2019) and Aarhus's Dokk1 (opened 2015; robotic book delivery, 1,000-car automated parking) are the global benchmarks for future library space, with book-transport robots and only ~100,000 books on Oodi's top floor - the rest devoted to events and making.
  • Gamification is mainstream in U.S. libraries: 95% of public libraries run summer reading programs, now routinely gamified via Beanstack, READsquared, and badge systems, and library escape rooms (built on a format that spread globally in the 2010s) teach information literacy through timed puzzle-solving.
  • Future library space is being redefined around flexible, maker, and media uses: Chicago Public Library's 2013 Innovation Lab Maker Lab, Fayetteville (AR)'s 2021 teaching kitchen and innovation center, and Allen County Public Library's TV studios and radio station exemplify the shift from book storage to community creation.

Libraries are engines of small business creation — providing business databases, co-working spaces, patent resources, and SBDC partnerships.

Economic Impact

Library business services generate measurable local economic impact by reducing the cost of starting a business, supporting business survival and growth, and channeling federal SBA resources into communities. While library-wide return-on-investment studies have long shown libraries returning several dollars per dollar invested, entrepreneurship-specific impact is increasingly documented through ALA, ULC, SBDC, and SCORE reporting.
Overview
Economic-impact evidence supports continued public investment in library business services and underpins ALA/ULC advocacy for federal and state funding, including IMLS LSTA appropriations and statewide database licensing.
Policy Significance

Key Findings

  • U.S. public libraries have become a core piece of the nation's entrepreneurship infrastructure, providing free market-research databases, business-plan workshops, mentorship, co-working space, and prototyping equipment to founders who lack access to commercial alternatives.
  • Premium business databases licensed by libraries - ReferenceUSA (Data Axle), Gale Business, Morningstar, Value Line, IBISWorld, and LinkedIn Learning - eliminate thousands of dollars in annual subscription costs for individual entrepreneurs, a particularly significant equity benefit for low-capital and minority founders.
  • ALA's Libraries Build Business initiative (2020, Kauffman Foundation-supported) formalized library entrepreneurship support as a national program, funding cohorts of public libraries to expand programming with an explicit focus on BIPOC, women, immigrant, rural, and veteran entrepreneurs.
  • The Urban Libraries Council (ULC) runs a parallel entrepreneurship initiative documenting and scaling urban library business centers and incubators, publishing toolkits such as 'Entrepreneurship Spaces in Your Library.'
  • The USPTO's Patent and Trademark Resource Centers (PTRCs, formerly PTDLs) number roughly 80+ designated libraries and provide inventors free patent and trademark search assistance, with the PTDLA serving as the librarians' professional association.
  • Libraries are the most common physical venue for SCORE mentoring and a frequent co-location site for Small Business Development Center (SBDC) advising, extending the SBA's national small-business support network into neighborhoods and rural communities.
  • Dedicated business libraries and innovation centers - NYPL's Thomas Yoseloff Business Center, Boston Public Library's KBLIC, Cleveland Public Library's BEL (est. 1899), and Chattanooga's 4th Floor - demonstrate mature models of integrated research-plus-workspace service.
  • Library makerspaces (Chicago Public Library Maker Lab, Fayetteville Free Library Fab Lab, Pikes Peak Library 21c) support maker-business and tech-startup prototyping, lowering the capital barrier to physical product development.

Key Facts

  • Americans checked out 1.71 billion items from public libraries in FY2022 - roughly 5.2 items per person in the United States.
  • 155 million Americans hold library cards - about 45% of the population served by public libraries.
  • Electronic (e-book/e-audio) circulation reached 316 million, about 18.5% of total circulation - up from near-zero two decades ago.
  • Children's materials account for 36.9% of circulation (630M items), making children the single largest user demographic by material type.
  • Americans made 869 million visits to public libraries - 2.5 visits per capita.
  • Libraries hosted 5.1 million programs attended by 105 million people.
  • 101 million people used public library computers/internet in FY2022.
  • Public library collections hold 639 million book volumes nationally.

Key Facts

  • Approximately 155 million Americans were registered library borrowers in FY2024 (IMLS PLS); the ALA estimates ~166-170 million Americans have library cards when counting active and household cards.
  • US public libraries circulated 1.71 billion items in FY2024 (official IMLS total; ~1.86B including best estimates for states that suppressed the field) — about 4.97 items per person in the population served.
  • Children's materials make up ~37% of all circulation, the single largest content category.
  • Digital/electronic circulation is ~28% of circulation; physical materials remain ~72%.
  • September is Library Card Sign-Up Month, an ALA national campaign launched in 1987 to encourage card enrollment, timed with the start of the school year.
  • Pew Research (2013) found 61% of Americans age 16+ have a library card (down from 63% in 2012); cardholders skew female, white, under 65, suburban/urban, higher-education, and higher-income.
  • 86% of Americans age 16+ have used a public library at some point (in person or via website); 54% used one in the past 12 months (Pew 2013).
  • Circulation peaked around FY2019 (~2.19B items), fell ~25% in FY2020 during COVID-19, rebounded to ~1.96B by FY2023, then eased to ~1.71B in FY2024 as digital borrowing normalized.

Libraries & Workforce Development

Public libraries are the most widely available workforce-development on-ramp in the country: virtually every U.S. public library offers some combination of job-search help, digital-literacy instruction, and access to online learning platforms — services that connect millions of job seekers to employment each year.

Job Training

Public libraries provide and host job-training resources that range from self-paced online learning platforms to instructor-led classes delivered in partnership with workforce boards, community colleges, and nonprofit trainers.

Career Services

Beyond training, libraries deliver direct career services that help patrons navigate the job-search process from start to finish. These services frequently fill gaps left by overburdened public workforce offices.

  • Resume-writing workshops and one-on-one resume review clinics, often staffed by volunteer HR professionals or library business librarians.
  • Cover-letter coaching and templates, including guidance on tailoring applications to specific job postings and applicant tracking systems (ATS).
  • Mock interviews and interview-prep sessions, sometimes recorded for review, with feedback from career coaches.
  • Job-search strategy coaching: using job boards, company research with library business databases (such as Reference Solutions, AtoZdatabases, and Mergent), and networking guidance.
  • Career assessment and exploration tools, including access to O*NET, CareerOneStop content, and interest inventories.
  • Job fairs and employer hiring events hosted in library spaces, often co-organized with chambers of commerce and workforce boards.
  • Support for online job applications, including help creating email accounts, uploading documents, and navigating employer portals that are difficult to use on mobile phones.
  • LinkedIn profile-building workshops and professional-online-presence coaching.
  • Print and fax services for submitting applications to employers that still require paper or faxed materials.

Workforce Partnerships

Libraries are recognized partners in the publicly funded workforce development system. The Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act (WIOA), enacted in 2014, established a framework in which libraries can serve as access points and optional partners within the one-stop / American Job Center delivery system.

['WIOA (Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act) reauthorized and modernized the former Workforce Investment Act (WIA), emphasizing integrated service delivery through American Job Centers (AJCs).', "American Job Centers (formerly 'one-stop career centers') are the front door of the public workforce system, offering labor-market information, job-search assistance, training referrals, and services for targeted populations including dislocated workers, youth, and adults with barriers to employment.", 'Many public libraries host AJC satellite services or co-locate with AJCs, providing shared space where workforce staff offer case management while library staff provide technology access, research help, and digital-literacy support.', 'The required WIOA one-stop partner programs include Adult and Dislocated Worker programs, the Youth program, Adult Education and Literacy (Title II), and Vocational Rehabilitation (Title IV); libraries commonly coordinate with these partners locally even where not formally mandated as partners.', 'Local Workforce Development Boards (WDBs) frequently include library representation and contract with libraries for services such as computer access, digital navigation, and outreach.']

Digital Skills

Digital literacy is a prerequisite for nearly every step of modern job seeking, from finding postings to submitting applications and participating in virtual interviews. Libraries are the primary free provider of digital-skills instruction in most U.S. communities.

Northstar Digital Literacy Assessment was developed by Literacy Minnesota (originally the Minnesota Literacy Council) in partnership with the Saint Paul Public Library. It defines and assesses foundational digital-literacy skills across modules including Basic Computer Use, Internet, Windows, Mac OS, Email, Microsoft Word, Excel, PowerPoint, Google Docs, Social Media, Information Literacy, and Career Search Skills. Libraries nationwide use Northstar both to benchmark learner skills and to award certificates that job seekers can show employers. A Northstar subscription enables proctored assessment and certificate generation, and many libraries serve as official Northstar testing sites.

Economic Impact

The economic impact of library workforce services extends from individual earnings gains to community-level labor-market participation and business formation.

Individual Level

  • Improved employment outcomes for patrons who use library job and skills services, including faster re-employment and access to better-paying roles through credentialing.
  • Reduced cost barriers to training via free access to online learning platforms that would otherwise require paid subscriptions.
  • Pathways from foundational skills (ESL, ABE, GED) into occupational credentials and sustained employment.

Community Level

  • Higher labor-force participation in communities with strong library workforce programming, especially where libraries complement under-resourced AJCs.
  • Business formation supported through library-based entrepreneurship services (SCORE/SBDC partnerships, market-research databases).
  • Reduced digital divide, which translates into more residents able to participate in online hiring and remote work.
  • Cost leverage for the public workforce system: libraries provide space, technology, and digital-navigation capacity that AJCs would otherwise have to fund independently.

Key Findings

  • Public libraries are de facto workforce-development institutions: virtually every U.S. public library provides some combination of job-search help, digital-literacy instruction, and access to online learning, making them the most widely available workforce on-ramp in the country.
  • Digital literacy is the gateway skill. Without basic computer and internet skills, patrons cannot complete online job applications, file for unemployment, or participate in virtual interviews; libraries-and tools like Northstar Digital Literacy-address this gap directly.
  • WIOA created an explicit framework for libraries to participate in the American Job Center system, and many do so through co-location, referral, and shared service delivery with Local Workforce Development Boards.
  • Adult education and workforce development are inseparable at the library: ESL, adult basic education, and GED/HSE preparation feed directly into occupational training and employment, reflecting WIOA's emphasis on integrated education and training.
  • ALA's 'Libraries = Helping Jobs' initiative and PLA workforce resources formalize and advocate for a role libraries were already performing, giving the field shared language, data, and tools to scale effective practice.
  • Partnerships are the multiplier. Libraries that co-locate AJC services, host SCORE/SBDC advising, and align with community colleges deliver far greater impact than libraries operating workforce services in isolation.
  • Economic shocks accelerate library workforce demand: recessions and the COVID-19 pandemic drove surges in unemployment-claim navigation, remote-interview access, and reemployment upskilling that libraries absorbed alongside workforce partners.
  • Return-on-investment studies consistently rank workforce and small-business services among the highest-value public library offerings, reinforcing the case for sustained and expanded funding of these programs.
  • Connectivity is a workforce prerequisite: hotspot and device lending, extended hours, and digital-navigator staffing close the home-access gaps that keep low-income job seekers out of the online labor market.
  • Entrepreneurship is a workforce pathway: by supporting business plan development, market research, and mentoring, libraries help patrons create jobs as well as find them.

Libraries & Youth Services

From dedicated teen spaces and STEM robotics labs to summer reading programs that combat learning loss, U.S. public libraries are foundational youth-development institutions — codified in the PLA's 2007 service responses and supported by ALSC and YALSA divisions of the ALA.

Teen Programs

Public libraries run an expanding portfolio of programs designed specifically for teenagers (roughly ages 12-18), a population that has historically been harder to serve than younger children or adults. Teen services rest on several building blocks: teen advisory boards that give young people a voice in library decisions, dedicated teen spaces designed to feel distinct from children's areas, gaming and esports programs that meet teens where their interests are, coding and maker programs that build technical skills, and a professional framework -- led nationally by the American Library Association's Young Adult Library Services Association (YALSA) -- that trains librarians to serve this age group.

{'description': 'Teen advisory boards (TABs), teen councils, and teen advisory groups are among the most common teen-engagement structures in U.S. public libraries. A TAB is a group of teen volunteers who meet regularly with a teen librarian to advise the library on programs, collections, and spaces for their age group, and who often help plan and run teen events.', 'typical_activities': ['Selecting teen materials and recommending purchases for the young-adult collection.', 'Planning teen programs such as after-hours events, lock-ins, anime clubs, and author visits.', 'Designing and decorating the teen space and producing teen displays.', 'Organizing teen volunteer and community-service projects.', 'Acting as a youth voice in library planning, strategic planning, and facilities design.', 'Hosting teen book reviews and contributing to library social media and blogs.'], 'significance': 'TABs operationalize a core principle of youth development -- giving young people genuine decision-making power -- and are frequently cited in library literature as a leading strategy for increasing teen attendance and ownership of the library.'}

STEM & STEAM for Youth

Science, technology, engineering, the arts, and mathematics (STEM/STEAM) programming for youth has become one of the fastest-growing areas of public-library service. Libraries run coding clubs, robotics programs, and hands-on science workshops, and they partner with national STEM organizations to bring curricula, kits, and expertise that local schools may not offer. This work is supported by PLA's service response 'Satisfy Curiosity: Lifelong Learning' and by grant programs from the Institute of Museum and Library Services (IMLS).

  • NASA @ My Library: {'name': 'NASA@ My Library', 'operator': "STAR Library Network (STAR Net), a program of the Space Science Institute's National Center for Interactive Learning,
  • Girls Who Code: {'name': 'Girls Who Code', 'operator': 'Girls Who Code (international nonprofit, founded 2012)', 'description': "Girls Who Code is an internation
  • FIRST Robotics: {'name': 'FIRST Robotics Competition (and FIRST programs)', 'operator': 'FIRST (For Inspiration and Recognition of Science and Technology), a nonprofit founded in 19
  • Coding in Libraries: {'description': 'Beyond the named national partners, thousands of libraries run their own coding and computer-science programs for youth, often using free curricula and platforms design

Summer Learning

Summer is both an opportunity and a risk for young library users. The 'summer slide' -- academic-skills loss during the long break -- is well documented, and libraries have responded with summer reading programs, summer STEM and coding camps, and, critically, summer meal programs for children who lose access to school meals. Project Outcome, launched in 2015 by the Public Library Association, formally includes 'Summer Reading' as one of its measured outcome areas (Wikipedia, 'Public libraries in North America').

{'description': 'Summer reading programs are the single most universal youth program in U.S. public libraries. Nearly every public library system runs a summer reading program, typically built around a shared annual theme, with reading logs, incentives, and events designed to keep children and teens reading over the break.', 'collaborative_summer_library_program': {'name': 'Collaborative Summer Library Program (CSLP)', 'description': "CSLP is a nonprofit, charitable organization that supports literacy, education, and science through summer reading events in public libraries across the United States (Wikipedia, 'Collaborative Summer Library Program'). According to the CSLP website (cslpreads.org), fetched live during compilation, CSLP helps public libraries spark a love of reading; through shared resources and collaborative committees, CSLP makes it easier and more affordable for libraries to host high-quality summer reading programs that enrich their communities.", 'what_cslp_provides': ['Nationally themed programs with engaging artwork and slogans.', 'Ready-to-use marketing materials and reading incentives (posters, reading logs, bookmarks, certificates).', 'Public-service announcements and promotions raising awareness about libraries.', 'Collaborative committees that develop the annual theme and programming manual.']}, 'ala_alsc_summer': 'ALSC and YALSA also produce summer-reading and summer-learning resources, booklists, and program ideas, and YALSA has historically offered summer-learning grants supporting teen programming.'}

{'description': "Recognizing that summer reading and summer hunger happen in the same communities, a growing number of libraries now serve summer meals to children and teens. Libraries partner with the USDA's Summer Food Service Program (SFSP) and the Child and Adult Care Food Program (CACFP) to become meal sites, pairing food with programming so that children who rely on free or reduced-price school meals do not lose that nutrition over the summer.", 'usda_programs': ['Summer Food Service Program (SFSP) -- USDA-funded meals and snacks served at approved sites during summer break.', "Child and Adult Care Food Program (CACFP) -- USDA assistance providing a daily subsidized food service for an estimated 3.3 million children and 120,000 elderly or disabled adults in non-residential day-care settings (Wikipedia, 'Child and Adult Care Food Program'; CFDA 10.558; authorized under the National School Lunch Act)."], 'library_role': ['Serving as an open SFSP meal site where any child can receive a free summer meal.', 'Pairing meals with summer reading, STEM, and enrichment programming so the visit is productive.', 'Coordinating with school districts and food banks to fill geographic gaps in meal coverage.', 'Offering meals in low-income neighborhoods where few other summer meal sites exist.'], 'significance': 'Library summer-meal programs reframe the library as a year-round community welfare institution, not only an academic one, and they directly address the summer nutrition gap that falls hardest on low-income children.'}

ALA Youth Divisions

The American Library Association (ALA) administers youth services through two long-standing divisions: the Association for Library Service to Children (ALSC), focused on services to children, and the Young Adult Library Services Association (YALSA), focused on services to teens. Together these divisions set professional standards, run continuing education, and administer the most prestigious children's and young-adult book awards in the United States.

Key Findings

  • Youth services are codified core library functions. The Public Library Association's 2007 eighteen service responses explicitly include 'Create Young Readers: Early Literacy,' 'Succeed in School: Homework Help,' 'Learn to Read and Write: Adults, Teens, and Family Literature,' and 'Express Creativity: Create and Share Content,' formally recognizing youth as a primary library constituency (Wikipedia, 'Public libraries in North America').
  • Two ALA divisions anchor the profession's youth work. ALSC (over 4,000 members; nearly 60 active committees) serves children and YALSA (established 1957; over 5,200 members) serves teens; YALSA is scheduled to reunify with ALSC on September 1, 2026, consolidating ALA's youth services into a single division (Wikipedia, 'Association for Library Service to Children'; 'Young Adult Library Services Association').
  • Libraries administer the most prestigious U.S. youth literature awards. ALSC awards the Newbery Medal (proposed 1921, the world's first children's book award) and the Caldecott Medal (proposed 1937); YALSA administers the Printz, Edwards, Alex, and William C. Morris YA Debut awards, which collectively shape what is published, purchased, and read by young people (Wikipedia, award articles).
  • Summer reading is a measured library outcome. Project Outcome, launched by PLA in 2015, includes 'Summer Reading' alongside 'Early Childhood Literacy' and 'Digital Learning' as formal outcome areas, institutionalizing summer youth programming as a reportable dimension of library impact (Wikipedia, 'Public libraries in North America').
  • CSLP makes universal summer reading possible. The Collaborative Summer Library Program, a nonprofit, supplies a nationally themed summer reading program -- artwork, slogans, marketing materials, and incentives -- that makes it affordable for libraries nationwide to run high-quality summer reading, especially small and rural systems (Wikipedia, 'Collaborative Summer Library Program'; cslpreads.org).
  • Libraries have become STEM providers at scale. Through NASA@ My Library (STAR Net/Space Science Institute, with NASA and IMLS), Girls Who Code clubs (nearly 600,000 alumnae as of 2022), and FIRST robotics partnerships (3,468 teams in 28 countries in 2024), libraries extend STEM/STEAM access to youth who lack it at school (Wikipedia, 'Girls Who Code'; 'FIRST Robotics Competition'; starnetlibraries.org).
  • Online tutoring is a major library equity tool. Through subscriptions to live-tutoring platforms (such as Brainfuse HelpNow and Tutor.com), libraries give cardholders -- including rural students and those who cannot afford private tutoring -- one-on-one academic help, often state- or system-wide licensed.
  • Libraries feed children in summer. By partnering with USDA's Summer Food Service Program and the Child and Adult Care Food Program (which serves an estimated 3.3 million children), libraries become summer meal sites, pairing nutrition with programming to close the summer hunger gap that falls hardest on low-income children (Wikipedia, 'Child and Adult Care Food Program').
  • Dedicated teen spaces reshape adolescent library use. Pioneered by spaces like YOUmedia at Chicago's Harold Washington Library Center and the Teen Loft at Charlotte's ImaginOn (a 102,000-sq-ft library-theatre collaboration), the dedicated teen-space model -- distinct from children's areas, technology-rich, and teen-informed -- has been replicated nationwide through IMLS-funded Learning Labs (Wikipedia, 'Harold Washington Library'; 'ImaginOn').
  • Libraries are foundational after-school infrastructure. As documented community locations for after-school activities, libraries serve latchkey children with free, supervised space between school dismissal and the end of the workday, and research links structured after-school programs to better homework completion and higher grades (Wikipedia, 'After-school activity'; 'Latchkey kid').

Library Technology Infrastructure

The technology backbone of America's public libraries: integrated library systems (ILS), discovery layers, Wi-Fi networks, public computing, and the self-service technologies that power modern library operations.

Integrated Library Systems (ILS)

An Integrated Library System (ILS) -- also called a Library Management System (LMS) -- is the foundational software platform that automates a library's core operations: cataloging, circulation, acquisitions, serials control, OPAC (online public access catalog), and reporting. The U.S. ILS market has consolidated sharply over the past two decades. Marshall Breeding's annual Library Systems Report (published in American Libraries / Smart Libraries Newsletter) documents that a handful of vendors now dominate. Following Ex Libris's 2020 acquisition of Innovative Interfaces, the combined Ex Libris/Innovative portfolio held over 70% of the academic-library ILS market and over 50% of the public-library ILS market in the United States as of 2019 figures, while OCLC held roughly 10% of each segment. The market spans large proprietary vendors, an open-source movement anchored by Koha and Evergreen, and specialized school-library products.

{'ex_libris_acquires_innovative_2020': "Innovative Interfaces was bought by Ex Libris (a ProQuest/Clarivate company) in 2020, making Ex Libris the dominant U.S. ILS supplier: over 70% market share for academic libraries and over 50% for public libraries, versus OCLC's approximately 10% of both segments (2019 figures, as documented in the Wikipedia article on OCLC, itself citing Roger Schonfeld, Ithaka S+R, December 2019).", 'breeding_reports': "Marshall Breeding publishes the annual 'Library Systems Report' (American Libraries; Smart Libraries Newsletter; librarytechnology.org) tracking ILS installations, migrations, and market share. The report is the field's standard reference for automation-product adoption and is the basis for most cited ILS market-share figures.", 'consortium_vs_solo': 'Most U.S. public libraries operate an ILS either independently or as members of a consortium that shares a single ILS instance (e.g., a county or statewide shared catalog). Shared ILS deployments reduce per-site costs and enable resource sharing but limit local customization.'}

Open Source Movement

{'description': 'The open-source ILS movement, begun with Koha in 1999 and extended by Evergreen in 2006, introduced community-developed, GPL-licensed library automation as a credible alternative to proprietary systems. Open-source ILS products eliminate per-library licensing fees, enable full local customization, and are supported by a mix of community contributions and commercial support vendors. Adoption is concentrated among public libraries and consortia that have the technical capacity to host or contract for support.', 'key_products': ['Koha (1999, first open-source ILS)', 'Evergreen (2006, consortial scale)'], 'drivers': ['Lower total cost of ownership (no vendor licensing fees).', 'Avoidance of vendor lock-in and annual maintenance hikes.', 'Full source-code access for customization and integration.', 'Consortial sharing of a single shared catalog.', 'Community governance and shared development.']}

Discovery Layers

A discovery layer (or discovery interface) is the patron-facing search platform that sits atop a library's catalog and licensed databases, providing a single unified search experience across local holdings, e-books, journals, and subscription content. Discovery layers emerged in the late 2000s as libraries sought Google-like single-search-box experiences. As Marshall Breeding wrote in American Libraries (January 2019), libraries choose between bundling a discovery service with the resource-management system from the same vendor (e.g., Ex Libris Primo with Alma, or OCLC's WorldCat Discovery with WorldShare Management Services) or acquiring a discovery product 'a la carte.'

Wi-Fi Infrastructure

Public WiFi is now near-universal in U.S. public libraries and is one of the most heavily used technology services they provide. WiFi serves both in-building patrons (who bring their own laptops, tablets, and phones) and, increasingly, patrons in parking lots and surrounding areas through extended-range access points and outdoor hotspots. The IMLS Public Libraries Survey (PLS) FY2024 records approximately 418.4 million annual Wi-Fi sessions across reporting systems.

Public Computing

Public-access computers are a core library technology service. The IMLS Public Libraries Survey (PLS) FY2024 is the authoritative national source for counts of public internet computers and their usage. In FY2024, U.S. public libraries provided approximately 260,000 public-access internet computers and recorded roughly 100.9 million annual public-internet-user sessions. Combined with ~418.4 million Wi-Fi sessions, libraries hosted roughly 519 million in-library internet uses in a single year.

Printing Services

Printing, scanning, and (where available) faxing are standard public-library technology services. Modern libraries have extended printing well beyond black-and-white document printing to include 3D printing, large-format/poster printing, and specialized photo printing (including passport photos). Pew's 2013 survey grouped 'printers' with computers and internet as the basic technological resources patrons value.

3D Printing

{'description': "3D printing in libraries emerged with the makerspace movement. A library makerspace is an area offering patrons tools to create intellectual and physical materials, including computers, 3D printers, 3D scanning, audio/video capture and editing, and traditional arts and crafts (Wikipedia, 'Library makerspace').", 'first_public_library_makerspace': 'The Fayetteville Free Library (Fayetteville, NY) makerspace, conceived by Lauren Britton as MLIS coursework, began around 2011 and is widely cited as the first public library makerspace.', 'typical_equipment': ['3D printers', '3D scanners', 'laser cutters', 'sewing machines', 'soldering irons', 'coding/robotics kits', 'wood carving machines', 'audio/video editing tools', 'board games', 'arts and crafts'], 'national_count_note': 'No single authoritative national count of libraries with 3D printers/makerspaces is published by IMLS or ALA; industry surveys indicate makerspaces spread rapidly after 2011 and are now offered by a large share of medium and large urban systems, but a precise national total is not centrally aggregated.'}

Self-Checkout

Self-checkout technology allows patrons to borrow and return materials without staff mediation, reducing queues and freeing staff for reference and programming. Self-checkout rests on two core item-identification technologies -- barcodes and RFID -- plus an emerging layer of mobile checkout applications. Self-service kiosks typically integrate with the library's ILS circulation module via SIP2 or NCIP protocols.

{'description': "Radio Frequency Identification (RFID) self-checkout uses RFID tags on items; a patron can place a stack of items on an RFID pad and the kiosk reads them all at once (one-step, multi-item checkout). RFID also enables automated returns sorting, security gates, and inventory wand scans. Wikipedia ('Library catalog') notes that navigational guidance to retrieve a resource 'may be assisted through signage, maps, GPS systems, or RFID tagging.'", 'adoption_drivers': ['Faster multi-item checkout (read a stack at once).', 'Automated return sorting via RFID drop-off bins.', 'Security gates that detect un-deactivated items.', 'Rapid inventory via handheld RFID wands.', 'Reduced repetitive strain for staff.'], 'cost_barriers': 'RFID requires upfront tagging of the entire collection (often a major project) plus RFID-compatible gates, pads, and staff wands, making it a significant capital investment typically undertaken by mid-sized and large systems.', 'national_count_note': 'No central national count of RFID-equipped libraries is published by IMLS or ALA; adoption is concentrated in larger systems and consortia that can amortize the tagging investment.'}

{'description': 'Some libraries now offer mobile self-checkout via a smartphone app: the patron scans item barcodes with the phone camera and the app checks the items out through the ILS. This extends self-service to patrons anywhere in the building without requiring a fixed kiosk.', 'integration_note': "Mobile checkout apps typically integrate with the ILS via SIP2/NCIP and with the library's discovery/account platform; availability depends on ILS vendor support."}

Key Findings

  • The U.S. ILS market has consolidated dramatically: after Ex Libris acquired Innovative Interfaces in 2020, the combined Ex Libris/Innovative portfolio held over 70% of the academic-library ILS market and over 50% of the public-library ILS market, versus OCLC's roughly 10% of each segment (2019 figures, Wikipedia 'OCLC', citing Schonfeld, Ithaka S+R).
  • Open-source ILS products -- pioneered by Koha (1999, the first open-source ILS, created by Katipo Communications for New Zealand's Horowhenua Library Trust) and Evergreen (2006, built for the Georgia PINES consortium) -- provide a credible, lower-cost alternative to proprietary systems and are used by hundreds of U.S. libraries.
  • Public WiFi is effectively universal in U.S. public libraries: 91% offered free wireless Internet as of 2012 (Wikipedia, 'Public library'), and the IMLS PLS FY2024 records approximately 418.4 million annual Wi-Fi sessions across reporting systems -- roughly 4x the number of wired public-internet-user sessions (~100.9 million).
  • In FY2024, U.S. public libraries provided approximately 260,000 public-access internet computers (averaging ~29 per system) and recorded ~100.9 million annual public-internet-user sessions (IMLS PLS FY2024). Combined with Wi-Fi, libraries hosted roughly 519 million in-library internet uses in a single year.
  • Libraries remain critical digital-equity infrastructure: 24 million U.S. households still lack home internet (ALA), and in 2006, 73% of library branches reported being the only local provider of free public computer/Internet access. Among patrons without home internet, 56% rated library tech resources 'very important' (Pew, 2013).
  • The E-Rate program (Section 254 of the Telecommunications Act of 1996) is the principal federal funding source for library connectivity, discounting 20-90% of eligible telecom, internet access, and internal connections. Annual demand almost triples the $2.25B cap, and the FCC raised the budget to $3.9B in December 2014.
  • E-Rate policy is in flux: the FCC added off-premises Wi-Fi hotspot and school-bus eligibility in 2024, then in October 2025 voted 2-1 to end that eligibility; in June 2026 the FCC asked whether E-Rate should be scaled back or sunset entirely. ALA and the SHLB Coalition launched the 'Save Our E-Rate' campaign.
  • The Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation's U.S. Libraries Program (begun 1997) was the largest philanthropic technology infusion in U.S. public library history: a single 2003 grant of $250 million installed 47,000+ computers and trained staff in ~11,000 libraries across all 50 states.
  • Discovery layers have replaced the classic OPAC as the patron search experience. The market is dominated by Ex Libris Primo (paired with Alma), EBSCO Discovery Service, and OCLC WorldCat Discovery, with BiblioCommons serving public libraries and Polaris PowerPAC serving Polaris-ILS libraries.
  • 3D printing and makerspaces, beginning with the Fayetteville Free Library (~2011, conceived by Lauren Britton), have extended library technology services into patron 'creation stations' offering 3D printers, scanners, laser cutters, and audio/video tools, though no central national count of makerspace-equipped libraries is published.

Library Assessment & Measurement

How libraries measure their impact: from IMLS Public Libraries Survey data and ISO 16439 international standards to PLA's Project Outcome toolkit and return-on-investment studies that quantify the economic and social value of library services.

Performance Metrics

Library performance measurement in the United States rests on a layered set of frameworks: the IMLS Public Libraries Survey (PLS), the national administrative dataset; ISO international standards for library statistics, performance indicators, and impact assessment; and e-resource usage standards that let libraries compare electronic collection use. Together these frameworks give libraries a common vocabulary for inputs (funding, staffing, collections), outputs (visits, circulation, programs), and increasingly outcomes (what changed for the user).

ISO 16439

{'name': 'ISO 16439:2014 - Information and documentation - Methods and procedures for assessing the impact of libraries', 'publisher': 'International Organization for Standardization (ISO), Technical Committee ISO/TC 46, Information and documentation', 'published': '2014', 'scope': 'ISO 16439:2014 defines internationally agreed methods and procedures for assessing the impact of libraries on individuals and society. It distinguishes inputs, outputs, and outcomes/impact, and provides guidance on qualitative and quantitative impact-assessment techniques including surveys, interviews, observation, and analysis of usage data.', 'why_it_matters': 'ISO 16439 is the principal international standard that formalizes the shift from counting library outputs (visits, circulation) to measuring library impact (the difference made for users and communities). It underpins comparable, methodologically sound outcome reporting across countries and library types.', 'companion_standards': [{'standard': 'ISO 2789', 'title': 'International library statistics', 'role': 'Defines the core statistical vocabulary (registered users, loans, visits) that feeds performance and impact measurement.'}, {'standard': 'ISO 11620', 'title': 'Methods and procedures for assessing the impact of libraries - Library performance indicators', 'role': 'Defines standardized performance indicators (e.g., cost per loan, loans per capita, titles per capita) derived from ISO 2789 statistics.'}, {'standard': 'ISO 21248', 'title': 'Information and documentation - Accident losses in libraries - wait, actually: Statistics on library collections and usage of library e-resources', 'role': 'Standardizes collection and electronic-resource usage statistics.'}], 'citation': "ISO 16439:2014. Wikipedia 'List of ISO standards 16000-17999' confirms the title and 2014 publication date."}

CIPP Evaluation Model

{'name': 'CIPP Evaluation Model (Context, Input, Process, Product)', 'developer': 'Daniel L. Stufflebeam and colleagues', 'developed': '1960s', 'scope': 'CIPP is a decision-focused program-evaluation model that emphasizes the systematic provision of information for program management and operation. It structures evaluation into four components: Context (needs, assets, problems), Input (alternative designs and resources), Process (implementation), and Product (outcomes and impacts).', 'library_application': 'Library directors and evaluators use CIPP to structure assessments of programs, services, and grant-funded initiatives -- mapping community needs (context), staffing and collections (input), service delivery (process), and measured outcomes (product). It is widely used in IMLS grant evaluations and library school evaluation courses.', 'citation': "Wikipedia 'CIPP evaluation model' (extract confirms Stufflebeam, 1960s, Context/Input/Process/Product)."}

Project Outcome (PLA)

Project Outcome is the Public Library Association's (PLA) national initiative to help public libraries measure the outcomes of their programs and services. It grew out of PLA's work in the early 2010s to move libraries beyond counting outputs (attendance, circulation) toward measuring outcomes (what patrons gained). Libraries use standardized, ready-to-use surveys to capture patron-reported change immediately after a program, aggregate results in an online toolkit, and report outcomes to stakeholders and funders.

  • {'area': 'Civic/Community Engagement', 'focus': 'Whether patrons gained awareness, connections, or intended to take action after a civic or community program.'}
  • {'area': 'Digital Inclusion', 'focus': 'Whether patrons improved digital skills, accessed needed technology, or got help with online tasks (e-government, job applications).'}
  • {'area': 'Early Literacy', 'focus': 'Whether caregivers and children gained early-literacy knowledge or behaviors after storytimes and early-literacy programs.'}
  • {'area': 'Economic Development', 'focus': 'Whether patrons advanced job searches, business plans, or financial literacy after workforce or business programs.'}
  • {'area': 'Education/Lifelong Learning', 'focus': 'Whether patrons gained knowledge, skills, or confidence for school, work, or personal learning goals.'}
  • {'area': 'Health', 'focus': 'Whether patrons gained health information, behaviors, or access to care after health and wellness programming.'}
  • {'area': 'Job Skills / Workforce Development', 'focus': 'Whether patrons gained job-readiness skills or made employment progress.'}
  • {'area': 'Summer Reading (seasonal)', 'focus': 'Whether children and teens maintained or improved reading habits over the summer, addressing summer learning loss.'}

Social Return on Investment

Social return on investment (SROI) and economic-impact studies translate library value into monetary terms by comparing the dollars invested in libraries to the economic value they return to the community. Multiple state and municipal studies conducted between 2004 and 2018 consistently find that every $1 invested in public library services returns roughly $3 to $6.54 in direct and indirect economic value. These studies are central evidence in library advocacy and funding cases.

ALA Public Library Funding & Technology Access Study (PLFTAS)92% of libraries provide job resources, 76% help with online job applications, 96% assist with e-government; 62% of unemployed Americans used libraries during the recession.

Pew Research Center - How Americans Value Public Libraries in Their CommunitiesAbout 90% of Americans ages 16 and older say public libraries are important to their communities, and a large majority say libraries are important to themselves and their families.
Pew Research Center - Parents, Children, Libraries, and ReadingParents of minor children are more likely than other adults to use libraries, to say libraries are important, and to value children's and family programming.
Pew Research Center - Younger Americans and Public LibrariesYounger Americans (16-29) are as likely as older adults to use libraries and read books, and are especially engaged with library technology and e-content.

Cooper et al. Summer Learning Loss Meta-AnalysisMeta-analysis of 39 studies found students lose about one month of overall learning and two to three months of reading achievement over summer; losses largest for low-income students.
Alexander, Entwisle, & Olson - Summer Learning and the Achievement GapBaltimore longitudinal study: two-thirds of the ninth-grade socioeconomic achievement gap is explained by unequal summer learning in the elementary years.

Key Findings

  • U.S. public library performance measurement rests on the IMLS Public Libraries Survey, a near-census of about 9,249 library systems (FY2024) that provides the authoritative national dataset on inputs, outputs, staffing, and finances.
  • The field has shifted from counting outputs to measuring outcomes: ISO 16439:2014 formalizes methods for assessing library impact, and PLA Project Outcome gives hundreds of libraries ready-to-use patron-reported outcome surveys across seven outcome areas.
  • Outcome-based evaluation is now embedded in federal library funding: IMLS grant programs require logic models and outcome reporting, making the Context-Input-Process-Product (CIPP) and logic-model frameworks part of everyday library grant practice.
  • Social ROI studies consistently find libraries return roughly $3 to $6.54 for every $1 invested, with Florida's 2004 study documenting the highest measured ROI at $6.54 per dollar -- evidence that libraries are among the highest-return public investments measured.
  • The U.S. IMPACT Study (2010) found 77 million Americans age 14+ used a library computer or Wi-Fi network in a single year, with education (44%), employment (40%), and health (37%) the leading uses -- a landmark in measuring digital-inclusion impact.
  • Project COUNTER (Release 5) standardizes electronic-resource usage reporting, turning e-resource use into a comparable performance metric that supports collection assessment and ROI analysis across publishers.
  • Benchmarking is organized at national (PLA Benchmarking, PLDS), state (state library agency tools), and system levels, giving libraries per-capita and peer comparisons on visits, circulation, programs, expenditures, and staffing.
  • Persistent evaluation challenges limit comparability and causal claims: the attribution problem (hard to prove the library caused the change), qualitative-vs-quantitative trade-offs, definitional inconsistency across systems, and resource constraints in small libraries.
  • About 90% of Americans say public libraries are important to their communities (Pew Research Center), a durable public valuation that complements the measured economic and social returns.
  • Annual assessments like the ALA State of America's Libraries report and IMLS PLS trend data show U.S. public libraries in FY2024 recording about 869,277,475 visits, 1,705,466,066 circulation, and 5,085,068 programs with 105,321,602 attendees -- a vast service volume that outcome frameworks seek to translate into demonstrated community value.
